On filtered algebraic K K -theory of stacks I: characteristic zero
Elden Elmanto, Dmitry Kubrak, Vladimir Sosnilo

Abstract
. Given a compact Lie group G G acting on a space X X , the classical Atiyah-Segal completion theorem identifies topological K K -theory of the homotopy quotient X / G X/G with an explicit completion of G G -equivariant topological K K -theory of X X . We prove an analog of this result for algebraic K K -theory over a field of characteristic 0. In our setting G G is a reductive group that acts on a derived algebraic space X X with the assumption that all stabilizer groups are nice (in the sense of Alper). Our main result identifies the value R dAff ​ K ​ ( [ X / G ] ) R^{\mathrm{dAff}}K([X/G]) of right Kan extension of the K K -theory functor from schemes to stacks with the completion of K K -theory of the category Perf ⁡ ( [ X / G ] ) \mathrm{Perf}([X/G]) at the augmentation ideal of K 0 ​ ( Rep ​ ( G ) ) K_{0}(\mathrm{Rep}(G)) . The main novelty of our results is that X X is allowed to be singular or even derived. This generality is achieved by employing and improving analogous versions of completion theorem for negative cyclic homology (after Ben-Zvi–Nadler and Chen) and for homotopy K K -theory (after van den Bergh–Tabuada). We also show that in the singular setting the completion theorem does not necessarily hold without the nice stabilizer assumption. We view our results as a part of the general paradigm of extending the motivic filtration on algebraic K K -theory of schemes to algebraic K K -theory of stacks.



1.
Introduction

The goal of this paper is to offer computational and structural
results about the nonconnective algebraic
K
-theory of a large class of algebraic stacks over
\mathbb{Q}
, i.e., those which are of equicharacteristic zero. The meaning of the algebraic

K
-theory of a stack can be ambiguous. On the one hand, after the work of
[
BGT13
]
, we know that algebraic
K
-theory is a universal functor

K\colon\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathrm{Spt},

enjoying the key property that it respects certain localization sequences. In particular, the
K
-theory of a stack
\mathcal{X}
can be defined as
K(\mathcal{X}):=K(\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X}))
1
1

1

Throughout this paper, by
\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X})
we mean the subcategory of
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})
spanned by dualizable objects. In contrast to the situation for quasi-compact quasi-separated schemes, subcategories of
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(X)
spanned by compact and, respectively, dualizable objects need not coincide. Furthermore, the
\infty
-category
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})
is not necessarily compactly generated
[
HNR19
]
.
. This has the advantage that we can sometimes describe
K(\mathcal{X})
in rather simple terms. For example, if
\mathcal{X}
is the classifying stack
B\mathbb{G}_{m}
over a field
k
then, identifying
\mathrm{Perf}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})
with graded perfect complexes over
k
, yields:

(1.0.1)

K(B\mathbb{G}_{m})\simeq\bigoplus_{\mathbb{Z}}K(k),

where each component
K(k)
corresponds to a single weight. However, for more general
\mathcal{X}
, we typically do not have such a good control of
\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X})
, and it can be very hard to compute
K(\mathcal{X})
explicitly.

On the other hand, we know that the algebraic
K
-theory of schemes
enjoys a wealth of structure, chief among which is the
motivic filtration
. This is captured succinctly by the functor landing in filtered
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebras in spectra:

\mathrm{Fil}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\star}K:\left(\operatorname{Sch}^{\mathrm{qcqs}}_{\mathbb{Z}}\right)^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{CAlg}{}(\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Spt})).

On sufficiently nice schemes the filtration
\mathrm{Fil}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\star}K
is complete; its associated graded is given by the motivic cohomology
\mathbb Z(\star)^{\mathrm{mot}}[2\star]
, which in general is much more accessible for computations.
It is thus natural to contemplate the following problem:

Question 1.0.2
.

Does the motivic filtration on
K
-theory of schemes extend to algebraic stacks?

We remark, however, that the value on algebraic stacks of any reasonably defined motivic filtration on
K
-theory
will typically not be complete. Moreover, we do need completeness of the filtration to have many of the desired properties of
\mathrm{Fil}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\star}K
, as the following examples hopefully illustrate.

Example 1.0.3
.

The formula (
1.0.1
) can be written multiplicatively as:

K(B\mathbb{G}_{m})\simeq K(k)[x^{\pm 1}]\qquad x=[\mathcal{O}(1)]\in K_{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})

so that, in particular, we have that
K_{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})\cong\mathbb Z[x^{\pm 1}]
. A motivic filtration on
K
-theory will then induce a filtration on
K_{0}
whose graded pieces are displayed in the
E_{\infty}
-page of the corresponding Atiyah-Hirzebruch spectral sequence. One expects that any reasonable motivic filtration on stacks to have the following property as in the schemes case
[
EM23
, Lemma 5.10]
: the Euler class
e(\mathcal{O}(1)):=1-[\mathcal{O}(1)]=1-x
in
K_{0}(B\mathbb G_{m})
lifts to motivic filtration degree
\geqslant 1
. Therefore if we want the motivic filtration on
K(B\mathbb{G}_{m})
be multiplicative and complete, then it forces
K_{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})\simeq\mathbb Z[x^{\pm 1}]
to be
(x-1)
-adically complete, which it is clearly
not
. Note that
(x-1)\subset K_{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})
is exactly the kernel of the pull-back map
K_{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow K_{0}(k)
for the covering
\operatorname{Spec}k\rightarrow B\mathbb G_{m}
; this is a particular case of the so-called
Atiyah-Segal ideal
.

Example 1.0.4
.

This example is supposed to illustrate that some completion is necessary, if we want to have a splitting of
K(\mathcal{X})_{\mathbb Q}
into the product of Adams eigenspaces, i.e., a rational motivic filtration. Recall that for (sufficiently nice) schemes the motivic filtration on rationalized
K
-theory splits functorially
[
EM23
, Appendix B]
: namely,

(1.0.5)

K(X)_{\mathbb Q}\simeq\oplus_{j\geqslant 0}K(X)_{\mathbb Q}^{(j)}\simeq\oplus_{j\geqslant 0}\mathbb Q(j)(X)^{\mathrm{mot}}[2j]

where the
j
-th graded piece is canonically identified with the weight
j
Adams eigenspace.

Let again
\mathcal{X}=B\mathbb G_{m}
. The
\ell
-th Adams operation
\psi_{\ell}
acts on
K_{0}(X)_{\mathbb Q}\simeq\mathbb Q[x,x^{-1}]
by sending
[\mathcal{O}(1)]
to
[\mathcal{O}(1)^{\otimes\ell}]=[\mathcal{O}(\ell)]
; in other words we have
\psi_{\ell}(x)=x^{\ell}
. If
\ell>1
, it is clear that the only eigenvector of
\varphi_{\ell}
in
\mathbb Q[x,x^{-1}]
are the scalars (on which the action is trivial); thus we see that for
K(B\mathbb G_{m})_{\mathbb Q}
there cannot be any splitting analogous to (
1.0.5
).

The function in
x
that would generate “
K(B\mathbb G_{m})_{\mathbb Q}^{(j)}
” should satisfy the equation
f(x^{\ell})=\ell^{j}\cdot f(x)
for all
\ell
; such a function is given by
\log(x^{j})
. Note that
\log(x^{j})
makes perfect sense as an element of the
(x-1)
-adic completion
\mathbb Q[x,x^{-1}]^{\wedge}_{(x-1)}\simeq\mathbb Q[[x-1]]
(but not before completion). Moreover,
\log(x^{j})\in(x-1)^{j}\subset\mathbb Q[[x-1]]
and it’s not hard to check that the map

\prod_{j\geqslant 0}\mathbb Q\cdot\log(x^{j})\rightarrow\mathbb Q[[x-1]],\quad(a_{j})_{j\geqslant 0}\mapsto\sum_{j\geqslant 0}a_{j}\cdot\log(x^{j})

is an isomorphism. This way we see that the splitting in (
1.0.5
) (in the form of direct product) does generalize to
B\mathbb G_{m}
if we are willing to replace
K(B\mathbb G_{m})
by the
Atiyah-Segal completion

K(B\mathbb G_{m})^{\wedge}_{(x-1)}
.

In this paper, we propose a candidate for the motivic filtration on
K(\mathcal{X})
. By construction
2
2

2

At least if the underlying classical stack
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}
is of finite type.
, the completion of
\mathrm{Fil}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\star}K(\mathcal{X})
is identified with the right Kan extension of
K
-theory from affine schemes. The main point of the paper, however, is that if
\mathcal{X}=[X/G]
this right Kan extension can often be described intrinsically in terms of
K(\mathcal{X})
and the map
K(BG)\rightarrow K(\mathcal{X})
induced by the presentation of
\mathcal{X}
as a quotient stack. More precisely, it is the completion of
K(\mathcal{X})
at the Atiyah-Segal ideal
I_{G}\subset K_{0}(BG)
which, in the case of
B\mathbb{G}_{m}
coincides with the ideal generated by the Euler class of
\mathcal{O}(-1)
. In effect, we prove an analog of the Atiyah-Segal completion theorem
[
AS69
]
, in a quite general form, for algebraic
K
-theory and other related localizing invariants.

1.1.
Formulation of main results

Let
k
be a base commutative ring. We recall that a finitely presented flat
k
-group scheme
G
is called
nice

[
HR15
, Definition 1.1]
(resp.
linearly reductive

[
Alp13
, Definition 12.1]
) if it sits in an extension

1\rightarrow G^{0}\rightarrow G\rightarrow H\rightarrow 1,

where
G^{0}
is an algebraic torus and
H
is a finite, locally constant group scheme whose order is invertible in
k
(resp. the functor of derived invariants is
t
-exact). The notion of a nice group appears in the stacks literature where the notion of an
ANS stack
(see Definition
3.1.26
) has been isolated as a key condition that allows for many structural results that one would like to use in practice. By definition, ANS stacks only have nice groups as stabilizers of points.

For a linearly reductive
G
we consider
K_{0}(BG)
which is naturally identified with the (algebraic) representation ring of
G
whenever
k
is a field; we recall that the notion of linear reductivity and reductivity agrees for
G
an algebraic group over a field
k
of characteristic zero. Now further assume that
k
is a field of characteristic 0 and let
G
be a reductive group over
k
. The
Atiyah-Segal ideal
, denoted by
I_{G}
, by definition is the kernel of the “rank” map
K_{0}(BG)\rightarrow K_{0}(k)\simeq\mathbb Z
(induced by the covering
\operatorname{Spec}k\rightarrow BG
). Let
X
be a derived algebraic space over
k
and assume that we have an action of
G
on
X
; we have a natural map
\mathcal{X}:=[X/G]\rightarrow BG
inducing a
K(BG)
-module structure on
K(\mathcal{X})
. Our main theorem identifies the derived
I_{G}
-adic completion of
K(BG)
(in the sense of spectral algebraic geometry
[
Lur18
, Chapter 7]
) with the right Kan extension of
K
-theory from affine schemes in some generality.

Theorem 1.1.1
.

Fix
G
a reductive group over a characteristic zero field
k
. Let
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow BG
be a representable morphism of derived algebraic stacks, such that the morphism of classical stacks
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}\rightarrow BG
is of finite type. Assume further that
\mathcal{X}
is ANS. Then we have a canonical equivalence:

(1.1.2)

K(\mathcal{X})^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\operatorname*{lim}_{\operatorname{Spec}R\rightarrow\mathcal{X}}K(R),

where the limit is taken across all morphisms from derived affine schemes to
\mathcal{X}
.

One can also rephrase the statement above as follows: up to completion at the ideal
I_{G}
, the
K
-theory of the stack
\mathcal{X}
is in fact right Kan extended from derived affine schemes. We will call this type of result “Atiyah-Segal (AS) completion theorem”.

Let us now comment on what sort of algebraic stacks
\mathcal{X}
are allowed by Theorem
1.1.1
.

Remark 1.1.3
.

The condition that the morphism
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow BG
is representable implies that
\mathcal{X}
can be written as
[X/G]
where
X
is a derived algebraic space over
k
. The condition that
\mathcal{X}
is ANS
3
3

3

In our convention derived algebraic spaces have affine diagonal.
means that the stabilizers at all points are nice groups. Let us emphasize however that the group
G
that appears in Theorem
1.1.1
need not be nice.
We also can show that the result is not necessarily true if we do not assume that
\mathcal{X}
is ANS: see the remark just below.

Remark 1.1.4

(Counterexample to Atiyah-Segal completion)
.

In Section
8.3
, we show that the AS completion theorem for
K
-theory is
false
in general. The counterexample we offer is in the
\mathbb Q
-stack
B\mathbb{G}_{a}\times\operatorname{Spec}{\mathbb{Q}[\varepsilon]/\varepsilon^{2}}
, viewed as a stack over
BSL_{2}
via the identification
B\mathbb G_{a}\simeq[\mathbb A^{2}\smallsetminus\{0\}/SL_{2}]
. Our calculation is based on the Levy’s generalization
[
Lev22
]
of Dundas–Goodwillie–McCarthy theorem to
(-1)
-connective rings which reduces it to an explicit analysis of the negative cyclic homology
HC^{-}
.

The right-hand-side of the isomorphism in Theorem
1.1.1
is the value of
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}K
, the right Kan extension of the
K
-theory of derived affine schemes to stacks, on the stack
\mathcal{X}
. This invariant carries a natural filtration given by right Kan extension of the motivic filtration on schemes. We define
motivic filtration
on
K(\mathcal{X})
via the pullback square:

(1.1.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fil}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\star}K(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\mathrm{dAff}}\mathrm{Fil}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\star}K(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\mathrm{dAff}}K(\mathcal{X}).}

As explained in Example
1.0.3
, the motivic filtration on
K(\mathcal{X})
will often not be complete. On the other hand, at least if
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}
is of finite type,
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}\mathrm{Fil}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\star}K(\mathcal{X})
is complete, since it can be written as a limit of
K
-theory of finite type affine schemes, where motivic filtration is complete. This forces identification of the completion of
K(\mathcal{X})
with respect to motivic filtration and
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}\mathrm{Fil}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\star}K(\mathcal{X})
. In this terms, Theorem
1.1.1
can also be seen as a way to characterize this completion in a way that is more intrinsic to
\mathcal{X}
.

Corollary 1.1.6
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be as in Theorem
1.1.1
, then the completion of
K(\mathcal{X})
with respect to the motivic filtration in (
1.1.5
) is given by
K(\mathcal{X})^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}
.

We view this statement as our main contribution towards understanding motivic filtrations on
K
-theory beyond the case of schemes.

The main novelty of Theorem
1.1.1
is that it works generally without assuming any smoothness hypotheses on
\mathcal{X}
. In this situation algebraic
K
-theory is not
\mathbb A^{1}
-homotopy invariant, so many standard tools can not be applied. As with other results concerning algebraic
K
-theory of non-smooth schemes, we employ trace methods which are centered around the transformation
K\rightarrow TC
, from
K
-theory to
topological cyclic homology
. This map fits as the top part of the following square:

(1.1.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KH}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\mathrm{cdh}}TC.}

When restricted to quasicompact and quasiseparated schemes, this square is cartesian thanks to results of Kerz-Strunk-Tamme
[
KST17
]
and Land-Tamme
[
LT19
]
(we also refer to
[
EM23
, Theorem 3.8]
on how to deduce the general case from the noetherian case). The cartesian-ness of this square encodes two key phenomena:

(1)

that Weibel’s homotopy
K
-theory
KH
identifies with
\mathrm{cdh}
-sheafified
K
-theory
[
KST17
, Theorem 6.3]
;

(2)

that the fiber of the cyclotomic trace map is a
\mathrm{cdh}
sheaf
[
LT19
, Corollary 3.6]
.

In characteristic zero,
TC(-)\simeq HC^{-}(-/\mathbb Q)
, the invariant known as
negative cyclic homology
. In this setting, these
two ingredients are somewhat older; (1) was proved by Haesemeyer in his thesis
[
Hae04
]
and (2) was proved by
Cortiñas
[
Cn98
]
. Our work includes an extension of the square (
1.1.7
) to the case of ANS stacks, at
least assuming resolution of singularities, which allows to reduce Theorem
1.1.1
for
K
-theory to analogous results for its
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant approximation (known as
KH
) and for Hochschild homology
(and related theories, like
HC^{-}
). In the next two sections of the introduction we will discuss the relevant variants of Theorem
1.1.1
: namely, for
KH
and other
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant
localizing invariants (in Section
1.2
), and for localizing invariants born out of Hochshild homology (in Section
1.3
). Let us emphasize that the methods used in these two contexts are very different: the first relies crucially on equivariant resolution of singularities and cdh-descent, while the second uses the geometry of corresponding loop stacks.

1.2.
Completion theorems for
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant localizing invariants

Theorem
1.1.1
fits into the following general framework. Suppose that
E
is a localizing invariant and that we have a morphism
f\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow BG
where
\mathcal{X}
is a perfect stack; for the formulation of the statements, we do not need to assume anything about
f
, while the perfect stack
5
5

5

Recall that an algebraic stack is called
perfect
if its derived category of quasi-coherent sheaves
D_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})
is compactly generated by perfect complexes: in other words, if
D_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})\simeq\mathrm{Ind}(\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X}))
. In characteristic 0 many stacks are perfect
4
4

4

More generally, any quotient stack of a quasi-projective scheme by a linear algebraic group is perfect.
, in particular any quasi-compact ANS stack is.
assumption is simply to let us make sense of
E(\mathcal{X})
in terms of perfect complexes over
\mathcal{X}
. Given a
k
-linear localizing invariant
E
,
E(\mathcal{X})
naturally promotes to a
K(BG)
-module, and we obtain a
K(BG)
-linear map
E(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E(\mathcal{X})
. It is not hard to see (Corollary
5.3.5
) that the target here is always
I_{G}
-complete, and thus we obtain an induced map

(1.2.1)

E(\mathcal{X})^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}\rightarrow R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E(\mathcal{X}).

Given this setup, we say that the
Atiyah-Segal (AS) completion theorem holds
for
E,G,\mathcal{X}
if (
1.2.1
) is an equivalence. While there are no a priory reasons for this to always hold, we note that there are interesting precedents to the veracity of this statement.

So far, most AS completion theorems in the literature have involved those
E
’s which are also
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant. In this paper, using equivariant resolution of singularities and cdh-descent, we bootstrap the results of Tabuada-van den Bergh
[
TdB20
]
for quotient stacks of a smooth projective variety with a torus action, and get the following general form of AS completion theorem in the
\mathbb A^{1}
-invariant context.

Theorem 1.2.2
.

Fix
G
a reductive group over a characteristic zero field
k
and
E
a truncating localizing
invariant which is also
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant. Let
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow BG
be representable morphism. Assume that
\mathcal{X}
is
ANS and
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}\rightarrow BG
is finite type. Then we have an equivalence:

(1.2.3)

E(\mathcal{X})^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E(\mathcal{X}).

The conditions of being truncating and
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant are allied. The former implies that
E
satisfies cdh descent, as explained in
[
LT19
]
. On the other hand, for those
E
’s which are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant and also left Kan extended from smooth
\mathbb{Z}
-algebras Cisinski’s theorem in motivic homotopy theory
[
Cis13
]
implies cdh descent for
E
. A combination of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance and cdh descent is necessary to prove Theorem
1.2.2
in the stated generality. Previous incarnations of such theorems were proved in the case
X
is a smooth projective scheme or, more generally, smooth
filtrable
scheme (note that in these cases
KH
is equivalent to
K
). Note, however, that in Theorem
1.2.2
we get rid of any of these assumptions.

Remark 1.2.4

(Remark on geometric classifying spaces)
.

The formulation of previous iterations of the Atiyah-Segal completion theorem (see, for example,
[
Kri18
,
TdB20
]
), rather uses the geometric classifying space of Morel and Voevodsky
[
Mor
, Section 4.2]
, which imitates the classical Borel construction in algebraic topology. Such a construction was also used by Totaro to define Chow groups of classifying stacks
[
Tot16
]
. As follows from
[
KR22
, Theorem 3.6]
, in the truncating
\mathbb A^{1}
-invariant setup this agrees with right Kan extension from affine schemes.

This model also happens to work in a very specific situation of
BGL_{n}
without
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance thanks
to the work of Annala-Iwasa (see
[
AI22
, Theorem 3.1]
and
[
AHI23
, Theorem 5.3]
). But in general
such a model relies heavily on
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance and plainly does not work in more general settings. It is
therefore crucial, in order to prove Theorem
8.2.1
in the generality of not necessarily smooth stacks, that
our completion theorems are formulated uniformly both in the case of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant theories and Hochschild-type theories in terms of the
right Kan extension from derived affine schemes
.

The idea that evaluating on a geometric classifying space is equivalent to just taking the right Kan extension is an idea
that has been around in the community already for some time. The work of
[
KR21
]
, which we rely on, has used this elegantly to
provide an extension of the motivic six functor formalism to certain suitable stacks.

1.3.
Completion theorems for Hochschild homology and related theories

In an orthogonal direction, it remains to discuss AS completion theorem for the invariants built from Hochschild homology. The starting point here is an observation due to Ben-Zvi–Francis–Nadler
[
BZFN10
]
that for perfect stacks the
Hochshild homology

HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q):=HH(\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X})/\mathbb Q)
has a geometric interpretation as the ring of functions on the
loop stack

\mathcal{L}\mathcal{X}
, defined as the mapping
\mathbb Q
-stack from
S^{1}
to
\mathcal{X}
(see our Section
7.2.1
for a short review).
Out of
HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)
together with its natural
S^{1}
-action, one can then also extract some other invariants: the
negative cyclic homology

HC^{-}(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)
(as homotopy
S^{1}
-fixed points), as well as
cyclic homology

HC(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)
(as homotopy
S^{1}
-orbits) and
periodic cyclic homology

HP(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)
(as the Tate construction).

To prove the completion theorem for Hochschild homology, given
\mathcal{X}=[X/G]
we wish to compare the value of right Kan extension
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)
and the Atiyah-Segal completion
HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}
. It has been already observed in a slightly different form by Nadler–Ben-Zvi
[
BZN12
]
, and later Chen
[
Che20
]
, that these two versions of Hochschild homology correspond geometrically to completing
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{X}
along two closed substacks. Namely,

(1)

R^{\mathrm{dAff}}HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)
is given by global functions on the derived completion
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\mathcal{X}
along the substack of
constant loops

\mathcal{X}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{L}\mathcal{X}
. This was observed by Ben-Zvi–Nadler in
[
BZN12
]
; we revisit their results in Appendix
A
to make sure they remain true in the required generality (see Remark
1.3.2
).

(2)

The other completion is given by the stack of
unipotent loops

\underline{\mathcal{M}\mathrm{aps}}(B\mathbb{G}_{a},\mathcal{X})=:\mathcal{L}^{u}\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}\mathcal{X}.

As discussed in
[
Che20
]
one can view
\mathcal{L}^{u}\mathcal{X}
as the completion of
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{X}
at a closed substack that picks up the unipotent elements in the stabilizer group of every point. The map to
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{X}
is induced by the affinization map
S^{1}\rightarrow B\mathbb{G}_{a}
. As we discuss in Section
7.2.17
, one has a natural identification
HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}\simeq\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{L}^{u}\mathcal{X})
.

This way, the completion theorem for
HH
reduces to showing that the map
{\mathcal{L}}^{u}\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\mathcal{X}
between the two stacky completions is an isomorphism. A pleasant observation is that this is essentially equivalent to stabilizers of all points of
\mathcal{X}
being nice or, in other words, the stack
\mathcal{X}
should be ANS. This gives the following:

Theorem 1.3.1
.

Fix
G
a reductive group over a characteristic zero field
k
. Let
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow BG
be representable morphism where
\mathcal{X}
is derived algebraic stack, such that the morphism
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}\rightarrow BG
is of finite type. Assume further that
\mathcal{X}
is ANS. Then we have canonical equivalences

\displaystyle HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}\operatorname*{lim}_{\operatorname{Spec}R\rightarrow\mathcal{X}}HH(R)

\displaystyle HC^{-}(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}\operatorname*{lim}_{\operatorname{Spec}R\rightarrow\mathcal{X}}HC^{-}(R)

\displaystyle HP(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}\operatorname*{lim}_{\operatorname{Spec}R\rightarrow\mathcal{X}}HP(R)

\displaystyle HC(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}\operatorname*{lim}_{\operatorname{Spec}R\rightarrow\mathcal{X}}HC(R).

In fact, while the case of
HC^{-}
formally follows from the case
HH
, the case of
HP
is a particular case of our completion theorem for
\mathbb A^{1}
-localizing invariants (Theorem
1.2.2
); both then imply the statement for
HC
.

The work of Chen
[
Che20
]
was the first to consider both completions
{\mathcal{L}}^{u}\mathcal{X}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\mathcal{X}
in a systematic way. He proved a version of the Atiyah-Segal completion theorem for
HP
for stacks over an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero (
[
Che20
, Theorem B]
), and Theorem
1.3.1
can be considered as a partial generalization of his result. Note that Chen’s theorem does not require
\mathcal{X}
to be ANS; however, this is due to
\mathbb A^{1}
-invariance of
HP
which eliminates the difference between the two completions (since, morally,
\mathbb A^{1}
-invariance makes the unipotent cone of any group “contractible”). In Remark
7.3.2
we show that the Atiyah-Segal completion theorem for the other Hochschild-type invariants does not hold without the ANS assumption on
\mathcal{X}
.

Let us also note that the actual definition of unipotent loops that we will consider in the body of the paper is slightly more ad hoc than the one we used above. The relation between the two is explained in Remark
7.2.27
.

Remark 1.3.2

(A remark on derived completions and
[
BZN12
]
)
.

One of the main results of Ben-Zvi–Nadler in
[
BZN12
]
is the identification of
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\mathcal{X}
and the completed shifted cotangent bundle
\widehat{\mathbb T}_{\mathcal{X}}[-1]
; using descent for cotangent complex one can then show that the construction
\mathcal{X}\mapsto\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\mathcal{X}
is left Kan extended from affine schemes. However, it is never specified in their work what they mean by derived completion, and if one follows their proof the definition is not completely clear, e.g.
\widehat{\mathbb T}_{\mathcal{X}}[-1]
is understood as a colimit of
n
-th neighbourhoods
\operatorname*{colim}_{n}\mathbb T_{\mathcal{X}}^{\leqslant n}
of the zero section. In fact, as we discuss in Remark
7.1.27
this is not quite accurate: namely, this formula will typically give a non-convergent stack (unless
\mathcal{X}
is smooth), and thus can’t agree with
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\mathcal{X}
(given the latter is defined via some sort of de Rham stack). Nevertheless, in Proposition
7.1.26
we show that the analogous
convergent
colimit does agree with other versions of formal completion (which we review in Section
7.1
), at least if
H^{0}(\mathbb L_{\mathcal{X}})
is locally finitely generated (which is true if
\mathcal{X}
is algebraic).

Another issue is deformation theory, to which the authors appeal in their proof. In a non necessarily finite type situation, to have access to usual deformation theory tools, one needs to use a slightly non-standard definition of de Rham stack (called the “absolute de Rham stack” in
[
Lur18
]
) to define the formal completion. There is also a Simpson’s formal completion
6
6

6

The completion
\mathcal{Y}^{\wedge}_{f}
along a map
f\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
is then defined as the fiber product
\mathcal{Y}\times_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{dR}}}\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{dR}}
.
, with the corresponding de Rham stack given by
\mathcal{X}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(R):=\mathcal{X}(\pi_{0}(R)^{\mathrm{red}})
, which is more convenient to use in practice. Note that in our situation, if
\mathcal{X}
an algebraic stack that is finite type over a field
k/\mathbb Q
, we are interested in
HH(-/\mathbb Q)
and thus are forced to consider
\mathcal{X}
as an algebraic stack over
\mathbb Q
, where it typically will not be of finite type. In Section
7.1
we systematically analyze the difference between these two versions of derived completion, showing that they are the same if the map of underlying classical stacks is locally finitely presented. This then applies in particular to completion at constant loops
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}\mathcal{X}
, when
\mathcal{X}
is an algebraic stack. More generally, we fully revisit the proof of Ben-Zvi–Nadler, making sure all steps work in the required generality (this is in Appendix
A
).

We would like to warn the reader that these various forms of formal completion (at constant loops and/or zero section) are only compatible if we take algebraic stacks in the sense that they have a
smooth
representable cover by the union of derived affine schemes. For algebraic stacks in the
fpqc
sense,
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)
will be given by functions on yet another “neighborhood completion”, details on which we might add in a sequel.

In the upcoming
[
RB
]
, Brav and Rozenblyum also give another perspective on the Ben-Zvi–Nadler theorem. Their argument is slightly different and generalizes the result in a different direction, namely for laft prestacks admitting a deformation theory. In other words, their proof significantly relaxes the geometricity condition on the stack, while remaining in a finite type situation.

1.4.
Relation with previous and contemporaneous works

We briefly survey the history of Atiyah-Segal completions in algebraic geometry as well as what is new in this paper in both generality and philosophy; we apologize to the many authors in this very rich field in advance for sins of omission. The subject began with Thomason’s construction of equivariant
K
and
G
-theories
[
Tho87
]
where an equivariant version of Quillen’s basic results on
K
-theory were proved. Perhaps inspired by Atiyah–Segal’s work
[
AS69
]
, Thomason proved a completion theorem for
G
-theory and
K
-theory in the smooth setting
[
Tho88
]
after
K(1)
-localization. The usual dévissage of reducing from a general reductive group scheme, to a torus and eventually to a diagonalizable group with trivial action was already explained in this work; we remark that this approach heavily relies on
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant properties to pass from a Borel subgroup to a torus. The relationship between equivariant
G
-theory and algebraic cycles on stacks, a shadow of the story about motivic filtrations, was first explored by Edidin–Graham
[
EG08
,
EG00
]
. The viewpoint there is very natural: up to completion and rationalization the Chow groups of algebraic stacks are discovered as the graded pieces of a split filtration on
G_{0}
.

With the advent of motivic homotopy theory and noncommutative motives, the completion theorems take on a richer flavor. In particular, Morel–Voevodsky’s construction of their geometric classifying space provides a target for the completed
K
-theory and other invariants. We mention the work of
[
CJ23
]
which deals with
G
-theory and
[
TVdB18
]
which proves similarly flavored theorems in the case of Deligne–Mumford stacks/orbifolds. Three papers which are very influential for us are Tabuada–van den Bergh’s definitive account of the completion theorem in the torus case for
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant localizing invariants (which we use)
[
TdB20
]
, Krishna’s
K
-theoretic completion theorem in the smooth, projective setting for
K
-theory
[
Kri18
]
and Chen’s work on equivariant hochschild homology
[
Che20
]
. These papers work with cohomological invariants such
K
-theory and Hochschild homology, as opposed to earlier accounts which deals with
G
-theory, and Borel-Moore type invariants. The complexion of the problem changes entirely — there is a lack of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance (if the input stack is not smooth) and there is a lack of localization-type results, both are standard approaches to the completion problem even in topology.

The novelty of our result is that it is
K
-theoretic (as opposed to
G
-theoretic) and is, as far as we know, the first to deal with stacks which are not smooth, reduced or even classical. This accounts for some of limitations (such as ANS) of our results. The new idea that we use is trace methods, combining an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant completion theorem with one for negative cyclic homology, and relying on the first and last authors’ extension of the Goodwillie theorem in
[
ES21
]
to the world of stacks. Like the work of Edidin-Graham we are also motivated by motives — one can view our results as an integral and higher refinement of their results where Chow groups are replaced by the
(2n,n)
-line of motivic cohomology in the sense of
[
EM23
]
.

Lastly, around the same time as this work, Krishna and Nath
[
KN25
]
also independently explored the completion theorem. We remark on the overlap and distinctions between their work and ours:

(1)

Given a non-smooth algebraic stack
\mathcal{X}
,
[
KN25
, Theorem 1.1]
proves completion results for the
G
-theory of
\mathcal{X}
; the generality here asks that
\mathcal{X}
is a quotient stack by a reductive group. On the other hand, our Theorem
1.1.1
is
K
-theoretic and only works with added hypotheses on the stabilizers of
\mathcal{X}
. Indeed, the main foci of the two papers somewhat differs — theirs follow, and in some sense complete, the tradition of
G
-theoretic completion theorems, while our aim is to explore the less-chartered
K
-theoretic territory.

(2)

Our Theorem
1.2.2
subsumes
[
KN25
, Theorem 1.6, Corollary 1.7]
in characteristic zero and our Theorem
1.3.1
subsumes
[
KN25
, Corollary 1.11, Theorem 1.14]
in that we do not need conditions on the stabilizers, the smoothness of
X
or algebraic-closedness of the base field. However, we mention the elegant
[
KN25
, Lemma 13.6]
which controls the completion of the cyclic homology term which should be useful for other future applications.

(3)

A very interesting aspect of
[
KN25
]
is that they also considered completions at other ideals, we do not address this at all in this paper.

1.5.
Outline

We begin the paper with Section
2
. The point of this section is to illustrate via examples one of the main perspectives that we take in this paper: that completion at the Atiyah-Segal ideal restores certain descent properties that one should expect for various invariants in the setting of algebraic stacks. In the preliminaries Section
3
, we review the derived algebraic geometry and the theory of localizing invariants that we will need in this paper. We work in the generality of animated rings over
\mathbb{Z}
, something more than necessary for the main results of this paper (which is over
\mathbb{Q}
) in view of the completion theorems in positive and mixed characteristics that will constitute the sequel to this paper. In Section
4
we discuss descent properties for cohomology theories of derived stacks. In particular, we introduce
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E
, the right-hand-side of the Atiyah-Segal completion theorem and establish its basic properties.

In Section
5
we define the Atiyah-Segal ideal and formulate the completion theorem. A useful result we will
turn to repeatedly is the “change-of-groups” Lemma
5.3.2
. This ensures that even
though we are working with stacks which are ANS, and hence its stabilizers are of a fairly restrictive class, we are allowed to complete with respect to
I_{G}
where
G
is a reductive group under the stack. Our first main theorem is established in Section
6
where we improve on the Tabuada-Van den Bergh completion theorem using cdh excision. Section
7
proves the completion theorem for Hochschild invariants — in particular for
HH,HC^{-},HC
and
HP
, which is our second main theorem. Putting all of this together in Section
8
, we prove the Atiyah-Segal completion theorem for ANS stacks in characteristic zero. In Section
9
, we discuss applications of our main theorem to building coherent transfers (aka spectral Mackey functors) on completed
K
-theory as well as propose a candidate for motivic cohomology of certain stacks.

The present paper constitutes three appendices. In Appendix
A
we revisit the Ben-Zvi–Nadler’s geometric version of the HKR theorem for geometric stacks.
It also contains certain constructions in derived algebraic geometry that we could not locate in suitable generality in the literature, including different versions of completions vector bundles and some cotangent complex related descent results. In Appendix
B
we extend a result of Deshmukh, which builds on work of Moret-Bailly, to derived stacks. It is a strong Nisnevich-local structural result for covering of stacks that we will need to control
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E
. Lastly, in Appendix
C
we record a (relatively straightforward) extension of the motivic filtration on schemes to derived algebraic spaces.

1.6.
Vista

This is the beginning of a series of papers on motivic filtrations on algebraic stacks, centered around completion theorems in
K
-theory and its cousins. As already laid out in this paper, our philosophy is that Atiyah-Segal completion theorems provide intrinsic descriptions of completions of genuine invariants along an induced motivic filtration. In the sequel, we plan to establish completion theorems for topological Hochschild and cyclic homology. While we build on the results of Ben-Zvi–Nadler in characteristic zero, the strategy in mixed and positive characteristics is new and more involved.

Towards this end we are working on a project, joint with Annala, in using the machinery of (derived) Theta-stratifications
[
HL20
]
, coupled with the second author’s work on
p
-adic Hodge theory
[
KP21
]
to “induct along the schematic locus” of a derived algebraic stack. We hope to use this strategy to establish a completion theorem for a nice enough collection of algebraic stacks in mixed and positive characteristics.

p
-adic geometry”; we apologize to (but also thank) the organizers, Jacob Lurie and Bhargav Bhatt that the work produced is in characteristic zero (for now).
