2.
Examples and phenomenology

We begin with an informal explanation of some examples of the phenomena that this paper aims to capture. To simplify matters all schemes and stacks in this section are classical. Let
E:\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathrm{Spt}
be a localizing invariant; we review this concept in more detail in Section
3.2
. The value of
E
on a qcqs scheme
X
is defined to be
E(X):=E(\mathrm{Perf}(X))
and the functor
X\mapsto E(X)
enjoys some reasonable descent properties. At the very least, it satisfies Nisnevich descent, as already is implicit in the work of Thomason-Trobaugh
[
TT90
]
(see, for example,
[
CMNN20
, Proposition A.15]
). Often, one can actually do better: the functors constructed out of trace considerations like
THH,HH(-/k)
,
TC
and their cousins in fact satisfy flat descent
[
BMS19
, Corollary 3.4]
, and so do telescopically-localized invariants
[
CMNN20
, Theorem 5.1]
.

We point out two further features of the functor
X\mapsto E(X)
. First, by the definition of a localizing invariant,
E(X)
is amenable to calculations from derived-categorical techniques. The point is that any localizing invariant splits semiorthogonal decomposition; for example Beilinson’s decomposition of
\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}_{X}^{n})

[
Bei78
]

\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}_{X}^{n})\simeq\langle\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}(1),\cdots,\mathcal{O}(n)\rangle,

leads to the projective bundle formula:
E(\mathbb{P}^{n}_{X})\simeq\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}E(X)
. Second, the functoriality of the assignment
X\mapsto\mathrm{Perf}(X)
endows
E(X)
with additional structure. For example, if
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a morphism then the pullback functor
f^{*}:\mathrm{Perf}(Y)\rightarrow\mathrm{Perf}(X)
admits a right adjoint
f_{*}
whenever
f
is proper and has finite tor-amplitude
[
Stacks
, Tag 0B6G]
. This endows the functor
X\mapsto E(X)
with the structure of transfers (more precisely, it organizes into a spectral Mackey functor as in
[
Bar17
]
), which are often useful for computations and are central to proving descent for rationalized/telescopically-localized localizing invariants, as was done in
[
CMNN20
]
. Under these considerations it is reasonable, for
\mathcal{X}
an algebraic stack, to set
E(\mathcal{X}):=E(\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X}))
. Just as in the case of schemes, such a functor splits semiorthogonal decomposition of stacks and admits transfers along proper morphisms of stacks that have finite tor-amplitude.

However, this definition has a major drawback, related to
descent
. We illustrate this in the next two examples. For simplicity, we work with localizing invariants linear over a field
k
of characteristic zero and consider the case of
E=HH(-/k)
, Hochschild homology. To formulate descent, we will also use the language of
E
(-universal) descent which we review in Definition
4.1.1
.

Example 2.0.1

(Étale coverings of Deligne-Mumford stacks are not of
HH(-/k)
-descent.)
.

The morphism
\operatorname{Spec}k\rightarrow B\mu_{n}
is an étale covering (recall the characteristic zero hypothesis). If
\operatorname{Spec}k\rightarrow B\mu_{n}
were of
HH
-descent, then we would have equivalences

(2.0.2)

HH(B\mu_{n}/k)\simeq\operatorname*{lim}_{[m]\in\Delta}HH(\mu_{n}^{\times m}/k)\simeq\operatorname*{lim}_{[n]\in\Delta}\mathcal{O}(\mu_{n}^{\times m})\simeq\mathcal{O}(B\mu_{n}).

Via Cartier duality, the category
\mathrm{Perf}(B\mu_{n})
is canonically equivalent to
\mathbb{Z}/n
-graded objects in
\mathrm{Perf}(k)
, in particular

\mathrm{Perf}(B\mu_{n})\simeq\mathrm{Perf}(k)^{\oplus\mathbb{Z}/n}

as small stable
\infty
-categories. Therefore, we get that
HH(B\mu_{n}/k)\simeq k[\mathbb Z/n]
. On the other hand,
\mathcal{O}(B\mu_{n})\simeq k\neq k[\mathbb Z/n]
.

Example 2.0.3

(Nisnevich surjections of Artin stacks are not of
HH(-/k)
-descent.)
.

Since any line bundle is Zariski-locally trivial, the standard covering map
\operatorname{Spec}k\rightarrow B\mathbb{G}_{m}
is a Zariski, and, in particular, Nisnevich surjection. If the morphism
\operatorname{Spec}k\rightarrow B\mathbb{G}_{m}
were of
HH
-descent, then we would have an equivalence:

(2.0.4)

HH(B\mathbb{G}_{m}/k)\simeq\operatorname*{lim}_{[n]\in\Delta}HH(\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times n}/k).

The HKR isomorphism furnishes an equivalence
HH(\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times n}/k)\simeq R\Gamma(\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times n};\prod_{i}\Sym^{i}(\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times n}}[1]))
. By descent for cotangent complex (see Proposition
A.2.1
) we then conclude that

\operatorname*{lim}_{[n]\in\Delta}HH(\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times n}/k)\simeq\prod^{\infty}_{i=0}R\Gamma(B\mathbb{G}_{m},\prod_{i}\Sym^{i}\left(\mathbb{L}_{B\mathbb{G}_{m}}[1])\right).

Now, the cotangent complex of a classifying stack
BG
is identified as
\mathbb{L}_{BG}\simeq\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}[-1]
(see, for example,
[
KP21
, Example A.3.8]
) where
\mathfrak{g}
is the Lie algebra of
G
. Therefore, the right-hand-side simplifies to
\prod_{i}R\Gamma(B\mathbb{G}_{m},\prod_{i}\Sym^{i}(\mathcal{O}_{B\mathbb G_{m}}))\simeq\prod_{i}k
. On the other hand, since
HH(-/k)
is a
finitary
localizing invariant and
\mathrm{Perf}(B\mathbb G_{m})\simeq\oplus_{i\in\mathbb Z}\mathrm{Perf}(k)
, we have that
HH(B\mathbb{G}_{m}/k)\simeq\bigoplus_{i}k
. We see that they cannot be the same.

Examples
2.0.1
and
2.0.3
suggest an alternative extension of
HH(-/k)
to algebraic stacks, one which
enjoys better descent properties and ensures that (
2.0.2
) and (
2.0.4
) holds. Consider
E
as a functor on
affine schemes and for an algebraic stack
\mathcal{X}
, we set

R^{\mathrm{Aff}}E(\mathcal{X}):=\operatorname*{lim}_{\operatorname{Spec}R\rightarrow\mathcal{X}}E(\operatorname{Spec}R).

This procedure is nothing but a right Kan extension and will be discussed more extensively in Section
4.2
. As
explained Lemma
4.2.1
,
R^{\mathrm{Aff}}E
inherits descent properties that
E
has in the following sense: if
E

is an
\tau
-sheaf on affine schemes, then any
\tau
-surjection of stacks is of universal
R^{\mathrm{Aff}}E
-descent. For
example if
E=HH(-/k)
, then

R^{\mathrm{Aff}}HH(B\mu_{n}/k)\simeq\mathcal{O}(B\mu_{n})\simeq k\qquad R^{\mathrm{Aff}}HH(B\mathbb{G}_{m}/k)\simeq\prod_{i}R\Gamma(B\mathbb{G}_{m},\prod_{i}\Sym^{i}(\mathcal{O}_{B\mathbb G_{m}}))\simeq\prod_{i}k.

Additionally, as already explained in the introduction,
R^{\mathrm{Aff}}E
inherits filtrations that
E
has on affine schemes. Of particular interest,
R^{\mathrm{Aff}}K
of a stack comes readily equipped with motivic filtrations.

The drawback of this extension is that it is not clear at all that
R^{\mathrm{Aff}}E
splits semiorthogonal decompositions and admits coherent transfers. The Atiyah-Segal completion theorem can be read as the rather surprising assertion that, in fact,

R^{\mathrm{Aff}}E
does. Theorem
1.1.1
presents an alternative formula
R^{\mathrm{Aff}}E(\mathcal{X})
, in certain cases,
which does not require the data of all affine schemes mapping into
\mathcal{X}
. This alternate formula instead asks for the data of
a
K(BG)
-module structure on
E(\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X}))
. We illustrate this theorem in light of Examples
2.0.1
and
2.0.3
.

Example 2.0.5

(The Atiyah-Segal completion for
HH(-/k)
)
.

We compute
R^{\mathrm{Aff}}HH(-/k)
of
B\mathbb{G}_{m}
and
B\mu_{n}
via the
Atiyah-Segal completion theorem. Both computations could be performed easily via descent and, in fact, already implicitly
carried out in Examples
2.0.1
and
2.0.3
. We hope that their computation via the completion theorem would
be illustrative for the reader.

Using the lax-monoidal structure on
HH
, the above computations of
HH(B\mathbb{G}_{m}/k)
and
HH(B\mu_{n}/k)
enhance to equivalences of derived commutative rings:

HH(B\mathbb{G}_{m}/k)\simeq k[\mathbb Z]\simeq k[t,t^{-1}]\qquad HH(B\mu_{n}/k)\simeq k[\mathbb Z/n]\simeq k[t]/(t^{n}-1)

(both rings being concentrated in cohomological degree 0). The Atiyah-Segal ideal in
HH
in both cases is generated by the element
t-1
; we refer to Example
5.2.4
and Proposition
7.2.26
for details.

The
(t-1)
-adic completion of
k[t]/(t^{n}-1)
picks out the component
k[t]/(t-1)\cong k
via the isomorphism
k[t]/(t^{n}-1)\cong k[t]/(t-1)\times k[t]/(t^{n-1}+t^{n-2}+\cdots+1)
, and therefore, by the Atiyah-Segal completion theorem, we have that:

R^{\mathrm{Aff}}HH(B\mu_{m})\simeq(k[t]/(t^{n}-1))^{\wedge}_{(t-1)}\simeq k[t]/(t-1)=k.

For
B\mathbb G_{m}
, the
(t-1)
-adic completion
k[t,t^{-1}]^{\wedge}_{(t-1)}
is simply given by power series in
t-1
, and thus we get

R^{\mathrm{Aff}}HH(B\mathbb{G}_{m})\simeq k[t,t^{-1}]^{\wedge}_{(t-1)}\simeq k[[t-1]].

Example 2.0.6

(Rational
K
-theory of stacks)
.

Another interesting invariant which enjoys étale descent for schemes is rationalized algebraic
K
-theory. As already pointed out in
[
CMNN20
, Remark A.16]
, it does note enjoy descent along the simplest étale covering of stacks
\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}\rightarrow BC_{2}
. Our main results also apply to establish étale descent for Atiyah-Segal completed rational algebraic
K
-theory.

Example 2.0.7

(Right Kan extended
K
-theory)
.

In the
HH
situations, the right Kan extended theories are easily computed because
HH
enjoys étale descent on schemes. For
K
-theory, which infamously does not, the right Kan extended theories more easily computed via the Atiyah-Segal completion and semiorthogonal decompositions of categories. For example, from our main theorem we easily read off:

R^{\mathrm{Aff}}K(B\mathbb{G}_{m})\simeq(K(k)[t,t^{-1}])^{\wedge}_{(t-1)}\simeq K(k)[[t-1]].

3.
Preliminaries

3.1.
Generalities on prestacks and stacks

In this section we make a review of notions of derived algebraic geometry that we will use. We also set up notations and conventions for the paper which, to warn the reader, might differ slightly from the standard ones.

While most of the results of this paper concern derived algebraic stacks over characteristic zero fields, we still aim to set up notations and foundations for future use (particularly the sequel where will investigate the Atiyah-Segal completion theorems in positive and mixed characteristics). The paper also contains some general results that don’t require characteristic 0 assumption (like the motivic filtration on K-theory of derived algebraic spaces in Appendix
C
). This is why below we will set up everything in the context of
\mathbb Z
-linear derived algebraic geometry.

We sometimes refer to
[
GR17a
,
GR17b
]
which assumes that the base is a field of characteristic zero throughout, however, for the results that we cite it is never important.

3.1.1.
Derived prestacks

Let
\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{cl}}
denote the 1-category of classical commutative rings.
Let
\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}}:=\mathrm{Ani}(\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{cl}})
denote the
\infty
-category of animated commutative rings, given by “animation” of
\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{cl}}
. Recall that by definition this is the
\infty
-category of product preserving functors from polynomial
\mathbb Z
-algebras,
\mathrm{PSh}_{\Sigma}(\mathrm{Poly}Z)\simeq\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}}
. Given an animated ring
R
we denote by
\mathrm{CAlg}{}_{R}^{\mathrm{an}}
the under category
(\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}})_{R/}
. In case
R
is classical, we have
\mathrm{CAlg}{}_{R}^{\mathrm{an}}:=\mathrm{Ani}(\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{cl}}_{R})
. If
R
is a
\mathbb Q
-algebra, then
\mathrm{CAlg}{}_{R}^{\mathrm{an}}
is naturally identified with the category
\mathrm{CAlg}{}(\mathrm{D}(R)_{\geqslant 0})
of
\mathbb E_{\infty}
-algebras in connective
R
-modules.

Definition 3.1.2
.

We say that
A\in\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}}
is

(1)

n
-truncated
if
\pi_{i}(A)=0
for
i>n
;

(2)

truncated
if it is
n
-truncated for some
n\geqslant 0
.

We denote by

\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an},[0,\infty)}\subset\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}}

the full subcategory spanned by truncated animated commutative rings.

Definition 3.1.3
.

Let
R\in\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}}
. Recall the following notions:

(1)

A
derived prestack
over
R
is a functor
\mathcal{X}\colon\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{Spc}
; we denote by
\mathrm{dPStk}_{R}
the
\infty
-category of derived prestacks over
R
.

(2)

A prestack
\mathcal{X}
is called
convergent
if

\mathcal{X}(A)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname*{lim}_{n}\mathcal{X}(\tau_{\leqslant n}A)

for any
A\in\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}}_{R}
.

We will denote the full subcategory spanned by convergent derived prestacks by

\mathrm{dPStk}^{\mathrm{conv}}_{R}\subset\mathrm{dPStk}_{R}.

Example 3.1.4
.

We denote by
\operatorname{Spec}A
the prestack represented by an animated algebra
A\in\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}}_{R}
. We call such prestacks
derived affine schemes
and denote by
\mathrm{dAff}_{R}\subset\mathrm{dPStk}_{R}
the corresponding full subcategory. By Yoneda’s lemma
\mathrm{dAff}_{R}\simeq(\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}}_{R})^{\mathrm{op}}
.

Example 3.1.5
.

Let
S\in\mathrm{Spc}
be a space. A functor
\underline{S}
sending any
A\in\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}}_{R}
to
S\in\mathrm{Spc}
is the
constant prestack
associated to
S
. Functor
S\mapsto\underline{S}
commutes with all limits and colimits. In particular, we have
\underline{*}\simeq\operatorname{Spec}R
is the final object and

\underline{S}\simeq\operatorname*{colim}_{S}(\operatorname{Spec}R),

where the diagram in the colimit is constant with value
\operatorname{Spec}R
.

Remark 3.1.6
.

Restricting to
\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an},[0,\infty)}\subset\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}}
induces an equivalence

\mathrm{dPStk}^{\mathrm{conv}}_{R}\simeq\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an},[0,\infty)}_{R},\mathrm{Spc})

with the inverse functor given by right Kan extension. The embedding

\mathrm{dPStk}^{\mathrm{conv}}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{dPStk}_{R}

commutes with limits, but typically not colimits. E.g. given an animated algebra
A\in\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}}_{R}
which is not necessarily truncated we have

\operatorname{Spec}A\simeq\operatorname*{colim}_{n}\operatorname{Spec}\tau_{\leqslant n}A

in
\mathrm{dPStk}^{\mathrm{conv}}_{R}
, but typically not in
\mathrm{dPStk}_{R}
since the identity map
A\rightarrow A
doesn’t need to factor through any of the truncations
\tau_{\leqslant n}A
.

Example 3.1.7
.

Any affine derived scheme
\mathcal{X}:=\operatorname{Spec}A
or a constant prestack
\underline{S}
is convergent.

Sometimes, we will also need to consider classical prestacks.

Definition 3.1.8
.

Let
R
be a classical ring. A
classical prestack
over
R
is a functor
\mathcal{X}\colon\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{cl}}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{Spc}
. We denote by
\mathrm{PStk}_{R}
the category of classical prestacks over
R
.

Let
R\in\mathrm{CAlg}{}_{R}^{\mathrm{an}}
. The fully faithful embedding
\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{cl}}_{\pi_{0}(R)}\subset\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}}_{R}
defines a restriction functor

\mathrm{dPStk}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{PStk}_{\pi_{0}(R)},\quad\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}.

We will call
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}
the underlying classical stack of
\mathcal{X}
. We also have the left Kan extension along the above inclusion, which gives a functor

L^{\mathrm{cl}}\colon\mathrm{PStk}_{\pi_{0}(R)}\rightarrow\mathrm{dPStk}_{R}

in the other direction.

Definition 3.1.9
.

The
classical truncation

\tau^{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathcal{X})
of
\mathcal{X}
is defined as

\tau^{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathcal{X}):=L^{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}).

By adjunction, we have a natural map
\tau^{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
.

Example 3.1.10
.

If
\mathcal{X}=\operatorname{Spec}A
is affine, then
\tau^{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathcal{X})=\operatorname{Spec}\pi_{0}(A)
. The map
\tau^{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
is induced by the natural map
A\rightarrow\pi_{0}(A)
.

3.1.11.
Quasi-coherent sheaves

Given a derived prestack
\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{dPStk}_{R}
one defines the
\infty
-category
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})
of
quasi-coherent sheaves on
\mathcal{X}
via right Kan extension from derived affine schemes; explicitly this is given as

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X}):=\operatorname*{lim}_{\operatorname{Spec}A\rightarrow\mathcal{X}}\mathrm{D}(A)

where
\mathrm{D}(A)
is the unbounded derived
\infty
-category of
A
-modules. This is a stable, presentable
\infty
-category equipped with a symmetric monoidal structure coming from the limit of symmetric monoidal structures on
\mathrm{D}(A)
. By construction, if
\mathcal{X}=\operatorname{Spec}A
one has
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})\simeq\mathrm{D}(A)
. A map of prestacks
f\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
induces the
pull-back functor

f^{*}\colon\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{Y})\rightarrow\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X}).

The functor
f^{*}
commutes with colimits and thus has a right adjoint which we denote by

f_{*}\colon\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{Y}).

In the case of the final object
\mathcal{Y}=\operatorname{Spec}R
in
\mathrm{dPStk}_{R}
and the projection
p\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}R
the pushforward
p_{*}
is identified with the
global sections functor

\Gamma(\mathcal{X},-)\colon\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\mathrm{D}(R).

Example 3.1.12
.

The association
(\operatorname{Spec}A\rightarrow\mathcal{X})\mapsto A\in\mathrm{D}(A)
defines an object
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})
which is called the
structure sheaf
. We will often denote
\Gamma(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}})
as
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{X})
. It can be explicitly computed as

\Gamma(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}})\simeq\operatorname*{lim}_{\operatorname{Spec}A\rightarrow\mathcal{X}}A.

An object
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})
is called a
perfect complex
if it is dualizable in the sense of the
symmetric monoidal structure. We denote by
\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X})\subset\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})
the full subcategory spanned by perfect sheaves. We also have

\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X})\simeq\operatorname*{lim}_{\operatorname{Spec}A\rightarrow\mathcal{X}}\mathrm{Perf}(A);

see, for example, the discussion at the beginning of
[
HLP23
, Appendix A]
. If
\mathcal{X}\colon\mathrm{dAff}^{\mathrm{op}}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{Spc}
is an accessible functor, then
\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X})
is a small
\infty
-category (see e.g.
[
HP24
, Proposition A.2]
). In practice, we will only consider the category
\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X})
when
\mathcal{X}
is accessible.

Example 3.1.13
.

Since
A\in\mathrm{Perf}(A)
for any
A
we get that the structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})
is a perfect sheaf on
\mathcal{X}
.

3.1.14.
Stacks

By a
derived stack
over
R
we mean a prestack which is a sheaf in the étale topology
7
7

7

We remark that this is different from the convention in some sources where a stack is supposed to be a sheaf in the flat topology; see, for example,
[
Stacks
, Tag 026N]
.
on
\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}}_{R}
(as in
[
TV08
, Definition 2.2.2.12]
or similar to
[
GR17a
, Chapter 2]
). We denote by
\mathrm{dStk}_{R}\subset\mathrm{dPStk}_{R}
the full subcategory spanned by derived stacks. The fully faithful embedding
\mathrm{dStk}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{dPStk}_{R}
has a left adjoint

L_{\text{\'{e}t}}\colon\mathrm{dPStk}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{dStk}_{R}

given by étale sheafification. The functors
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}
,
\mathrm{Perf}
,
\mathcal{O}
are right Kan extensions of functors on derived affine schemes that are sheaves in étale topology. As such, they factor through
L_{\text{\'{e}t}}
: namely, given a prestack
\mathcal{X}
, the map

\mathcal{F}(L_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}\mathcal{X})\xrightarrow{}\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X})

(induced by canonical map
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow L_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}\mathcal{X}
) is an equivalence for
\mathcal{F}
being any of the functors above.

We will reserve the word
surjection
for an étale-effective epimorphism (=étale surjection). Namely, a map of derived stacks
f\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
will be called a surjection if the induced map
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\pi_{0}(\mathcal{Y})
is a surjection of étale sheaves of sets. Here by
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{X})\colon\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{Set}
we mean the étale sheafification of the functor which sends
R
to
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{X}(R))
.

3.1.15.
Algebraic and geometric stacks

We now carefully state our conventions on algebraicity of stacks; we follow quite closely
[
GR17a
, Chap. 2, Sec. 4]
, except we will also make systematic use of derived algebraic spaces which
are omitted in the reference. For a Grothendieck topology
\tau
on
\mathrm{dAff}_{R}
, we will denote by
\mathrm{dStk}_{R}^{\tau}\subset\mathrm{dPStk}_{R}
the full subcategory spanned by
\tau
-sheaves. We will also make use of the
sheafification functor

L_{\tau}\colon\mathrm{dPStk}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{dStk}^{\tau}_{R},

whenever the latter exists, in particular for the Nisnevich and étale topologies which feature prominently in this paper.

If
\mathbf{P}
is a property of a map of (derived) affine schemes, then we say that a morphism of (derived) prestacks
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
is
affine and is
\mathbf{P}
if for any map
\operatorname{Spec}A\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
, the pullback
\operatorname{Spec}A\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}A
is affine and is
\mathbf{P}
. Here
\mathbf{P}
could be empty, in which case we get a definition of affine map of derived prestacks.

The derived stacks we consider this paper typically are examples of
n
-Artin stacks (defined as in
[
GR17a
, Chap. 2, Sec. 4]
or
[
TV08
, Sec 1.3.3]
for the original reference). We will not recall the general definition of
n
-Artin stack, since all the important examples in the paper are at most 2-Artin. Instead, below we make a short glossary for different types of “algebraic” stacks that the reader will meet in the paper. We warn that our terminological conventions might diverge slightly from those in some other sources.

Definition 3.1.16
.

We fix the following terminology throughout the paper:

(1)

A stack
\mathcal{X}
over
R
is called
derived 0-Artin
if it is isomorphic to
L_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\sqcup_{i\in I}U_{i})
where
U_{i}
are derived affine
R
-schemes. A map of prestacks
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
is called
0-representable
if for any map
\operatorname{Spec}A\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
the base change
\operatorname{Spec}A\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}A
is a derived 0-Artin stack.

(2)

A stack
\mathcal{X}
is called a
derived
R
-scheme
if it is obtained as a quotient

X_{1}\rightrightarrows X_{0}

by a correspondence where
X_{i}
are derived 0-Artin and the maps are Zariski open embeddings. We denote by
\mathrm{dSch}_{R}
the
\infty
-category of derived
R
-schemes.

(3)

A stack
\mathcal{X}
over
R
is called a
derived algebraic space
if the diagonal
\Delta_{\mathcal{X}}\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
is affine, there is an étale 0-representable cover
U\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{X}
where
U
is 0-Artin, and
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}
is a classical algebraic space
8
8

8

If
\mathcal{X}
is classical, this notion will be stronger than being a quasi-separated algebraic space in the sense of
[
Stacks
, Tag 025X, Tag 02X5]
where the diagonal is required to be representable in quasi-compact schemes.
over
\pi_{0}(R)
. We denote by
\mathrm{dAlgSpc}_{R}
the
\infty
-category of derived algebraic spaces over
R
.

(4)

We will say that a morphism of derived prestacks
f\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
is
representable
if it is represantable in derived algebraic spaces: namely, for any point
\operatorname{Spec}A\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
the fiber
\operatorname{Spec}A\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}A
is a derived algebraic space over
A
. If
\mathbf{P}
is a property of a morphism of derived algebraic spaces, then we say that a map of prestacks
f\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}

is
\mathbf{P}
if it is representable and
\operatorname{Spec}A\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}A
is
\mathbf{P}
for any
\operatorname{Spec}A\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
.

(5)

A derived stack
\mathcal{X}
over
R
is called
algebraic
if the diagonal
\Delta_{\mathcal{X}}\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
is representable and there exists a smooth map
U\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{X}
from a
0
-Artin stack which is surjective. We denote by
\mathrm{dAlgStk}_{R}
the
\infty
-category of derived algebraic stacks.

(6)

A derived stack
\mathcal{X}
over
R
is called
geometric
if it is algebraic and diagonal
\Delta_{\mathcal{X}}\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
is affine. We denote by
\mathrm{dGeoStk}_{R}
the
\infty
-category of derived geometric stacks.

(7)

A derived stack
\mathcal{X}
over
R
is called
Deligne–Mumford
if it is algebraic and
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}
is a classical Deligne-Mumford stack over
\pi_{0}(R)
.

Remark 3.1.17
.

We reminder the reader of certain properties
\mathbf{P}
of morphisms of derived algebraic spaces which will appear throughout the paper. Let
f\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
be a morphism of derived algebraic spaces.

(1)

For
\mathbf{P}=\{\text{proper},\text{closed immersions}\}
, then we say that
f
is
\mathbf{P}
if the map of classical algebraic spaces
f^{\mathrm{cl}}x\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{\mathrm{cl}}
is
\mathbf{P}
in the usual sense.

(2)

For
\mathbf{P}=\{\text{\'{e}tale},\text{smooth},\text{open immersion},\text{flat}\}
, then
f
is
\mathbf{P}
if for any étale 0-representable cover
U\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{X}
(so that
U=L_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\sqcup_{i\in I}\operatorname{Spec}A_{i})
) the composite
U\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
, which is
0
-representable morphism, is
\mathbf{P}
.

By definition, we have the sequence of inclusions

\mathrm{dAlgSpc}_{R}\subset\mathrm{dGeoStk}_{R}\subset\mathrm{dAlgStk}_{R}\subset\mathrm{dStk}_{R}.

A derived algebraic stack
\mathcal{X}
is called
quasi-compact (qc)
if there is a smooth surjective map
\operatorname{Spec}A\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{X}
from a derived affine scheme. It is called
quasi-separated (qs)
if diagonal
\Delta_{\mathcal{X}}\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
is quasi-compact, meaning that the pull-back of
\Delta_{\mathcal{X}}
to any point
\operatorname{Spec}A\rightarrow\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
is a quasi-compact derived algebraic space.

We denote by

\mathrm{dAlgStk}^{\mathrm{qcqs}}_{R}\subset\mathrm{dAlgStk}_{R},

the inclusion of the
\infty
-category of qcqs derived algebraic stacks into all derived algebraic stacks; we us a similar notation for
\mathrm{dGeoStk}^{\mathrm{qcqs}}_{R}
.

Remark 3.1.18
.

By the argument in
[
GR17a
, Chapter 2, Proposition 4.4.9]
any algebraic (or, more generally, any
n
-Artin) stack is convergent.

Example 3.1.19

(Classifying stack)
.

Let for simplicity
R=k
be a field of characteristic 0.
Let
G
be a finite type affine group scheme (aka algebraic group) over
k
; note that
G
is automatically smooth. For any
A\in\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}}_{k}
the space
G(A)
has a natural structure of a group-like
\mathbb E_{1}
-monoid and we can consider its classifying space
B(G(A)):=\{*\}_{hG(A)}
. The
classifying stack
BG
is defined as étale sheafification of the presheaf
A\mapsto B(G(A))
. Furthermore,
BG
is a geometric stack: the map
\operatorname{Spec}k\rightarrow BG
(induced by
\{*\}\rightarrow B(G(A))
on
A
-points) gives a smooth cover, and the diagonal is affine.

Example 3.1.20

(Quotient stack)
.

More generally, given a derived algebraic space
X
over
k
endowed with an action of
G
we can form
the quotient stack

[X/G]
, defined as the étale sheafification of the prestack
R\mapsto X(R)_{hG(R)}
. The map
X\rightarrow[X/G]
is a smooth affine cover and one can check that
[X/G]
is a geometric stack (since in our convention
X
has affine diagonal).

3.1.21.
ANS and perfect stacks

We now recall conditions on stacks which are ultimately about their stabilizer groups. These conditions are rather standard in stacky extensions of motivic and
K
-theoretic objects; see for example
[
Hoy17
]
,
[
KR21
]
,
[
BKRS20
]
,
[
ES21
]
. We will be somewhat terse and refer the reader to
[
ES21
, Section 2.2.16]
for more details.

Definition 3.1.22
.

An affine group scheme
G
of finite type over a classical ring
R
is called
linearly reductive
if the global sections functor

\Gamma(BG,-)\colon\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(BG)\to\mathrm{D}(R)

is
t
-exact for the standard
t
-structure.

Example 3.1.23
.

The group
\mathbb{G}_{m}
is linearly reductive over any base ring
R
.
If
R=k
is a field of characteristic
0
then
G
is linearly reductive if and only if it is reductive: namely, its unipotent radical is trivial. In particular, in this case the general linear group
GL_{n}
and all finite group schemes are linearly reductive. The group
\mathbb{G}_{a}
is an example of a non-linearly reductive group.

Definition 3.1.24
.

A group scheme
G
is said to be
nice
if it fits into a short exact sequence

1\to G_{1}\to G\to G_{2}\to 1

such that
G_{2}
is finite locally free and
G_{1}
is an algebraic torus (in other words the base change to the algebraic closure
(G_{1})_{\overline{k}}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n}
for some
n
).

Remark 3.1.25
.

Nice group schemes are linearly reductive (see
[
AHR19
, Remark 2.2]
).

The following class of stacks is the most important one for our considerations.

Definition 3.1.26
.

An algebraic
derived stack
\mathcal{X}
is called
ANS
9
9

9

ANS = algebraic with nice stabilizers
if the diagonal of

\mathcal{X}
is affine and the stabilizer group of every point
\operatorname{Spec}K\to\mathcal{X}
is nice.

One of the structural results on ANS stacks that will be useful for us is the following theorem of Alper–Hall–Halpern-Leistner–Rydh (see
[
AHLHR22
, Theorem 1.9]
; also
[
KR21
, Theorem 2.12]
):

Theorem 3.1.27
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a quasi-compact ANS derived algebraic stack over a field
k
.
Then there is an affine Nisnevich cover
\{[\operatorname{Spec}A_{i}/G_{i}]\to\mathcal{X}\}_{i\in I}
, where
R_{i}
are some connective
k
-algebras and

G_{i}
are nice group
k
-schemes.

Remark 3.1.28
.

Note that by definition an ANS stack
\mathcal{X}
is a derived geometric stack. Also, by
[
BKRS20
, Lemma A.1.7]
if
f\colon\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
is representable, then
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
is ANS if
\mathcal{X}
is.

Example 3.1.29
.

Over a field of characteristic zero all derived Deligne-Mumford stacks with affine diagonal are ANS.

Example 3.1.30
.

Given a nice group scheme
G
acting on a derived algebraic space
X
, the quotient stack
[X/G]
is ANS.

Lastly, we will often impose the following sheaf-theoretic restriction on our stacks. It ensures that derived
\infty
-category of quasicoherent sheaves admits an ample supply of dualizable objects.

Definition 3.1.31

(
[
BZFN10
]
,
[
BKRS20
, Definition A.3.1]
)
.

A derived algebraic stack
\mathcal{X}
is called
perfect
if the natural functor

\mathrm{Ind}(\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X}))\rightarrow\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})

is an equivalence of categories.

Example 3.1.32
.

In characteristic zero, many quotient stacks are perfect. Indeed, by
[
BZFN10
, Corollary 3.22]
the quotient
[X/G]
of a derived scheme
X
quasi-projective over a field in characteristic 0 by
an algebraic group
G
is perfect. Also, by
[
BKRS20
, Theorem A.3.2]
a quasi-compact ANS stack is
perfect.

3.2.
Localizing invariants

In this section, we recall some preliminaries about localizing invariants that we will need in this paper, following
[
BGT13
]
and the relative version as developed in
[
HSS17
]
. Throughout, let us fix

\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{CAlg}{}(\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{perf}}),

a symmetric monoidal, idempotent complete stable
\infty
-category (whose tensor product is exact in each variable) which is
rigid
: all of its objects are dualizable. We set the
\infty
-category of
\mathcal{E}
-modules in
\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}
as:

\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}(\mathcal{E}):=\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}).

We have the forgetful functor
U_{\mathcal{E}}:\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}(\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}
.

Example 3.2.1
.

Examples of
\mathcal{E}
include
\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X})
where
\mathcal{X}
is a perfect stack in the sense of Definition
3.1.31
. The example that we will mostly care about is
\mathcal{X}=BG
where
G
is an affine algebraic group in characteristic zero.

We will use the terminology of
[
CDH+20
]
.

Definition 3.2.2
.

A pair of composable morphisms

\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{i}\mathcal{B}\xrightarrow{p}\mathcal{C}

in
\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}
is called a
Karoubi sequence
(see
[
CDH+20
, Definition A.3.5, Proposition A.3.7]
), if the composite is null,
i
is fully faithful and the quotient map (in stable categories)

\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
is an equivalence in
\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}
(in other words, equvialence after idempotent completion).

A pair of composable morphisms

\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{i}\mathcal{B}\xrightarrow{p}\mathcal{C}

in
\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}(\mathcal{E})
is said to be a
Karoubi sequence
(see
[
HSS17
, Definition 5.3]
) if
U_{\mathcal{E}}
of it is a Karoubi sequence in the above sense. Let
\mathcal{V}
be a stable
\infty
-category. An
(
\mathcal{V}
-valued) localizing invariant (of
\mathcal{E}
-linear categories)

is a functor

F:\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}(\mathcal{E})\to\mathcal{V}

which sends
0
to
0
and Karoubi sequences to fiber sequences. Such an
F
is
finitary
if it preserves filtered colimits.

To formulate the completion theorem, we will need the following structure on
K
-theory of
\mathcal{E}
-linear categories.

Construction 3.2.3
.

According to the formula of
[
HSS17
, Theorem 5.25]
, generalizing the main theorem of
[
BGT13
]
, (non-connective)
K
-theory promotes to a lax monoidal functor

K\colon\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}(\mathcal{E})\to\mathrm{Spt}.

Since
\mathcal{E}
is the unit object of
\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}(\mathcal{E})
,
K(\mathcal{E})
is canonically an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebra and the functor
K
factors uniquely through

(3.2.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{K(\mathcal{E})}(\mathrm{Spt})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}(\mathcal{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spt}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}

Therefore, if
\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}(\mathcal{E})
, then there is a canonical
K(\mathcal{E})
-module structure on
K(\mathcal{C})
. We will use this structure implicitly without further comment.

Remark 3.2.5

(A remark on localizing invariants as “
K
-modules”)
.

In fact, more is true. Let
\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}(\mathcal{E}),\mathrm{Spt})
be the

\infty
-category of finitary localizing invariants of
\mathcal{E}
-linear categories. Under Day convolution, we have a symmetric
monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}(\mathcal{E}),\mathrm{Spt})^{\otimes}
and, by

[
HSS17
, Theorem 5.25]
,
K
-theory is the unit object and therefore any localizing invariant admits a coherent action of

K
-theory as a functor out of
\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}(\mathcal{E})
. We will not need this general structure as other multiplicative
localizing invariants involved in this paper will come with preferred multiplicative maps from
K
-theory.

There are other properties of localizing invariants that will be useful for us. Let

E\colon\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}(\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\mathcal{V}

be a localizing invariant, valued in
\mathcal{V}
a stable, presentable
\infty
-category.

(1)

E
is said to be
truncating
if for any connective algebra
A
, and an
\mathcal{E}
-module stucture on
\mathrm{Perf}(A)
the map
E(\mathrm{Perf}(A))\to E(\mathrm{Perf}(\pi_{0}A))
is an equivalence;

(2)

E
is said to be
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant
if for any
\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}(\mathcal{E})
, the canonical map
10
10

10

Here, we denote
\mathcal{C}[t]:=\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb S[t])
.

E(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow E(\mathcal{C}[t])
is an equivalence and
E
factors as
F\circ E^{\prime}
where
E^{\prime}
is a
finitary
localizing invariant
11
11

11

This technical assumption appears as (C3) in
[
TdB20
]
. All known localizing invariants satisfy that.
.

We now discuss some examples which will feature prominently throughout the paper.

Example 3.2.6

(
K
-theoretic examples)
.

Here we list some examples of localizing invariants of
K
-theoretic nature that will be relevant for us.

(1)

The primary example is the nonconnective algebraic K-theory
K
;
it is neither truncating, nor
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant, and is the central character of the present paper.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{E}=\mathrm{Perf}(R)
where
R
is a base commutative ring spectrum. We define Weibel’s homotopy-invariant
K
-theory
KH
as

KH(\mathcal{C})=\operatorname*{lim}\limits_{n\in\Delta}KH(\mathcal{C}\otimes_{R}\mathrm{Perf}_{R[t]^{\otimes n}}).

As name suggests, this is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant and is the initial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant localizing invariant under
K
-theory. In fact the same construction may be performed with any localizing invariant to produce an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant approximation thereof.

(3)

Thomason’s
K(1)
-local algebraic
K
-theory,
L_{K(1)}K
is defined by applying the
K(1)
-localization functor (recall that we have picked an implicit prime here) to
K
-theory. Results of Land-Meier-Mathew-Tamme
[
LMMT24
, Corollaries 4.23 and 4.25]
shows, in particular, that
L_{K(1)}K
is both
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant and truncating whenever
\mathcal{E}=\mathrm{Perf}(R)
, where
R
is a classical commutative ring.

(4)

Let
\mathcal{E}=\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{C})
. Blanc’s semitopological
K
-theory
K^{\mathrm{st}}
and topological
K
-theory
K^{\mathrm{Top}}

[
Bla16
]
are also examples of localizing invariants constructed out of algebraic
K
-theory. A theorem of Konovalov
[
Kon21
]
proves that
K^{\mathrm{st}}
is truncating, while its
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance is established in
[
AH18
, Lemma 3.1]
. This leads to the same properties for topological
K
-theory.

Example 3.2.7

(Hochschild-theoretic examples)
.

Another class of localizing invariants that we will study are those constructed out of Hochschild homology.

(1)

Fixing a base commutative ring spectrum
R
and a small
R
-linear stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
the trace map

\mathrm{D}(R)\to\mathrm{Ind}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}})\otimes_{R}\mathrm{Ind}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathrm{D}(R)

is determined by its value on the unit
R\in\mathrm{D}(R)
. The corresponding object

HH(\mathcal{C}/R)\in\mathrm{D}(R),

is called the
Hochshild homology
of
\mathcal{C}
and determines a
\mathrm{D}(R)
-valued localizing invariants of
\mathrm{Perf}(R)
-linear categories. If
R=\mathbb{S}
, the sphere spectrum
HH(\mathcal{C}/\mathbb{S})=THH(\mathcal{C})
is often referred to as
topological Hochschild homology
. For the purposes of this paper, we are mostly interested in the case that
R
is a base discrete commutative ring. Hochschild homology is a localizing invariant, which is neither
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant
nor truncating. There is a natural
S^{1}
-action on
HH(\mathcal{C}/R)
. In fact it has a further cyclotomic structure
[
NS18
]
; but since we are not really considering the topological variant of it too seriously we will not go into it further. In section
7.2.1
, we will review another perspective of Hochschild homology in terms of loop stacks. This will be crucial in understanding completions of Hochschild homology at various ideals.

(2)

The
S^{1}
-action on
HH(\mathcal{C}/R)
gives rise to other invariants:

HC^{-}(\mathcal{C}/R)=:HH(\mathcal{C}/R)^{hS^{1}}\qquad HH(\mathcal{C}/R)_{hS^{1}}=:HC(\mathcal{C}/R)\qquad HH(\mathcal{C}/R)^{tS^{1}}=:HP(\mathcal{C}/R).

These invariants fit into a fibre sequence:

HC(\mathcal{C}/R)[1]\to HC^{-}(\mathcal{C}/R)\to HP(\mathcal{C}/R).

(3)

Let
k
be a field of characteristic zero. Then the
HP(\mathcal{C}/k)
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant and truncating. The latter basically follows from the former and is a theorem of Goodwillie
[
Goo85a
]
. For a modern account, we refer the reader to
[
Ram
]
. While these accounts only prove
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance on
k
-dga’s the arguments of
loc. cit.
can be used to promote the results to arbitrary
k
-linear categories.

4.
Descent for cohomology theories on derived stacks

In this section, we discuss descent for cohomology theories for algebraic stacks. We start with a cohomology theory (more specifically, a localizing invariant)
E\colon\mathrm{dAff}^{\mathrm{op}}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{Spt}
on derived affine schemes. Invoking the Knutson-Lurie Theorem
4.3.11
, which states that every qc derived algebraic space is covered by derived affine schemes, we can canonically extend
E
to
\mathrm{dAlgSpc}_{R}^{\mathrm{qc}}
using Nisnevich descent. At this point, to further extend
E
to derived algebraic stacks, we have two natural options: for a Grothendieck topology
\tau
on
\mathrm{dAff}_{R}
for which
E
is a
\tau
-sheaf (e.g. Nisnevich), one could impose descent with respect to suitably defined geometric
\tau
-coverings
or with respect to all
\tau
-surjections
. We discuss this difference in detail in Section
4.3
. In particular, we will see that the functor
\mathcal{X}\mapsto E(\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X}))
has descent with respect to Nisnevich covers when restricted to perfect stacks (Corollary
4.3.8
), while extending
E
via right Kan extension has descent with respect to all Nisnevich surjections (Theorem
4.2.5
). From this point of view, the Atiyah-Segal completion theorem bridges the gap between these two different forms of descent.

Lastly, in Section
4.4
we discuss cdh excision for localizing invariants of stacks.

4.1.
Universal descent

To formulate descent results on stacks, we will use the language of universal
E
-descent as in
[
LZ14
]
.

Definition 4.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
\infty
-category with pullbacks,
\mathcal{D}
an
\infty
-category with all limits, and suppose that we are given a functor

E\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D};

we say that a morphism
f\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
in
\mathcal{C}
is
of (resp. universal)
E
-descent
if the canonical map

E(\mathcal{Y})\rightarrow\operatorname*{lim}_{\Delta}\left(E(\mathcal{X})\rightrightarrows E(\mathcal{X}\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{X})\begin{smallmatrix}\to\\
\to\\
\to\end{smallmatrix}\dots\right)

is an equivalence (resp. remains so after every pullback of
f
in
\mathcal{C}
). We also say that
E

satisfies (resp. universal) descent
with respect to a collection of morphisms
\mathcal{E}
in
\mathcal{C}
if any
f\in\mathcal{E}
is of (resp. universal)
E
-descent.

Example 4.1.2
.

Let
\tau
be a Grothendieck topology on an
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
with pullbacks and coproducts. Let
\{X_{i}\rightarrow X\}
be collection of morphisms generating a
\tau
-sieve. Then, by definition, the map
\coprod X_{i}\rightarrow X
is of universal descent for any
\tau
-sheaf.

The following records standard stability properties of morphisms of
E
-descent
[
LZ14
, Lemma 3.1.2]
:

Lemma 4.1.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
\infty
-category with pullbacks,
\mathcal{D}
an
\infty
-category with limits, and suppose that we are given a functor

E\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}.

(1)

Every retraction is of universal
E
-descent.

(2)

If (composable) morphisms
f,g
are of universal
E
-descent, then so is the composition
f\circ g
.

(3)

If the composition
f\circ g
is of universal
E
-descent, then
f
is.

(4)

Morphisms of universal
E
-descent are stable under base change.

4.2.
Right Kan extensions of cohomology theories

In this section, we will focus on answering Question
for the extension of
E
to algebraic stacks given by right Kan extension. Most of the material here is covered and extended further in the work of Khan-Ravi
[
KR21
]
and Chowdhury
[
Cho21
]
(see Remark
4.2.7
). We will only develop some rudiments of this formalism needed for the Atiyah-Segal completion theorem.

Let
R\in\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}}
be a base animated commutative ring. For any functor
E\colon\mathrm{dAff}_{R}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{\mathcal{D}}
to a complete
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}
we can consider its right Kan extension

R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E\colon\mathrm{dPStk}_{R}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}

with respect to the embedding
\mathrm{CAlg}{}_{R}^{\mathrm{cn}}\simeq\mathrm{dAff}_{R}^{\mathrm{op}}\subset\mathrm{dPStk}_{R}^{\mathrm{op}}

(cf.
[
KR21
, Construction 12.1 and Remark 12.3]
). In most examples of our interest, the functor on affine schemes will be of the form

E\circ\mathrm{Perf}\colon\mathrm{dAff}_{R}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Spt},

where
E\colon\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{Spt}
is an
R
-linear localizing invariant. In this case we will still simply write
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E
for the corresponding right Kan extension.

Note that since
\mathrm{dPStk}_{R}
is freely generated by
\mathrm{dAff}_{R}\subset\mathrm{dPStk}_{R}
under colimits,
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E
sends colimits in
\mathrm{dPStk}_{R}
to limits in
\mathcal{C}
. Since any
\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{dPStk}_{R}
is reconstructed as

\operatorname*{colim}_{\{\operatorname{Spec}A\rightarrow\mathcal{X}\}}\operatorname{Spec}A\in\mathrm{dPStk}_{R},

we can explicitly compute
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E(\mathcal{X})
as

(4.2.1)

R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E(\mathcal{X})\simeq\operatorname*{lim}_{\operatorname{Spec}A\rightarrow\mathcal{X}}E(\operatorname{Spec}A).

The next proposition is a purely formal consequence of this observation.

Proposition 4.2.2
.

Let
\tau
be a Grothendieck topology on
\mathrm{dAff}_{R}
and let
\mathcal{D}
be a presentable
\infty
-category. Suppose that

E\colon\mathrm{dAff}_{R}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D},

is a
\tau
-sheaf. Then any
\tau
-surjection
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
in
\mathrm{PStk}_{R}
is of universal
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E
-descent.

Proof.

First, let us assume that
\mathcal{D}=\mathrm{Spc}
so that
E\colon\mathrm{dAff}_{R}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Spc}
is itself a prestack; (
4.2.1
) then states that:

R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E(\mathcal{X})\simeq\Maps_{\mathrm{PStk}_{R}}(\mathcal{X},E).

Since
\tau
-sheafification is a localization, we get that
\Maps_{\mathrm{PStk}_{R}}(\mathcal{X},E)\simeq\Maps_{\mathrm{PStk}_{R}}(L_{\tau}\mathcal{X},E)
. By definition, if
\pi
is a
\tau
-surjection, then
|\mathcal{X}_{\bullet}|\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
is an
L_{\tau}
-equivalence. We thus conclude that

\Maps_{\mathrm{PStk}_{R}}(\mathcal{X},E)\simeq\operatorname*{lim}_{[\bullet]\in\Delta}(R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E(\mathcal{X}_{\bullet})).

Since any presentable
\infty
-category is a localization of a category of presheaves valued in spaces
[
Lur17
, Proposition 5.5.1.1]
, the result follows.
∎

The previous proposition states that
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E
satisfies universal descent for
\tau
-surjections, whenever
E
is a
\tau
-sheaf on
\mathrm{dAff}_{R}
. We now will also characterize certain functors which are of the form
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E
via the following general lemma. Let us recall some terminology. Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
\infty
-category with pullbacks and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{C}
be a full subcategory, stable under pullbacks. We say that a morphism
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
in
\mathcal{C}
is
representable in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
if for all
Z\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and all morphisms
Z\rightarrow Y
, the pullback
Z\times_{Y}X
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 4.2.3
.

[
LZ14
, Proposition 4.1.1]
Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
\infty
-category with pullbacks and
\mathcal{D}
a presentable
\infty
-category. Let
\mathcal{E}\subset\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{1}}
a collection of morphisms stable under pullback and composition. Let
\iota\colon\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{C}
be a full subcategory closed under pullbacks. Assume that for any
X\in\mathcal{C}
there exists a morphism
f\colon Y\rightarrow X
such that

(1)

Y\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
,

(2)

f
is representable in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and

(3)

f
is in
\mathcal{E}
.

Consider a presheaf

\mathcal{F}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D},

such that any arrow in
\mathcal{E}
is of universal
\mathcal{F}
-descent. Then the canonical map

\mathcal{F}\rightarrow R^{\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F}|_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}})

is an equivalence.

Remark 4.2.4
.

Let us also formulate Lemma
4.2.3
as an equivalence of categories, as is done
[
LZ14
, Proposition 4.1.1]
. With the notation of Lemma
4.2.3
we can consider the full subcategories

\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{D})\subset\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{D})\qquad\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(\left(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{D})\subset\mathrm{Fun}(\left(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{D})

spanned by functors of universal
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
-descent correspondingly.
The right Kan extension functor

\iota_{\ast}\colon\mathrm{Fun}(\left(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{D})\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{D})

implements the right adjoint to restriction along
\iota
. Lemma
4.2.3
then asserts an equivalence over this functor, in other words a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(\left(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\left(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{D}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\ast}}

The next theorem characterizes
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E
as the universal extension of
E
to a presheaf on a large class of stacks which respects Nisnevich surjections. Let
\mathrm{dAlgStk}_{R}^{\mathrm{qcqs},\mathrm{sd}}\subset\mathrm{dAlgStk}_{R}^{\mathrm{qcqs}}
denote the full subcategory spanned by stacks with separated diagonal.

Theorem 4.2.5
.

Let
E
be a
k
-linear localizing invariant. Then there exists a unique functor

\widetilde{E}\colon\left(\mathrm{dAlgStk}_{R}^{\mathrm{qcqs},\mathrm{sd}}\right)^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Spt}

characterized by the following properties:

(1)

there is an equivalence of presheaves on
\mathrm{dAff}_{R}
:
E\xrightarrow{\simeq}\widetilde{E}|_{\mathrm{dAff}_{R}^{\mathrm{op}}}
,

(2)

any Nisnevich surjection is of universal
\widetilde{E}
-descent.

Proof.

The existence part is given by setting
\widetilde{E}:=R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E
and invoking Proposition
4.2.2
which asserts that
\mathrm{Nis}
-surjections are of universal descent for such a functor. We now prove the uniqueness part. Let
\widetilde{E}
be such a functor, it then admits a canonical map
\eta\colon\widetilde{E}\rightarrow R^{\mathrm{dAff}}(\widetilde{E}|_{\mathrm{dAff}})
. By part (1) of the hypothesis, we have an equivalence
(\widetilde{E}|_{\mathrm{dAff}})\simeq E
. Hence we just need to show that the map
\eta
is an equivalence. We claim that
\eta^{\prime}
is an equivalence by Lemma
4.2.3
. Indeed, set
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{C}
to be the inclusion
\mathrm{dAff_{R}^{\mathrm{qcqs}}}\subset\mathrm{dAlgStk}_{R}^{\mathrm{qcqs},\mathrm{sd}}
and
\mathcal{E}
be the collection of Nisnevich surjections. The hypotheses are then verified by the derived Deshmukh’s Theorem
B.0.1
.

∎

Remark 4.2.6
.

We expect Theorem
B.0.1
to also hold without the separated diagonal assumptions. In the classical case this has been verified in a new version of
[
Des23
]
where an improvement of the original result was obtained by Deshmukh and Hall. This uses a somewhat nontrivial ingredient — the theory of Hilbert stacks. To prove a derived version of this more general result would probably require us to delve into the theory of the derived Hilbert stack which is surely interesting. Since, in this paper, we are mostly concerned with ANS stacks which are derived geometric stacks we leave it to the interested reader to extend Theorem
4.2.5
to that generality.

Remark 4.2.7

(Chowdhury and Khan-Ravi’s generalized cohomology of stacks)
.

We sketch briefly what is done in
[
Cho21
]
and
[
KR21
]
and how it relates to the present discussion. In both work, Chowdhury and Khan-Ravi begins with a six functor formalism defined on (derived) schemes; the most basic data of such is a functor
\mathrm{D}^{\ast}\colon\operatorname{Sch}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}
. These categories “house” cohomology theories which are representable in these categories. In
[
Cho21
]
the extension to stacks is performed by taking the right Kan extension to stacks which one denotes by
\mathrm{D}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Kan}}
, while
[
KR21
]
takes a smaller “lisse” extension: for an algebraic stack
\mathcal{X}
they set
\mathrm{D}^{\ast}_{\vartriangleleft}(\mathcal{X}):=\operatorname*{lim}_{(U,u)}\mathrm{D}^{\ast}(U)
where
u\colon U\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
is a smooth surjective morphism and
U
a scheme. These two extensions, typically agree for a large class of stacks. If
\mathrm{D}^{\ast}
satisfies étale descent then this is even true for any
n
-Artin stacks; if
\mathrm{D}^{\ast}
is just a Nisnevich sheaf the best known generality is due to Deshmukh
[
Des23
, Corollary 3.12]
: namely, the two extensions for quasi-separated algebraic stacks with a separated diagonal.

The focus of
[
Cho21
]
and
[
KR21
]
is on developing a full six functor formalism for these extended theories. In particular one gets a notion of homology, Borel-Moore homology and compactly supported cohomology on stacks by taking global sections of some objects. In this paper, we are interested in extensions of cohomology theories from derived affine schemes to derived stacks and, in this particular section, we essentially perform a “decategorified” version of
[
Cho21
]
and
[
KR21
]
. One big difference is that our cohomology theories are not necessarily represented in the versions of
\mathrm{D}^{\star}
considered in these papers; for example they are not assumed to have
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance, though in all examples they are Nisnevich sheaves on affine derived schemes.

4.3.
Nisnevich covers

We now explain a relatively large class of morphisms which are of universal descent for any
E
a localizing invariant. Nisnevich covers on algebraic stacks has been defined in several places in the literature:
[
KØ12
, Section 6.1]
,
[
HR18
, Section 3]
,
[
HK19
, Section 2C]
,
[
BKRS20
, Section 2.1]
to name a few. We define it in the context of derived stacks here, via a trivial modification.

To define Nisnevich covers, we first need to define the notion of being
completely decomposed
for algebraic stacks. If
\mathcal{X}
is a derived algebraic stack, and
x\colon\operatorname{Spec}K\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
is a morphism over
k
where
K
is a field over
k
, then it factors through
\tau^{\mathrm{cl}}\mathcal{X}
. In which case we get a map
\operatorname{Spec}k\rightarrow\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}
and it makes sense to speak of its
residual gerbe

\eta_{x}\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}K
as in
[
Stacks
, Tag 06ML]
; we refer to
[
Ryd11
, Appendix B]
and
[
Stacks
, Tag 06RD]
for the existence of residual gerbes on qs algebraic stacks. Roughly speaking
\eta_{x}\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}K
is of the form
BG
where
G
is the stabilizer of the point at
x
and the morphism
\eta_{x}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}
is a closed immersion.

Definition 4.3.1
.

A morphism
f\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
is said to be
completely decomposed
if for any field point
y\colon\operatorname{Spec}K\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
, there is a
K
-point
x\colon\operatorname{Spec}K\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
over
y
such that the induced map on residual gerbes
\eta_{y}\rightarrow\eta_{x}
is an isomorphism.

Definition 4.3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{dAlgStk}^{\mathrm{qcqs}}_{R}
and let
\{p_{i}\colon\mathcal{X}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}\}_{i\in I}
be a collection of representable étale morphisms where
\mathcal{X}_{i}
is qcqs. Then we say that that the above collection is a
Nisnevich cover
if
\sqcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{X}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
is completely decomposed.

Example 4.3.3
.

If
f\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
is a morphism of derived algebraic spaces, then the completely decomposed condition just means that any field point to
\mathcal{Y}
lifts to
\mathcal{X}
.

Remark 4.3.4

(Nisnevich covers vs Nisnevich surjections)
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
be a Nisnevich cover of qcqs derived algebraic stacks. Then
f\colon\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
is a Nisnevich surjection. Indeed, it is enough to show that for any
\operatorname{Spec}A\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
, the map
\mathcal{Y}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}A\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}A
has a Nisnevich-local section. Using Knutson–Lurie Theorem
4.3.11
we can find a Nisnevich cover
\operatorname{Spec}S\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}A
; since the composite map
\operatorname{Spec}S\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}A
is also a Nisnevich cover this provides a desired Nisnevich-local section.

The converse is not true: not all Nisnevich surjections are Nisnevich coverings. Let
X\in\mathrm{dSch}_{R}
and suppose that
G
is a smooth
X
-group scheme such that any
G
-torsor over affine
\operatorname{Spec}A\rightarrow X
is Nisnevich-locally trivial. Examples include
G=\mathrm{GL}_{n},\mathrm{SL}_{n},\mathrm{Sp}_{4n}
. Then the morphism of stacks
X\rightarrow[X/G]
is a Nisnevich-effective epimorphism. However it is not, in general, a Nisnevich cover. Indeed, the map
\operatorname{Spec}k\rightarrow B\mathbb{G}_{m}
cannot be a Nisnevich cover since the morphism on the residual gerbes over the point
\operatorname{Spec}k\rightarrow B\mathbb{G}_{m}
is given by
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}k
.

One of the main features of the Nisnevich topology is the equivalence between Nisnevich descent and excision for Nisnevich squares.

Definition 4.3.5
.

A
Nisnevich square
in
\mathrm{dAlgStk}^{\mathrm{qcqs}}_{R}
is a Cartesian square of derived algebraic stacks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

where
j
is a Zariski open immersion
12
12

12

Since we are working in
\mathrm{Stk}^{\mathrm{qcqs}}
, it is implied that
j
is a qc open immersion; there are, however, plenty of non qc open of a stack.
and
p
is a representable étale morphism that induces an isomorphism away from
\mathcal{U}
.

Proposition 4.3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be an
\infty
-category with limits. Let

\mathcal{F}\colon\left(\mathrm{dAlgStk}^{\mathrm{qcqs}}_{R}\right)^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D},

be a presheaf. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

\mathcal{F}(\emptyset)\simeq\ast
and converts Nisnevich squares to cartesian squares;

(2)

any Nisnevich cover is of universal
\mathcal{F}
-descent.

Proof.

The case of classical algebraic stacks is discussed in
[
HK19
, Section 2C]
with the main content being
[
Ryd11
, Proposition 3.3]
. The derived case follows by the same argument.
∎

Proposition 4.3.7

(
[
BKRS20
, Corollary 4.1.2]
)
.

Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

be a Nisnevich square of derived algebraic stacks, where
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{V}
are perfect. Then for any localizing invariant
E
the induced square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\mathcal{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\mathcal{W})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}}

is Cartesian.

From Proposition
4.3.6
we get the following.

Corollary 4.3.8
.

For any localizing invariant
E
the restriction

E\colon\left(\mathrm{dAlgStk}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{R}\right)^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Spc},\qquad E\mapsto E(\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X}))

to the subcategory
\mathrm{dAlgStk}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{R}\subset\mathrm{dAlgStk}_{R}
of perfect stacks satisfies descent for Nisnevich covers.

Remark 4.3.9
.

Since any qc ANS stack is perfect we also get that
E
satisfies descent for Nisnevich covers when restricted to qc ANS stacks. Note that if we have a Nisnevich cover
\sqcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{X}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}
is ANS, then any of the
\mathcal{X}_{i}
is also ANS by Remark
3.1.28
.

4.3.10.
Cohomology theories on derived algebraic spaces

Zariski descent implies that any cohomology theory on qcqs derived schemes is controlled by its values on derived affine schemes. Nisnevich descent allows us to control cohomology theories on qcqs derived algebraic spaces by its values on derived affine schemes. In other words, for many purposes, defining a cohomology theory on affine schemes is as good as defining one in qcqs derived algebraic spaces. This principle is formalized by:

Theorem 4.3.11

(Knutson-Lurie)
.

Let
X
be qcqs algebraic space. Then there exists a finite collection of étale morphisms
\{\operatorname{Spec}A_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i=1,\cdots n}
constitute a Nisnevich covering of
X
.

Proof.

In the case of a classical algebraic space, this result is due to Knutson
[
Knu71
, Chapter II, Theorem 6.4]
. The derived extension of this result due to Lurie in
[
Lur18
, Example 3.7.1.5]
.
∎

As a straightforward application of Lemma
4.2.3
we note the following lemma which says that any cohomology theory on
\mathrm{dAff}_{R}
which satisfies Nisnevich descent extends uniquely to one which has descent with respect to Nisnevich covers on qcqs derived algebraic spaces. In the notation of Lemma
4.2.3
, we set
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{dAlgSpc}_{R}^{\mathrm{qcqs}}
. We set to be
\mathcal{E}=\mathrm{Nis}
, the collection of arrows of the form
\sqcup Y_{i}\rightarrow Y
, where
\{Y_{i}\rightarrow Y\}
is a Nisnevich cover. We let
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}:=\mathrm{dAff}_{R}
and
\mathcal{E}
restricts to
\mathrm{dAff}_{R}
as the collection of Nisnevich covers of derived affine schemes. If
\mathcal{D}
is any presentable
\infty
-category, we then obtain an equivalence

(4.3.12)

\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{Nis}}((\mathrm{dAlgSpc}_{R}^{\mathrm{qcqs}})^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{D})\simeq\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{Nis}}(\mathrm{dAff}_{R}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{D}),

implemented by right Kan extension.

Let
E
now be an
R
-linear localizing invariant. Let
\mathcal{X}
be a derived algebraic stack. The Atiyah-Segal completion theorem concerns the difference between
E
evaluated on
\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X})
and on the right Kan extension of
E
from derived affine schemes evaluated on
\mathcal{X}
. The next lemma says that no such ambiguity occurs for derived algebraic spaces.

Proposition 4.3.13
.

Let
E\colon\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{Spt}
be a localizing invariant. Then the functor
E\circ\mathrm{Perf}\colon(\mathrm{dAlgSpc}_{R}^{\mathrm{qcqs}})^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Spt}
is right Kan extended from
\mathrm{dAff}
. In particular, the morphism

E\circ\mathrm{Perf}\rightarrow R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E

is an equivalence.

Proof.

We need only to prove that if
\{Y_{i}\rightarrow Y\}
is a Nisnevich cover, then
E\circ\mathrm{Perf}
is of universal descent for
\sqcup Y_{i}\rightarrow Y
. This is recorded in
[
CMNN20
, Proposition A.13]
.

∎

4.4.
Cdh excision

Lastly, we discuss cdh-excision on stacks. We avoid going into the cdh topology on stacks, but rather view it as a property of a cohomology theory respecting certain naturally occurring pullback squares of derived stacks. To our knowledge, this idea was first considered in
[
HK19
, Section 6]
, and later in
[
BKRS20
, Section 2.1]
.

Definition 4.4.1
.

A
proper cdh square (or abstract blow-up square)
in
\mathrm{dAlgStk}^{\mathrm{qcqs}}
is a commutative square of derived algebraic stacks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

with the following properties:

•

the corresponding square of classical stacks is Cartesian:
(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{cl}}\simeq(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{Z})^{\mathrm{cl}}
;

•

f
is representable,
f^{\mathrm{cl}}
is finitely presented and proper,
i^{\mathrm{cl}}
is finitely presented, closed embedding whose complement is quasi-compact;

•

f
induces an isomorphism on the complements
13
13

13

We refer the reader to
[
Lur18
, Section 3.3.6]
for the discussion on the underlying topological space of a derived algebraic stack; we note that the open complement of a closed substack inherits the structure of a derived algebraic stack from
\mathcal{X}
.

f_{|\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}}\colon(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\mathcal{Z}^{\prime})\simeq(\mathcal{X}\smallsetminus\mathcal{Z})
.

A functor

E\colon\left(\mathrm{dAlgStk}_{R}^{\mathrm{qcqs}}\right)^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}

is called
cdh-excisive
if
E
converts abstract blow-up squares to pullback squares.

An important example of an abstract blow-up square is given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varnothing}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varnothing}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

Consequently, if
E
takes values in a stable
\infty
-category and converts the empty stack to
0
,
E
converts the closed immersion
\tau^{\mathrm{cl}}\mathcal{X}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}
to an equivalence. The following lemma will be useful.

Lemma 4.4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be an
\infty
-category with all limits and let

E\colon\mathrm{dAff}_{R}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}

be a functor such that for any
\operatorname{Spec}A\in\mathrm{dAff}_{R}
, the map
E(\operatorname{Spec}A)\rightarrow E(\operatorname{Spec}\pi_{0}(A))
is an equivalence. Then the map

R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow R^{\mathrm{dAff}}(\tau^{\mathrm{cl}}\mathcal{X}),

is an equivalence.

Proof.

As recalled from Remark
3.1.10
,
\tau^{\mathrm{cl}}\mathcal{X}
is obtained as the left Kan extension of the restriction of
\mathcal{X}
to classical affine schemes. We claim that the canonical map in prestacks:

\tau^{\mathrm{cl}}\mathcal{X}\simeq\operatorname*{colim}_{\mathrm{Aff}_{/\mathcal{X}}}\operatorname{Spec}A\rightarrow\operatorname*{colim}_{\mathrm{dAff}_{/\mathcal{X}}}\operatorname{Spec}\pi_{0}(A),

is an equivalence. Restricted to classical affine schemes, the map above is an equivalence. Hence it suffices to prove that the target is also left Kan extended from classical affine schemes. But now, each term is a classical affine scheme and this subcategory is closed under colimits. The result then follows from the formula for
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E
.

∎

Proposition 4.4.3
.

Let
E
be a
R
-linear, truncating localizing invariant. Then

R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E\colon(\mathrm{dAlgStk}_{R}^{\mathrm{qcqs},\mathrm{sd}})^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Spt}

converts an abstract blow-up square to a cartesian square.

Proof.

Fix an abstract blow-up square which we denote as
