{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.1091pt}{-14.98254pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.92 -20.73)} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Our goal is to prove that
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E(\square)
is cartesian. According to Theorem
B.0.1
we we may find a Nisnevich surjection
\pi\colon\operatorname{Spec}R\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
. Denote by
\square_{\pi}
the base change of
\square
along
\pi
. Forming the Čech nerve of
\square
along the map
\pi
, we have an augmented cosimplicial diagram of squares
\square\rightarrow\square_{\pi}^{\times\bullet}
. By Lemma
4.2.2
, we get that
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E(\square)\simeq\operatorname*{lim}_{\Delta}R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E(\square_{\pi}^{\times\bullet})
, hence it suffices to prove that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the square
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E(\square_{\pi}^{\times n})
is cartesian. Now, each such square is an abstract blow-up square of derived algebraic spaces. By Lemma
4.3.13
,
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E\simeq E\circ\mathrm{Perf}
and hence it suffices to prove that the latter preserves abstract blow-up squares. This is then a special case of
[
ES21
, Corollary 5.2.6]
.

∎

5.
Formulating the Atiyah-Segal completion theorem

As noted above, not all natural extensions of
E
satisfy descent with respect to
\tau
-surjections even if
E
does on derived affine schemes. Moreover,
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E
is an essentially unique extension of
E
that has satisfies this form of

\tau
-descent. In this section, we define another extension of
E
that is more intrinsic to
E
, at least when evaluated on quotient stacks
[X/G]
with a fixed group
G
. This extension is a completion of
E
at a certain ideal, called the Atiyah-Segal ideal in honor of their analogous work in topology in the context of topological
K
-theory
[
Ati61
,
AS69
]
.

5.1.
Preliminaries on completions

We begin by briefly recalling the machinery of completions of structured ring spectra as in
[
Lur18
, Chapter 7.3]
. To the authors’ knowledge, the relevance of this machinery to completion theorems for
K
-theory was first noted in
[
TdB20
]
. Let
R
be a commutative ring spectrum and
I\subset\pi_{0}(R)
an ideal. According to
[
Lur18
, Proposition 7.2.4.4]
there is a semiorthogonal decomposition

D(R)\simeq\langle D(R)^{I-\mathrm{nil}},D(R)^{I-\mathrm{loc}}\rangle.

Here,
D(R)^{I-\mathrm{nil}}
is the subcategory of
I
-nilpotent
modules: those
M
such that
M[x^{-1}]\simeq 0
for all
x\in I
and
I
-local
objects are exactly those which are right orthogonal to
I
-nilpotent objects. On the other hand, the subcategory
I
-complete modules
, denoted by
D(R)^{I-\mathrm{cpl}}
, is defined as the right orthogonal to
I
-local objects
[
Lur18
, Definition 7.3.1.1]
: in other words an
R
-module
M
is
I
-complete if and only if for all
I
-local module
N
we have that
\Maps(N,M)\simeq 0
.

Under mild hypotheses, the category of complete and nilpotent modules coincide; we formulate this equivalence. The inclusion
i_{*}:D(R)^{I-\mathrm{cpl}}\hookrightarrow D(R)
(resp.
D(R)^{I-\mathrm{nil}}\hookrightarrow D(R)
) admits a left adjoint
i^{*}\colon D(R)\rightarrow D(R)^{I-\mathrm{cpl}}

[
Lur18
, Notation 7.3.1.5]
(resp. a right adjoint
i^{!}\colon D(R)\rightarrow D(R)^{I-\mathrm{nil}}

[
Lur18
, Notation 7.2.4.6]
). The unit map

M\rightarrow i_{*}i^{*}M=:M^{\wedge}_{I}

witnesses the target as the
I
-adic completion of
M
, while the counit map

\Gamma_{I}(M):=i_{*}i^{!}M\rightarrow M

witnesses the domain as the
I
-local cohomology of
M
. We now further assume that
I
is
finitely generated
(which is the case for the ideals that appear throughout this paper), then each adjoint pair induces an equivalence
D(R)^{I-\mathrm{nil}}\simeq D(R)^{I-\mathrm{cpl}}

[
Lur18
, Proposition 7.3.1.7]
. Identifying this category as
D(R)^{I-\mathrm{cpl}}
, the inclusion

i_{*}:D(R)^{I-\mathrm{cpl}}\hookrightarrow D(R)

thus admits a left adjoint
i^{*}
and a right adjoint
i^{!}
and we denote the endofunctor
i_{*}i^{*}\colon D(R)\rightarrow D(R)
by
(-)^{\wedge}_{I}
. Furthermore, we note that the endofunctor
(-)^{\wedge}_{I}
is symmetric monoidal since
i^{*}
is a strong symmetric monoidal functor
[
Lur18
, Corollary 7.3.5.2]
.

Remark 5.1.1

(Formula for the
I
-completion)
.

Let
(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n})
be a set of generators for
I
and let
M\in D(R)
. For
r\in R
denote by
[R/r]
the cofiber
R\xrightarrow{r}R
. The module

M\otimes_{R}[R/r_{1}^{i_{1}}]\otimes_{R}\ldots\otimes_{R}[R/r_{n}^{i_{n}}]

is
I
-complete for any
n
-tuple of powers
(i_{1},\ldots i_{n})\in\mathbb N^{n}
(see e.g.
[
Lur18
, Corollary 7.3.3.3]
). The natural maps
M\rightarrow M\otimes_{R}[R/r_{1}^{i_{1}}]\otimes_{R}\ldots\otimes_{R}[R/r_{n}^{i_{n}}]

then give a map

M^{\wedge}_{I}\rightarrow\operatorname*{lim}_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})}M\otimes_{R}[R/r_{1}^{i_{1}}]\otimes_{R}\ldots\otimes_{R}[R/r_{n}^{i_{n}}],

which in fact is a quasi-isomorphism (this follows by induction on
n
from
[
Lur18
, Corollary 7.3.2.1]
, with the induction step provided by
[
Lur18
, Proposition 7.3.3.2]
).

Remark 5.1.2

(Change of rings)
.

Let
R\rightarrow R^{\prime}
be a homomorphism of commutative ring spectra. Let
M^{\prime}
be an
R^{\prime}
-module. Let
I\subset\pi_{0}(R)
be a finitely generated ideal and let
I^{\prime}\subset\pi_{0}(R^{\prime})
be the ideal generated by the image of
I
. Then by
[
Lur18
, Corollary 7.3.3.6]
the
I^{\prime}
-adic completion
(M^{\prime})^{\wedge}_{I^{\prime}}
exhibits the
I
-adic completion of
M^{\prime}
as an
R
-module. In such situation we will thus often write
(M^{\prime})^{\wedge}_{I}
instead of
(M^{\prime})^{\wedge}_{I^{\prime}}
to simplify the notation.

Remark 5.1.3

(A warning about various completions)
.

Starting with Definition
7.1.1
, we will see a proliferation of other completion functors. While these can be compared, the context differs slightly. We will mostly apply the theory
I
-adic completion to the
K
-theory spectra of various stacks over
BG
with the ideal
I
given by the augmentation ideal of
G
(as recalled in the next section). In particular, we only perform
I
-adic completions at
K(BG)
-module spectra. In contrast, completions via de Rham prestacks are performed on the geometric level of maps of
derived
(as opposed to spectral) prestacks. There is still a certain compatibility between the two notions: we refer the reader to Remark
7.1.7
, and also Lemma
7.1.17
for a comparison statement that will be useful for our main results.

5.2.
Atiyah-Segal ideal

We now set the base ring to be a field, which we will denote by
k
. Let
G
be a linearly reductive group over
k
. Let
\mathrm{Rep}_{k}^{\mathrm{fg}}(G)
denote the abelian category of finite-dimensional
G
-representations on
k
-vector spaces. We have a natural functor
14
14

14

Namely,
\mathrm{Rep}_{k}^{\mathrm{fg}}(G)
is identified with the heart
\mathrm{Perf}(BG)^{\heartsuit}
of the natural
t
-structure on
\mathrm{Perf}(BG)
.

\mathrm{Rep}_{k}^{\mathrm{fg}}(G)\rightarrow\mathrm{Perf}(BG)
, and the category
\mathrm{Perf}(BG)
splits as the direct sum

(5.2.1)

\mathrm{Perf}(BG)\simeq\bigoplus_{V_{\lambda}\in\mathrm{Irr}(G)}\mathrm{Perf}(k)\otimes V_{\lambda}

with
V_{\lambda}
given by isomorphism classes of irreducible
G
-representations. This way the ring
K_{0}(BG)
is naturally identified with the representation ring
\mathcal{R}_{k}(G)
of (virtual) finite-dimensional
G
-representations valued in
k
-vector spaces; if no confusion arises we will write
\mathrm{Rep}^{\mathrm{fg}}(G)=\mathrm{Rep}_{k}^{\mathrm{fg}}(G)
and
\mathcal{R}(G)=\mathcal{R}_{k}(G)
. The map
\operatorname{Spec}k\rightarrow BG
induces a ring homomorphism
K_{0}(BG)\rightarrow K_{0}(k)
which is naturally identified with the map
\dim\colon\mathcal{R}(G)\rightarrow\mathbb Z
sending the class
[V]\in\mathcal{R}(G)
to
\dim V\in\mathbb Z
for any finite-dimensional
G
-representation
V
.

Definition 5.2.2
.

The
Atiyah-Segal ideal
(or
AS-ideal
for short)
I_{G}\subset K_{0}(BG)
is defined as the kernel of this map. Via the isomorphism
K_{0}(BG)\simeq\mathcal{R}(G)
it is identified with the augmentation ideal
\ker(\dim)\subset\mathcal{R}(G)
.

Remark 5.2.3
.

Note that
\mathcal{R}(G)
is a finitely generated ring over
\mathbb Z
and that, consequently,
I_{G}
is a finitely generated
\mathcal{R}(G)
-ideal. Indeed, for the first assertion note that
G
fits into a short exact sequence

1\rightarrow G_{0}\rightarrow G\rightarrow G/G_{0}\rightarrow 1

with
G_{0}
being the connected component of the unit
e\in G(k)
and
G/G_{0}
finite étale. Let
\overline{k}
denote the algebraic closure of
k
and put
G_{\overline{k}}:=G\times_{k}\overline{k}
. By
[
Tit71
]
, irreducible representations of
G_{0}
are encoded by
\Gamma:=\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{k}/k)
-orbits in the character lattice
X^{*}(T_{\overline{k}})
of a maximal torus
T_{\overline{k}}\subset(G_{0})_{\overline{k}}
, and as generators of
\mathcal{R}(G_{0})
one can take the classes
[V_{\Gamma\cdot\omega_{i}}]
of irreducible representations corresponding to Galois orbits of the fundamental weights. To get from
G_{0}
to
G
one can take all direct summands of the inductions
\mathrm{Ind}_{G_{0}}^{G}(V_{\Gamma\cdot\omega_{i}})
: they form a finite set of tensor generators of
\mathrm{Rep}^{\mathrm{fg}}(G)
, and thus their classes in
\mathcal{R}(G)
generate it as a ring.

To get a sense of how the representation ring and the Atiyah-Segal ideal looks like, we offer the following examples.

Example 5.2.4
.

(1)

Let
G=T
be a split algebraic torus. Let
X^{*}(T):=\Hom_{\mathrm{gr-Sch}}(T,\mathbb G_{m})
denote the character lattice. The representation ring of
\mathcal{R}(T)
is given by the group ring
\mathbb Z[X^{*}(T)]
. In particular,
K_{0}(BT)
is naturally isomorphic to the group ring
\mathbb Z[X^{*}(T)]
. The Atiyah-Segal ideal
I_{T}\subset\mathbb Z[X^{*}(T)]
is generated by elements
[\chi]-1
for
\chi\in X^{*}(T)
.

(2)

Let
G
be a connected split reductive group. Let
T\subset G
be a maximal torus. The representation ring
\mathcal{R}(G)
is identified with the subring
\mathbb Z[X^{*}(T)]^{W}\subset\mathbb Z[X^{*}(T)]\simeq\mathcal{R}(T)
under the natural restriction map
\mathcal{R}(G)\rightarrow\mathcal{R}(T)
. Here
W
denotes the Weyl group of
G
. This way Atiyah-Siegal ideal
I_{G}\subset\mathcal{R}(G)
is described as the intersection
I_{T}\cap\mathbb Z[X^{*}(T)]^{W}\subset\mathbb Z[X^{*}(T)]^{W}
.

(3)

Let
G=GL_{n}
. This is a particular case of (2), where we can identify
W\simeq\Sigma_{n}
and
\mathbb Z[X^{*}(T)]\simeq\mathbb Z[t_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,t_{n}^{\pm 1}]
, with
\Sigma_{n}
acting by permutations of
t_{i}
. It is well-known that the
\Sigma_{n}
-invariants
\mathbb Z[t_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,t_{n}^{\pm 1}]^{\Sigma_{n}}
are given by the subring
\mathbb Z[e_{1},\ldots,e_{n-1},e_{n},e_{n}^{-1}]\subset\mathbb Z[t_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,t_{n}^{\pm 1}]
where
e_{i}\in\mathbb Z[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]
are elementary symmetric polynomials in
t_{i}
. The homomorphism
\dim\colon\mathcal{R}(GL_{n})\rightarrow\mathbb Z
is induced by the map sending
t_{i}
to 1, or, consequently,
e_{i}
to
\binom{n}{i}
. We see that the AS-ideal
I_{GL_{n}}
is generated by
(e_{i}-\binom{n}{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}
.

5.3.
The Atiyah-Segal completion and change of groups

Let
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow BG
be a derived prestack over
BG
. Let
E
be a localizing invariant; then
E(\mathcal{X}):=E(\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X}))
is naturally a module over
K(BG)
.

Definition 5.3.1
.

The
Atiyah-Segal completion
of
E(\mathcal{X})
is the
I_{G}
-completion
{E(\mathcal{X})}^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}
of
E(\mathcal{X})
as a
K(BG)
-module (in the sense of Section
5.1
).

Assume we have an embedding
H\hookrightarrow G
of reductive groups over
k
. This induces a map of classifying stacks
BH\rightarrow BG
and a functor
\mathrm{dPStk}/BH\rightarrow\mathrm{dPStk}/BG
given by post-composition.
In this case, together with the Atiyah-Segal ideal
I_{H}\subset K_{0}(BH)
we have the image
I_{G}\cdot K_{0}(BH)
of
I_{G}\subset K_{0}(BG)
under the induced map
K_{0}(BG)\rightarrow K_{0}(BH)
. It will be very useful to know that the completions of
E(\mathcal{X})
for a prestack
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow BH\rightarrow BG
with respect to these two ideals coincide.

Lemma 5.3.2
.

In the situation above, there exists
n\gg 0
such that

I_{H}^{n}\subset I_{G}\cdot K_{0}(BH)\subset I_{H}

Consequently, for any derived prestack
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow BH
and finitary localizing invariant
E
there is an equivalence of completions

E(\mathcal{X})^{\wedge}_{I_{H}}\simeq E(\mathcal{X})^{\wedge}_{I_{G}\cdot K_{0}(BH)}

as
K(BH)
-modules.

Proof.

Identifying
K_{0}(BH)\simeq\mathcal{R}(H)
and
K_{0}(BG)\simeq\mathcal{R}(G)
this follows e.g. from

[
Edidin-Graham
, Corollary 6.1]
. The second part follows since by the above the radicals of
I_{H}
and
I_{G}\cdot K_{0}(BH)
coincide.
∎

Remark 5.3.3
.

We note that by the general properties of derived completions, the underlying
K(BG)
-module of
E(\mathcal{X})^{\wedge}_{I_{G}\cdot K_{0}(BH)}
is equivalent to the Atiyah-Segal completion
E(\mathcal{X})^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}
(with
E(\mathcal{X})
considered as a
K(BG)
-module). In particular, we get an equivalence

E(\mathcal{X})^{\wedge}_{I_{H}}\simeq E(\mathcal{X})^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}

of
K(BG)
-modules.

Lemma 5.3.4
.

For any map
S=\operatorname{Spec}R\rightarrow BG
and a finitary localizing invariant
E
, the
K(BG)
-module
E(S)
is
I_{G}
-complete.

Proof.

Pick some embedding
G\hookrightarrow GL_{n}
. Since any vector bundle is Zariski-locally trivial, there is a Zariski cover
S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
such that the composition

S^{\prime}\rightarrow S\rightarrow BG\rightarrow BGL_{n}

factors through the cover
\operatorname{Spec}k\rightarrow BGL_{n}
. Let
S^{\prime}_{\bullet}
denote the Čech nerve of
S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
. The image of
I_{GL_{n}}
in
E_{0}(S^{\prime}_{\bullet})
is 0, and so by Lemma
5.3.2
it follows that image of
I_{G}^{n}
in
E_{0}(S^{\prime}_{\bullet})
is 0 for some
n
. Consequently,
E(S^{\prime}_{\bullet})
is
I_{G}
-complete. Recall that

E\colon\mathrm{dAff}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sp}
is a Nisnevich, and in particular Zariski, sheaf and so

E(S)\simeq\operatorname*{lim}_{[\bullet]\in\Delta}E(S^{\prime}_{\bullet}).

Thus
E(S)
is a limit of
I_{G}
-complete
K(BG)
-modules, and as such is
I_{G}
-complete.
∎

As a corollary, we obtain the right Kan extended theory is always complete.

Corollary 5.3.5
.

For any derived prestack
\mathcal{X}
over
BG
and finitary localizing invariant
E
, the
K(BG)
-module

R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E(\mathcal{X})
is
I_{G}
-complete.

Proof.

We have
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E(\mathcal{X})\simeq\operatorname*{lim}_{\operatorname{Spec}R\rightarrow\mathcal{X}}E(\mathrm{Perf}(R))
, and by Lemma
5.3.4
all terms in the limit are
I_{G}
-complete. As a limit of
I_{G}
-complete modules,
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E(\mathcal{X})
is
I_{G}
-complete.
∎

By Corollary
5.3.5
the target of the map
E(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E(\mathcal{X})
is
I_{G}
-complete and thus it factors through the
I_{G}
-completion:

(5.3.6)

E(\mathcal{X})^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}\rightarrow R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E(\mathcal{X}).

Definition 5.3.7

(Atiyah-Segal completion)
.

Let
E
be a
k
-linear localization invariant,
\mathcal{X}
is a derived prestack over
k
and
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow BG
be a morphism. We say that the
AS completion theorem holds
for
E,\mathcal{X},G
if the map (
5.3.6
) is an equivalence.

The rest of the paper will be dedicated in proving that, under certain conditions on
E
,
\mathcal{X}
and
G
, the AS completion theorem holds. We will also offer negative results in Section
8.3
which involves
E=K
on certain non-reduced stack.

5.4.
AS ideal in
K_{0}(BG)_{\mathbb Q}

We finish off this section with a discussion of the AS ideal in rationalized
K
-theory; this will be relevant to extend AS completion results to singular stacks. For this section, we
assume that
k
is a field of characteristic zero
. Let
G
denote a reductive group scheme over
k
. We have a short exact sequence

1\rightarrow G^{0}\rightarrow G\rightarrow\pi_{0}(G)\rightarrow 1

where
G_{0}
is the connected component of
e\in G(k)
and
\pi_{0}(G)
is the “group of connected components”; since we are in characteristic zero
\pi_{0}(G)
is étale. The fiber square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BG^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BG}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pt}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\pi_{0}(G)}

induces a map

r\colon\mathcal{R}(G)\otimes_{\mathcal{R}(\pi_{0}(G))}\mathbb Z\rightarrow\mathcal{R}(G^{0})

(where the tensor product is classical).

Lemma 5.4.1
.

The induced map

r_{\mathbb Q}\colon\mathcal{R}(G)\otimes_{\mathcal{R}(\pi_{0}(G))}\mathbb Q\rightarrow\mathcal{R}(G^{0})_{\mathbb Q}

admits a natural retraction. Moreover, the ideal generated by
r_{\mathbb Q}(I_{G}\otimes 1)
has the same radical as
I_{G^{0}}
.
If, moreover,
\pi_{0}(G)
is a constant group scheme over
k
then the left hand side naturally identifies with the (classical) invariants

\mathcal{R}(G^{0})_{\mathbb Q}^{\pi_{0}(G)}\subset\mathcal{R}(G^{0})_{\mathbb Q}.

The following Galois descent statement for
\mathrm{D}({\mathbb Q})
-valued localizing invariants will be useful.

Lemma 5.4.2
.

Let
E\colon\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{k}\rightarrow\mathrm{D}({\mathbb Q})
be a localizing invariant. Let
\ell/k
be a Galois extension with the Galois group
\Gamma_{\ell/k}
. Then

\pi_{i}(E(\mathcal{C}))\simeq\pi_{i}(E(\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathrm{Perf}({k})}\mathrm{Perf}({\ell})))^{\Gamma_{\ell/k}}.

Proof.

The pull-back
f^{*}\colon\mathrm{Perf}(k)\rightarrow\mathrm{Perf}(\ell)
has a natural right adjoint functor
f_{*}\colon\mathrm{Perf}({\ell})\rightarrow\mathrm{Perf}({k})
. They induce functors
g^{*}\colon\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathrm{Perf}({k})}\mathrm{Perf}({\ell})
and
g_{*}\colon\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathrm{Perf}({k})}\mathrm{Perf}({\ell})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. The composition
g_{*}g^{*}
is given by tensoring
-\otimes_{k}\ell
which induces multiplication by
\dim({\ell/k})
on
E(\mathcal{C})
which is an isomorphism. This splits
E(\mathcal{C})
off the Čech nerve
E(\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathrm{Perf}({k})}\mathrm{Perf}({\ell})^{\otimes\bullet})
which evaluates the
\Gamma_{\ell/k}
-fixed points of
E(\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathrm{Perf}({k})}\mathrm{Perf}({\ell}))
considered as a spectrum with
\Gamma_{\ell/k}
-action. The result for homotopy groups then follows since rationally taking
\Gamma
-invariants is
t
-exact.
∎

Example 5.4.3
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a perfect stack over
k
. Then (e.g. by
[
BZFN10
, Theorem 1.2(1)]
)
\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X})\otimes_{\mathrm{Perf}(k)}\mathrm{Perf}(\ell)\simeq\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X}_{\ell})
with
\mathcal{X}_{\ell}:=\mathcal{X}\times_{k}\ell
. Thus we get that for any
\mathrm{D}({\mathbb Q})
-valued localizing invariant
E\colon\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{k}\rightarrow\mathrm{D}({\mathbb Q})
we have

\pi_{i}(E(\mathcal{X}))\simeq\pi_{i}(E(\mathcal{X}_{\ell}))^{\Gamma_{\ell/k}}.

In particular, taking
E=K_{\mathbb Q}
and
\mathcal{X}=BG
for
G
reductive we get an isomorphism of rings

K_{0}(BG)_{\mathbb Q}\simeq K_{0}(BG_{\ell})_{\mathbb Q}^{\Gamma_{\ell/k}}.

One also gets that
I_{G,\mathbb Q}:=I_{G}\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb Q\simeq I_{G_{\ell},\mathbb Q}^{\Gamma_{\ell/k}}
.

Let
I_{G,\mathbb Q}:=I_{G}\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb Q\subset K_{0}(BG)_{\mathbb Q}
; it is clear that
I_{G,\mathbb Q}
maps inside
I_{G_{\ell},\mathbb Q}
under the map
K_{0}(BG)_{\mathbb Q}\rightarrow K_{0}(BG_{\ell})_{\mathbb Q}
. Even though it doesn’t necessarily generate it, it is true that their radicals coincide:

Corollary 5.4.4
.

For any reductive
k
-group
G
and
n\gg 0
we have

{(I_{G_{\ell},\mathbb Q})}^{n}\subset I_{G}\cdot K_{0}(BG_{\ell})_{\mathbb Q}

Proof.

This follows from Hilbert’s theorem in invariant theory.
∎

6.
Atiyah-Segal completion for
KH
and other
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant localizing invariants

In this section, we extend the results of
[
TdB20
, Theorem 1.5]
to a larger class of stacks, at least when the base field is assumed to be characteristic zero; this restriction comes from our use of resolution of singularities. As we already mentioned in the introduction, Theorems
8.2.1
and
1.2.2
have
been proved for
X
a smooth projective variety; more generally it is proved in
[
TdB20
, Corollary 1.3]
for stacks of the form
[X/G]
where
X
is a smooth, separated
k
-scheme,
G
a reductive group with a
k
-split maximal torus
T
such that the action of
T
on
X
is filtrable.

6.1.
Reminders on equivariant resolution of singularities

To proceed, we recall some results on functorial, equivariant resolution of singularities, mostly following
[
Kol07
]
.

Definition 6.1.1
.

[
Kol07
, Section 3.1]

Let
Y
be a reduced, separated finite type
k
-scheme. Then a morphism of
k
-varieties

X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}Y

is said to be a
resolution of singularities
if

(1)

\pi
is projective birational morphism,

(2)

\pi
is an isomorphism over the smooth locus of
Y
;

(3)

X
is smooth over
k
.

We say that
\pi
is a
strong resolution of singularities
if moreover the preimage of the singular locus

\pi^{-1}(\mathrm{Sing}Y)
is a simple normal crossings divisor.

The existence of a strong resolution of singularities which is “functorial enough” to be accommodate equivariance is recorded below.

Theorem 6.1.2
.

Let
k
be a field of characteristic zero and let
X
be a reduced separated scheme of finite type over
k
, endowed with the action of an algebraic group
G
.
Then there exists a
G
-equivariant map
\tilde{X}\to X
which is a strong resolution of
singularities.

Proof.

The existence of
\tilde{X}
follows from
[
Kol07
, Theorem 3.36]
(see also
[
Kol07
, Corollary 3.43]
). Now
[
Kol07
, Proposition 3.9.1]
yields that the action of
G

on
X
lifts to the action on its functorial resolution
\tilde{X}
.
∎

We can interpret Theorem
6.1.2
in terms of abstract blow-up squares of stacks as in Definition
4.4.1
as follows. If
X
is a reduced, separated scheme of finite type over
k
, equipped with a
G
-action and
Z\hookrightarrow X
is a
G
-invariant subscheme of
X
containing the singular locus of
\tilde{X}
, then there exists an abstract blow-up square

(6.1.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[W/G\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\tilde{X}/G\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[Z/G\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[X/G\right]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

The following result of the the first and third authors
[

, Corollary 5.2.6]
will play a key role for us:

Proposition 6.1.4
.

Let

be a truncating invariant and consider the square as in (

). Further assume that

is reductive. Then the square

(6.1.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\left(\left[X/G\right]\right)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\left(\left[Z/G\right]\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\left(\left[W/G\right]\right),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

is cartesian.

Lemma 6.1.6
.

Let
E
be an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant and truncating
15
15

15

Although many homotopy invariant theories
are in practice nilinvariant and truncating, it is not expected to be true in general.

localizing invariant, let
f:U\to X
be a
T
-equivariant open immersion of smooth algebraic spaces such that

is a normal crossings divisor, where

is a split torus.
Then there exists a fiber sequence

E([Z/T])\to E([X/T])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f^{*}}}{{\to}}E([U/T])

Proof.

First of all, observe that since

is a divisor in
X
, the pushforward along the inclusion
defines a map
\mathrm{Perf}_{[Z/T]}\to\mathrm{Perf}_{[X/T]}
and induces the first map in the fiber sequence.

By definition,
Z
is a union of
n
smooth divisors
\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}

that have smooth scheme-theoretic intersections of expected codimensions.
Note that since
T
is connected,
T\cdot D_{i}=D_{i}
(it contains
D_{i}
and has as many irreducible components
D_{i}
).
We prove the desired claim using induction on

.
If

[

.
Now set

and

From base change formula for quasi-coherent sheaves (and transversality of intersections) we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E([Z_{1}/T])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E([Z_{1}/T])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E([Z/T])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E([X/T])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E([X\smallsetminus Z/T])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E([Z\smallsetminus Z_{1}/T])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E([X\smallsetminus Z_{1}/T])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E([X\smallsetminus Z/T]).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

By induction, we know that the bottom row is a fiber sequence, as well as the middle column. To
show that the middle row is a fiber sequence, it suffices to show that the left column is a fiber sequence.
As above, we will use induction on the amount

of smooth components of

. If

then

and the claim is clear. In general, using

6.1.4
for the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[Z_{3}/T]}}

we may rewrite the sequence as a pullback of sequences:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E([Z_{1}/T])}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\emptyset)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E([Z_{3}/T])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E([Z_{3}/T])}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

The top and the middle sequences are fiber sequences since
E(\emptyset)=0
. The bottom sequence is a fiber sequence
by induction.
∎

In the case of schemes, any Zariski sheaf which is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant is automatically invariant for torsors under a vector bundle. This is a bit more subtle in case of stacks.

Lemma 6.1.7
.

Let
E
be an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant and truncating localizing invariant.
Let
\mathcal{X}
be an ANS derived algebraic stack of finite type and let
p:\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{X}
be an affine bundle (i.e. a torsor over a
vector bundle).
Then the pullback map

E(\mathcal{X})\to E(\mathcal{V})

is an equivalence.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.1.27
any
\mathcal{X}
admits an affine Nisnevich cover by stacks of the form
[\operatorname{Spec}R/G]
. So it suffices to prove the claim for quotient stacks
[X/G]
where
X
is
separated of finite type and
G
is nice.
By Theorem
6.1.2
any such quotient stack
[X/G]
admits an abstract blow-up square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[W/G\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\tilde{X}/G\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[Z/G\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[X/G\right]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

with
Z
and
W
of smaller dimension and
\tilde{X}
smooth. By
[
Hoy17
, Remark 3.13, Lemma 2.17]
, the result is true for smooth quotients; Hoyois’ observation asserts that any
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant Nisnevich sheaf on
\operatorname{Sm}_{BG}
is invariant for affine bundles. So the general claim follows by induction on dimension of
X
and Proposition
6.1.4
.
∎

6.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.2.2

We are now ready to prove Theorem
1.2.2
. For the reader’s convenience we recall how the theorem is stated:

Theorem 6.2.1
.

Fix
G
a reductive group over a characteristic zero field
k
and
E
a truncating localizing invariant which is also
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant. Let
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow BG
be representable morphism where
\mathcal{X}
. Assume that
\mathcal{X}
is ANS and
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}\rightarrow BG
is finite type. Then we have an equivalence:

(6.2.2)

E(\mathcal{X})^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E(\mathcal{X}).

Proof.

To start, we note that having a representable morphism
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow BG
whose restriction to the classical locus of the domain is finite type implies that
\mathcal{X}=[X/G]
where
X
is a derived algebraic space, whose classical locus is finite type over
k
. We now prove the results in a few steps.

(Step 1)

First, we reduce to the case that
X
is classical and reduced
. Because
E
is truncating, it is nilinvariant on quotient stacks where the group is embeddable
[
ES21
, Proposition 5.1.10]
. The same comment applies for the right Kan extended theory by Lemma
4.4.3
since it is cdh excisive on algebraic stacks. Hence we may assume
X
is classical and reduced.

(Step 2)

We first reduce our claim to the case that
G=T
a
k
-split torus.

First we note that for any linear algebraic group
G
, there is an embedding
G\to\mathrm{GL}_{n}
. In particular,
we have an isomorphism of stacks
[X/G]\simeq[X\times_{G}\mathrm{GL}_{n}/\mathrm{GL}_{n}]
. Denote
X\times_{G}\mathrm{GL}_{n}
by
X^{\prime}
.

For a Borel subgroup
B\subset\mathrm{GL}_{n}
consider the induced map

p:[X^{\prime}/B]\rightarrow[X^{\prime}/GL_{n}]
, we claim that the the induction and restriction functors induce a retraction diagram

\mathrm{Perf}_{[X^{\prime}/\mathrm{GL}_{n}]}\xrightarrow{p^{*}}\mathrm{Perf}_{[X^{\prime}/B]}\xrightarrow{p_{*}}\mathrm{Perf}_{[X^{\prime}/\mathrm{GL}_{n}]}.

Indeed, by the projection formula it suffices to prove that the canonical map

\mathcal{O}_{[X^{\prime}/GL_{n}]}\rightarrow p_{*}\mathcal{O}_{[X^{\prime}/B]}

is an equivalence. It suffices to check that this map is an equivalence after base change along

f:X^{\prime}\rightarrow[X^{\prime}/G]
. To check this, consider the Cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}\times G/B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[X^{\prime}/B\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[X^{\prime}/G\right].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

By base change, we get an equivalence
f^{*}p_{*}\mathcal{O}_{[X^{\prime}/B]}\simeq p^{\prime}_{*}f^{{}^{\prime}*}\mathcal{O}_{[X^{\prime}/B]}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}\otimes_{k}p_{G/B*}\mathcal{O}_{G/B}
, where
p_{G/B}:G/B\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}k
is the canonical map. Now Kempf’s theorem
[
Jan03
, Proposition 4.6(a)]
asserts that
p_{G/B*}\mathcal{O}_{G/B}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{k}
, thus we conclude that
f^{*}p_{*}\mathcal{O}_{[X^{\prime}/B]}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}
.

We claim that this gives us a diagram of retractions:

(6.2.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E([X^{\prime}/\mathrm{GL}_{n}])^{\wedge}_{I_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E([X^{\prime}/B])^{\wedge}_{I_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E([X^{\prime}/\mathrm{GL}_{n}])^{\wedge}_{I_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E([X^{\prime}/\mathrm{GL}_{n}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E([X^{\prime}/B])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E([X^{\prime}/\mathrm{GL}_{n}]).}

That the top row is a retraction is immediate from the previous paragraph. For the bottom row: choose a faithful representation of
GL_{n}
(concretely, we may choose the space of
n\times n
-matrices
M_{n}
),
\mathcal{E}
. For each
\nu\geqslant 1
, we set
U_{\nu}
to be the open of
\mathbb{V}(\mathcal{E})^{\oplus\nu}
where the diagonal action of
GL_{n}
is free; note that the quotient stack
[U_{\nu}/GL_{n}]
is schematic and so we simply write it as
U_{\nu}/GL_{n}
. We can also consider
U_{\nu}
as a
B
-scheme with a free
B
-action and the same comment applies to the quotient stack
[U_{\nu}/B]
. We consider the diagram, functorial in
\nu
:

(6.2.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E([X^{\prime}/\mathrm{GL}_{n}]\times_{BGL_{n}}U_{\nu}/GL_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E([X^{\prime}/B]\times_{BB}U_{\nu}/B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E([X^{\prime}/\mathrm{GL}_{n}]\times_{BGL_{n}}U_{\nu}/GL_{n}).}

The composite is an equivalence from the previous paragraph, hence the limit of composites is an equivalence. But now a result of Khan and Ravi
[
KR22
, Theorem 3.6]
, coupled with
[
KR21
, Theorem 12.27]
to ensure that their “lisse-extension” coincides with our right Kan extension shows that the limit of (
6.2.4
) is the bottom row of (
6.2.3
).

Now, observe that the map
[X^{\prime}/T]\to[X^{\prime}/B]
is an affine bundle and
[X^{\prime}/B]
is ANS of finite type since it is
affine over
[X^{\prime}/GL_{n}]=[X/G]
. By Lemma
6.1.7

we have an equivalence
E([X^{\prime}/B])\simeq E([X^{\prime}/T])
and by Lemma
5.3.2
we have the horizontal equivalences in the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E([X/B])^{\wedge}_{I_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E([X/T])^{\wedge}_{I_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E([X/B])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E([X/T]).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Therefore, combining this observation with Lemma
5.3.2
we reduce to showing the claim for
G=T
a split torus.

(Step 3)

Assume that the completion theorem holds for all smooth algebraic spaces of dimension
\leqslant d
then it holds for any quasi-separated finite type algebraic space of dimension
d
.

Let
X
be a quasi-separated algebraic space of dimension
d
.
Consider the comparison map as a map on the category of étale morphisms over
X

E([-/T])^{\wedge}_{I_{T}}\rightarrow R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E([-/T]):\left(\mathrm{Et}^{BT}_{X}\right)^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Spt}.

By Nisnevich descent and Theorem
4.3.11
(this uses the quasi-separated assumption on
X
),
we may assume that
X
is separated.

By Theorem
6.1.2
a separated algebraic space
X
admits an equivariant resolution of singularities map

\tilde{X}\to X
. This yields an abstract blow-up square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\tilde{Z}/T]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\tilde{X}/T]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[Z/T]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[X/T]},}

where
Z
is the subscheme of singularities and
\tilde{Z}
is the exceptional divisor.
Using the abstract blow-up excision for
E
(
6.1.4
) and the fact that completion commutes
with finite limits we reduce to proving the completion theorem for
[\tilde{Z}/T]
,
[Z/T]
and
[\tilde{X}/T]
. The
dimensions of
Z
and
\tilde{Z}
are less than
d
and
\tilde{X}
is smooth, so in all of these cases the completion
theorem is known by assumption.

(Step 4)

Assume that the completion theorem holds for all quasi-separated, finite type algebraic spaces of
dimension
\leqslant d-1
and that it holds for all smooth projective schemes of dimension
d
, then the completion theorem holds
for any quasi-separated algebraic space of dimension
d
.

By Step 3, it suffices to show the claim for
X
a smooth algebraic space of dimension
d
. Consider the comparison map as a
map on the category of
T
-equivariant étale morphisms over
X

E([-/T])^{\wedge}_{I_{T}}\rightarrow R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E([-/T]):\left(\mathrm{Et}^{BT}_{X}\right)^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Spt}.

By Nisnevich descent and Theorem
4.3.11
, it suffices to prove the result for
X=\operatorname{Spec}A
, a smooth affine scheme with a
T
-action.

Consider a finite dimensional subspace
V\subset A
that is invariant under the action
of
T
and generates
A
as a
k
-algebra. This exists because any finite dimensional subspace of a representation is contained in a finite
dimensional representation of
T
. Therefore, we have a
T
-equivariant closed immersion

\iota:X\to\mathbf{P}(V)

The closure of the image of
\iota
is a
T
-equivariant
projective variety. Resolving its singularities Theorem
6.1.2
we obtain an equivariant embedding

X\to\overline{X}
where
\overline{X}
is a smooth projective variety. The complement of
Z=\overline{X}\smallsetminus X
is a simple normal
crossings divisor.
Using Lemma
6.1.6
we reduce the completion theorem to the case of
Z
which has smaller dimension and the
case of
\overline{X}
which is a smooth projective, finite type
k
-scheme.
Both cases are handled by our assumptions.

(Step 5)

Verify the cases for
d=0
and for
X
a smooth projective
k
-scheme.
This then follows from the main theorem of
[
TdB20
]
; in particular we use
[
TdB20
, Theroem 1.5]
and appeal to
[
BB73
,
Hes81
]
for the fact that any smooth projective
k
-scheme with a
T
-action are
T
-filtrable.

∎

Remark 6.2.5
.

As pointed out in Examples
3.2.6
and
3.2.7
, the above result holds
for
KH,L_{K(1)}K
and
HP
(for
\mathbb{Q}
-linear categories). Since our arguments here bootstrap from the main results
of
[
TdB20
]
, they equally well apply to the examples discussed in the introduction there.

Remark 6.2.6

(The ANS hypothesis)
.

Our proof reduces the completion theorem for stacks of the form
[X/G]
to
stacks of the form
[X/T]
. What allows this to happen is Lemma
5.3.2
on how
completions compare along immersions of groups as well as affine bundle invariance. The latter is known only to hold for ANS
stacks by Lemma
6.1.7
. This hypothesis will appear again when we patch together Theorem
6.2.1
with the Hochschild homology results of the next section. The authors do not have a counterexample for a more general affine bundle invariance statement and this is the only bottleneck to generalizing the AS completion theorem for
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant, localizing invariants.

7.
Atiyah-Segal completion theorem for Hochschild invariants

The goal of this section is to show that in case
E
is either
HH
,
HC^{-}
,
HC
, or
HP
the comparison map

E([X/G])^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}\rightarrow R^{\mathrm{dAff}}E([X/G])

is an equivalence if
\mathcal{X}=[X/G]
is ANS and satisfies some further (very mild) assumptions. Our results generalize those of Chen’s
[
Che20
]
, where the completion theorem is formulated
in the language of
loop stacks
and
formal completions
thereof.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a perfect geometric stack over
\mathbb Q
, then
HH(\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X}))
is naturally identified with derived functions on the loop stack
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}:=\underline{\mathcal{M}\mathrm{aps}}(\underline{S^{1}},\mathcal{X})
, defined as the mapping stack from
S^{1}
to
\mathcal{X}
. As already explained in
[
Che20
]
, there are at least two natural completions of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}
that one can look at. On the one hand, we can take completion along the map
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}
embedding
\mathcal{X}
into
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{X}
as constant loops. On the other hand, we can consider the unipotent loops
\mathcal{L}^{u}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}:=\underline{\mathcal{M}\mathrm{aps}}(B\mathbb{G}_{a},\mathcal{X})
which itself looks as a completion of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}
at a (in general different) closed substack of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}
. Functions on each of the completions produce variants of completed Hochschild homology. In case that
\mathcal{X}=[X/G]
, there is yet a third option: one can complete
HH(\mathcal{X})
along the Atiyah-Segal ideal.

For Hochschild homology, the main results of this section clarifies these various completions.

(1)

in large generality, functions the completion along constant loops always computes the right Kan extended theory; Proposition
7.2.15
.

(2)

in the case that
\mathcal{X}=[X/G]
, the Atiyah-Segal completion agrees with functions on the unipotent completion; Proposition
7.2.26
, Remark
7.2.27
.

The Atiyah-Segal completion theorem for HH then holds when the completions along unipotent and constant loop agree, as happens for example if
G
is nice (Theorem
7.3.1
) or, under some nice assumptions, if
[X/G]
is ANS (see Theorem
7.3.3
). We note (see Remark
7.3.2
) that these completions
do not agree in general
, if we do not assume that
G
is nice.

7.1.
Reminders and complements on formal completions

In this section we recall the notion of formal completion along a map of derived prestacks. In applications, the prestacks we consider will
not
necessarily be of locally of finite presentation over the base; in this context various definitions of de Rham prestack (and its relative version which by definition is the formal completion) all differ. The original version of de Rham stack (which we will call “thin de Rham stack”) was introduced by Simpson and is probably better known. However, without finite presentation assumptions, as we are forced to in this paper, it is better to work with Lurie’s
absolute de Rham stack
(
[
Lur18
, Definition 18.2.1.1]
).

Definition 7.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{dPStk}
be a derived prestack.

(1)

The
thin de Rham prestack

\mathcal{X}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\textstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}}\colon\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathbb Z}\rightarrow\mathrm{Spc}
is defined via formula

\mathcal{X}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\textstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}}(R):=\mathcal{X}(\pi_{0}(R)^{\mathrm{red}}).

This is the standard Simpson’s definition of the de Rham prestack.

The (Lurie’s)
de Rham prestack

\mathcal{X}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}\colon\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathbb Z}\rightarrow\mathrm{Spc}
(called the “absolute de Rham prestack” in
[
Lur18
, Definition 18.2.1.1]
) is defined by assigning to any
R\in\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathbb Z}
the space

\mathcal{X}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(R):=\operatorname*{colim}_{I}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{0}(R)/I),

where the colimit runs over the filtered system of ideals
I\subset\pi_{0}(R)
that are nilpotent (meaning
I^{n}=0
for some
n
). The natural maps
R\rightarrow R/I\rightarrow\pi_{0}(R)^{\mathrm{red}}
induce maps

\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{dR}}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\textstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}}.

(2)

Let
f\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
be a map of prestacks. The
thin derived formal completion
of
\mathcal{Y}
along
f
is a prestack defined as the fiber product

{\mathcal{Y}}_{f}^{\curlywedge}:=\mathcal{Y}\underset{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\textstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}}}{\times}\mathcal{X}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\textstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}}.

By construction, for
R\in\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathbb Z}
we have

{\mathcal{Y}}^{\curlywedge}_{f}(R)\simeq\mathcal{Y}(R)\underset{\mathcal{Y}(\pi_{0}(R)^{\mathrm{red}})}{\times}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{0}(R)^{\mathrm{red}}).

Similarly, we consider the (absolute)
derived formal completion
, which we define as

{\mathcal{Y}}_{f}^{\wedge}:=\mathcal{Y}\underset{\mathcal{Y}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}}{\times}\mathcal{X}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}.

By construction,
R
-points of
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\wedge}_{f}
are given by

{\mathcal{Y}}^{\wedge}_{f}(R)\simeq\operatorname*{colim}_{I\subset\pi_{0}(R)}\left(\mathcal{Y}(R)\underset{\mathcal{Y}(\pi_{0}(R)/I)}{\times}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{0}(R)/I)\right).

where
I
runs over nilpotent ideals of
\pi_{0}(R)
. We have a natural commutative diagram of maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}}^{\wedge}_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}}^{\curlywedge}_{f}}

where both upper and lower compositions are canonically equivalent to the original map
f\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
.

Remark 7.1.2
.

The formalism of the two de Rham prestacks provides a robust geometric, functor-of-points approach towards formal completions. Among other advantages, note that it makes sense to complete along morphisms and not just immersions. To the authors’ knowledge this viewpoint was first offered by Rozenblyum in his thesis
[
Roz11
, Definition 2.4.5]
.

Remark 7.1.3

(Completion of a pull-back)
.

Given a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

in
\mathrm{PStk}
such that the corresponding square of classical stacks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}^{\mathrm{cl}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{cl}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}^{\mathrm{cl}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a fiber square, there are natural equivalences

\mathcal{Z}^{\curlywedge}_{g}\simeq\mathcal{Z}\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{Y}^{\curlywedge}_{f},\qquad\mathcal{Z}^{\wedge}_{g}\simeq\mathcal{Z}\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{Y}^{\wedge}_{f}.

Similarly, given
f\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
and
g\colon\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}
we have natural equivalences

(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{W})^{\curlywedge}_{f\times g}\simeq\mathcal{Y}^{\curlywedge}_{f}\times\mathcal{W}^{\curlywedge}_{g},\qquad(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{W})^{\wedge}_{f\times g}\simeq\mathcal{Y}^{\wedge}_{f}\times\mathcal{W}^{\wedge}_{g}.

Remark 7.1.4

(Other properties of derived formal completions)
.

(1)

Since the
R
-points of prestacks
\mathcal{X}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\textstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}}
and
\mathcal{X}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}
depend only on
\pi_{0}(R)
, these prestacks are convergent for any
\mathcal{X}
. Consequently, both
\mathcal{Y}^{\curlywedge}_{f}
and
\mathcal{Y}^{\wedge}_{f}
are convergent if
\mathcal{Y}
is.

(2)

Thin formal completion
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\curlywedge}_{f}
only depends on
\mathcal{Y}
and the map

f^{\mathrm{red}}\colon\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{red}}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{\mathrm{red}}

between the corresponding reduced prestacks (meaning prestacks on reduced rings). Moreover, if
f^{\mathrm{red}}
is an embedding
16
16

16

Meaning that it
(-1)
-truncated, or in other words, for any
R\in\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{cn}}
the map of spaces
\mathcal{X}(R)\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}(R)
is fully faithfull as a map of
\infty
-groupoids (meaning in turn that it is the embedding of a subset of connected components).
(e.g. open or closed embedding), then so is the map
\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{\curlywedge}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
. In this case the data of a lifting of
y\in\mathcal{Y}(R)
to a point in
\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{\curlywedge}(R)\subset\mathcal{Y}(R)
is not a “structure” but a property: namely,
y
belongs to
\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{\curlywedge}(R)
if and only if the restriction
y|_{\operatorname{Spec}(\pi_{0}(R)^{\mathrm{red}})}
lands in
\mathcal{X}(\pi_{0}(R)^{\mathrm{red}})\subset\mathcal{Y}(\pi_{0}(R)^{\mathrm{red}})
.

(3)

Similarly, the absolute formal completion
\mathcal{Y}^{\wedge}_{f}
only depends on
\mathcal{Y}
and the corresponding map of classical stacks

f^{\mathrm{cl}}\colon\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{\mathrm{cl}}.

If
f^{\mathrm{cl}}
is an embedding, then so is the map
\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{\wedge}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
. In this case an
R
-point
y\in\mathcal{Y}(R)
lies in
\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{\wedge}(R)\subset\mathcal{Y}(R)
if for some nilpotent ideal
I\subset\pi_{0}(R)
the restriction
y|_{\operatorname{Spec}(\pi_{0}(R)/I)}
lands in
\mathcal{X}(\pi_{0}(R)/I)\subset\mathcal{Y}(\pi_{0}(R)/I)
.

(4)

If
\mathcal{X}
is a derived stack, then so is
\mathcal{X}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\textstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}}
. Indeed, to check that
\mathcal{X}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\textstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}}\colon\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{cn}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Spc}
has étale descent it is enough to check that it commutes with products and that given an étale cover
R\rightarrow R^{\prime}
the natural map

\mathcal{X}(\pi_{0}(R)^{\mathrm{red}})\simeq\mathcal{X}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\textstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}}(R)\rightarrow\operatorname*{lim}_{[\bullet]\in\Delta}\mathcal{X}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\textstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}}(R^{\prime\otimes\bullet})\simeq\mathcal{X}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\textstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}}(\pi_{0}(R^{\prime\otimes\bullet})^{\mathrm{red}})

is an equivalence.
This follows form the fact that
R\mapsto\pi_{0}(R)^{{\operatorname{red}}}
commutes with products, and that the cosimplicial object
\pi_{0}(R^{\prime\otimes\bullet})^{\mathrm{red}}
is identified with the Čech object for the map
\pi_{0}(R)^{\mathrm{red}}\rightarrow\pi_{0}(R^{\prime})^{\mathrm{red}}
.

More generally, if we have a map of derived stacks
f\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
then the thin formal completion
\mathcal{Y}^{\curlywedge}_{f}
is also a derived stack.

(5)

Let
f\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
be a map of derived stacks and assume we have a presentation

\mathcal{Y}=L_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\operatorname*{colim}_{i\in I}\mathcal{Y}_{i})

as a colimit over some diagram of derived stacks
I\rightarrow\mathrm{PStk}
. Let
\mathcal{X}_{i}:=\mathcal{Y}_{i}\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{X}
and consider
f_{i}\colon\mathcal{X}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{i}
. Then the natural map

L_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\operatorname*{colim}_{i\in I}(\mathcal{Y}_{i})^{\curlywedge}_{f_{i}})\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{\curlywedge}_{f}

is an equivalence. Indeed, we have

\mathcal{Y}^{\curlywedge}_{f}\simeq\mathcal{Y}^{\curlywedge}_{f}\times_{\mathcal{Y}}L_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\operatorname*{colim}_{i\in I}\mathcal{Y}_{i})\simeq L_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\mathcal{Y}^{\curlywedge}_{f}\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\operatorname*{colim}_{i\in I}\mathcal{Y}_{i})\simeq L_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\operatorname*{colim}_{i\in I}(\mathcal{Y}^{\curlywedge}_{f}\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{Y}_{i}))\simeq L_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\operatorname*{colim}_{i\in I}(\mathcal{Y}_{i})^{\curlywedge}_{f_{i}})

which gives the inverse identification. Here we used that
L_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}
commutes with fiber products and that colimits in prestacks are universal.

The following example illustrates the potential difference between
\mathcal{Y}^{\wedge}_{f}
and
\mathcal{Y}^{\curlywedge}_{f}
.

Example 7.1.5
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
be a closed embedding of derived affine schemes (so
\mathcal{Y}:=\operatorname{Spec}A
and
\mathcal{X}:=\operatorname{Spec}B
and
f^{*}\colon A\rightarrow B
is surjective on
\pi_{0}
). Let
I\subset\pi_{0}(A)
be the kernel of
\pi_{0}(f^{*})\colon\pi_{0}(A)\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{0}(B)
.

Following Remark
7.1.4
, both
\mathcal{Y}^{\curlywedge}_{f}(R)
and
\mathcal{Y}^{\wedge}_{f}(R)
are embedded in
\mathcal{Y}(R)=\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{CAlg}}{}(A,R)
; more precisely,
\mathcal{Y}^{\curlywedge}_{f}(R)
is given by the subspace of maps
A\rightarrow R
that send
I\subset\pi_{0}(A)
to the nil-radical
\mathrm{Nil}(\pi_{0}(R))\subset\pi_{0}(R)
while
\mathcal{Y}^{\wedge}_{f}(R)
is the space of those maps
A\rightarrow R
for which the image of
I\subset\pi_{0}(A)
generates a
nilpotent
ideal of
R
. These subspaces are the same if we assume that
I
is finitely generated, but not necessarily otherwise.

Example 7.1.6

(Completion at finitely generated ideal)
.

Let
f
be as above and assume
I\subset\pi_{0}(A)
is a finitely generated ideal. Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in I
be a set of generators. In that case, by the argument in
[
GR14
, Proposition 6.7.4]
we have an explicit colimit presentation of the completion :

\mathcal{Y}^{\wedge}_{f}\simeq\operatorname*{colim}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\geqslant 0}\operatorname{Spec}([A/a_{1}^{i_{1}}]\otimes_{A}\ldots\otimes_{A}[A/a_{n}^{i_{n}}])\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}A,

where
[A/a]
denotes the pushout
A\otimes_{\mathbb Z[x]}\mathbb Z
, where
x
maps to
a
in
A
and 0 in
\mathbb Z
.

Remark 7.1.7

(Comparison with Section
5.1
)
.

On the level of functions there is compatibility between formal completion via de Rham prestacks and the completion discussed in Section
5.1
via semiorthogonal decomposition of categories. More precisely, we have

\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{Y}^{\wedge}_{f})\simeq\operatorname*{lim}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\geqslant 0}[A/a_{1}^{i_{1}}]\otimes_{A}\ldots\otimes_{A}[A/a_{n}^{i_{n}}],

which is naturally identified with the derived
I
-adic completion
A^{\wedge}_{I}
of
A
as a module over itself (see Remark
5.1.1
).

Example 7.1.8

(Comparison with classical completion)
.

In the case when
A
is classical and noetherian, the derived completion also agrees with the classical one: namely, there is a natural equivalence of prestacks

\mathcal{Y}^{\wedge}_{f}\simeq\operatorname*{colim}_{n}\operatorname{Spec}(A/I^{n}),

see
[
GR14
, Proposition 6.8.2]
. In particular, for the embedding
\{0\}\hookrightarrow\mathbb A^{n}_{\mathbb Z}\simeq\operatorname{Spec}(\Sym_{\mathbb Z}(\mathbb Z^{\oplus m}))
we get

(\mathbb A^{m}_{\mathbb Z})^{\wedge}_{0}\simeq\operatorname*{colim}_{n}\operatorname{Spec}(\Sym^{\leqslant n}_{\mathbb Z}(\mathbb Z^{\oplus m}))

where
\Sym_{\mathbb Z}^{\leqslant n}(\mathbb Z^{\oplus m}):=\Sym_{\mathbb Z}({\mathbb Z}^{\oplus m})/I_{0}^{n+1}
and
I_{0}
is the ideal of
\{0\}\in\mathbb A^{m}_{\mathbb Z}
.

7.1.9.
Comparing
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\wedge}_{f}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\curlywedge}_{f}

The following notion will be useful to identify
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\wedge}_{f}
with
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\curlywedge}_{f}
in some examples:

Definition 7.1.10
.

A map of classical prestacks
f\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
is called
locally of finite presentation
if for any filtered system
\{R_{\alpha}\}
of classical rings with
R\simeq\operatorname*{colim}_{\alpha}{R_{\alpha}}
the natural map

\operatorname*{colim}_{\alpha}\mathcal{X}(R_{\alpha})\rightarrow\mathcal{X}(R)\times_{\mathcal{Y}(R)}(\operatorname*{colim}_{\alpha}\mathcal{Y}(R_{\alpha}))

is an equivalence.

Remark 7.1.11
.

If
\mathcal{Y}=\operatorname{Spec}R
is affine and classical, then
f\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
is locally of finite presentation if and only if for any filtered colimit
A=\operatorname*{colim}_{\alpha}A_{\alpha}
of classical
R
-algebras one has

\mathcal{X}(A)=\operatorname*{colim}_{\alpha}\mathcal{X}(A_{\alpha}).

Moreover, a general map of classical prestacks
f\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
is locally of finite presentation if and only if all fibers
\mathcal{X}_{y}:=\operatorname{Spec}R\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}R
over all points
y\colon\operatorname{Spec}R\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
are. Indeed, assume the latter; we need to show that for any filtered colimit
R=\operatorname*{colim}_{\alpha}R_{\alpha}
the natural map

\operatorname*{colim}_{\alpha}\mathcal{X}(R_{\alpha})\rightarrow\mathcal{X}(R)\times_{\mathcal{Y}(R)}(\operatorname*{colim}_{\alpha}\mathcal{Y}(R_{\alpha}))

is an equivalence. This is enough to do on all fibers over
\operatorname*{colim}_{\alpha}\mathcal{Y}(R_{\alpha})
and then further over all points of
y_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{Y}(R_{\alpha})
for some
\alpha
. The map on the corresponding fibers is given by

\operatorname*{colim}_{\alpha\rightarrow\alpha^{\prime}}\mathcal{X}_{y_{\alpha}}(R_{\alpha^{\prime}})\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{y_{\alpha}}(R)

where we have put
\mathcal{X}_{y_{\alpha}}:=\operatorname{Spec}R_{\alpha}\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{X}
.

Remark 7.1.12

(Comparison to
[
Lur18
]
)
.

In
[
Lur18
, 17.4.1]
, a notion of locally of finite presentation is defined where one demands that natural map in Definition
7.1.10
is an equivalence for a filtered system of derived rings. We caution the reader that these two definitions are not, in general, equivalent; our notion is clearly weaker and could perhaps be called “locally of finite presentation on classical rings.” Definition
7.1.10
is the only one that will be used in this paper. Our weakening is motivated by comparison lemmas between different completions; see for example Lemma
7.1.15
where the absolute de Rham and thin completions are proved to be equivalent when the map in question has truncation which is locally of finite presentation as in Definition
7.1.10
.

The following lemma allows to control maps of finite presentation after étale sheafification.

Lemma 7.1.13
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
be a map of classical prestacks which is locally of finite presentation and assume that there exists
n\geqslant-2
such that for all classical algebras
R
the fibers of the map
\mathcal{X}(R)\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}(R)
are
n
-truncated. Then the induced map

L_{\mathrm{et}}(f)\colon L_{\mathrm{et}}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow L_{\mathrm{et}}(\mathcal{Y})

is also locally of finite presentation.

Proof.

The same argument as in
[
Lur18
, Lemma 17.4.3.2]
works here (with the simplification that we only consider classical algebras as the source category for
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
).
∎

Example 7.1.14
.

Let us also give some examples of maps locally of finite presentation:

(1)

If
f\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
is a finitely presented affine morphism (meaning that for any
R
-point of
