\mathcal{Y}
the classical affine scheme
\tau^{\mathrm{cl}}(\operatorname{Spec}A\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{X})
is given by the spectrum of a finitely presented
A
-algebra
B
) then it is locally of finite presentation in the sense of Definition
7.1.10
. Indeed, this follows from Remark
7.1.11
.

(2)

Any colimit
\mathcal{X}:=\operatorname*{colim}\mathcal{X}_{\alpha}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
of maps
\mathcal{X}_{\alpha}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
locally of finite presentation is again locally of finite presentation (this uses the fact that colimits in
\mathrm{Spc}
are universal).

(3)

Recall that any classical
n
-Artin stack
\mathcal{X}
over a classical ring
R
is obtained as the étale sheafification of an
n
-skeletal simplicial diagram
X_{\bullet}\colon\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{PStk}
where each
X_{i}
is a union of affine schemes (and the boundary maps
X_{i}\rightarrow X_{j}
are smooth). If each
X_{i}
is a disjoint union of affine schemes that are actually
finitely presented
over
R
then by
7.1.13
and above two examples we get that
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}R
is locally of finite presentation.
Note that since
X_{\bullet}
is
n
-skeletal,
\mathcal{X}=L_{\mathrm{et}}(|X_{\bullet}|)
as a functor on
\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{cl}}
takes values in
n
-truncated spaces. Thus, more generally, having a map
f\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
of classical prestacks which is representable in
n
-Artin stacks that are locally of finite presentation, it follows from Remark
7.1.11
that
f
is locally of finite presentation.

In particular, if
G
is a finitely presented smooth affine group scheme over a classical ring
R
then
BG
or, more generally,
[X/G]
for an algebraic space
X
of locally finite presentation over
R
is again of locally finite presentation over
R
.

Let us now return to the comparison of thin and absolute formal completions.

Lemma 7.1.15
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
be a map of derived prestacks such that the underlying map
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{\mathrm{cl}}
is locally of finite presentation. Then the natural map

{\mathcal{Y}}^{\wedge}_{f}\rightarrow{\mathcal{Y}}^{\curlywedge}_{f}

is an equivalence.

Proof.

Let
A\in\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathbb Z}
be an animated commutative ring. Note that one has an equivalence (of animated rings)

\pi_{0}(A)^{\mathrm{red}}\simeq\operatorname*{colim}_{I\subset\pi_{0}(A)}\pi_{0}(A)/I,

where the (homotopy) colimit runs over all nilpotent ideals of
\pi_{0}(A)
. Since
\tau^{\mathrm{cl}}(f)\colon\tau^{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\tau^{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathcal{Y})
is of finite presentation and both versions of de Rham prestack of a prestack
\mathcal{X}
only depend on
\tau^{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathcal{X})
, we get that the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{dR}}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\textstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{dR}}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\textstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}}(A)}

is a fiber square for any
A
. From this it follows that the map

{\mathcal{Y}}^{\wedge}_{f}:=\mathcal{Y}\times_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{dR}}}\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{dR}}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}\times_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\textstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}}}\mathcal{X}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\textstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{\curlywedge}}}}{{\mathrm{dR}}}}$}}}}}=:{\mathcal{Y}}^{\curlywedge}_{f}

is an equivalence.
∎

We finish with two more computations of formal completions which will be needed later.
First, let us note that Example
7.1.5
descends to a computation of the completion of quotient stacks.

Corollary 7.1.16
.

Let
G
be a classical
k
-algebraic group. Let
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
be a
G
-equivariant closed embedding of derived affine

k
-schemes such that
X^{\mathrm{cl}}\rightarrow Y^{\mathrm{cl}}
is finitely presented. Then the completion
Y^{\wedge}_{f}
admits a natural
G
-action and for
the corresponding map of quotient stacks
f/G\colon[X/G]\rightarrow[Y/G]
there is a natural equivalence

[Y/G]^{\wedge}_{f/G}\simeq[Y^{\wedge}_{f}/G].

Proof.

Note that since the map
X^{\mathrm{cl}}\rightarrow Y^{\mathrm{cl}}
is finitely presented, by Lemma
7.1.15
we have that
Y^{\wedge}_{f}\simeq Y^{\curlywedge}_{f}
. Moreover, by Lemma
7.1.13
and Example
7.1.14
(3) we have that
[X/G]^{\mathrm{cl}}\rightarrow[Y/G]^{\mathrm{cl}}
is also locally finitely presented, so
[Y/G]^{\wedge}_{f/G}\simeq[Y/G]^{\curlywedge}_{f/G}
. Thus it is enough to prove the statement for thin formal completions. Here, by
[
BKRS20
, Lemma B.1.8]
we have an equivalence

[Y/G]^{\curlywedge}_{f/G}\simeq{\operatorname*{colim}_{n}}^{\mathrm{Stk}}[X(n)/G]

for a sequence of
G
-equivariant derived schemes
X(n)
satisfying

\operatorname*{colim}_{n}(X(n)\to X(n+1)\to\cdots)\simeq Y^{\curlywedge}_{f}.

The result follows since taking quotient stacks commutes with colimits (in stacks).
∎

Lemma 7.1.17
.

Let
A\in\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{cn}}
and let
I\subset\pi_{0}(A)
be a finitely generated ideal. Let
\Spf A^{\wedge}_{I}
denote the formal completion of
\operatorname{Spec}A
along the map
\operatorname{Spec}(\pi_{0}(A)/I)\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}A
. Let
\mathcal{Y}
be a prestack with a map
\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}A
and denote by
\mathcal{Y}^{\wedge}_{I}
the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}^{\wedge}_{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spf A^{\wedge}_{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}A.}

Then there is an equivalence

\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{Y}^{\wedge}_{I})\simeq\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{Y})^{\wedge}_{I}

where the right-hand is the derived
I
-completion as an
A
-module.

Proof.

Fiber product
-\times_{\operatorname{Spec}A}\Spf A^{\wedge}_{I}
commutes with all colimits since colimits in any
\infty
-topos, in particular
\mathrm{PStk}
or
\mathrm{PStk}/\!\operatorname{Spec}A
, are universal. Thus the functor
\mathcal{Y}\mapsto\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{Y}^{\wedge}_{I})
sends colimits to limits; since derived
I
-completion preserves limits, the same is true for
\mathcal{Y}\mapsto\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{Y})^{\wedge}_{I}
. Any prestack
\mathcal{Y}
is identified with the colimit
\operatorname*{colim}_{\operatorname{Spec}B\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}}\operatorname{Spec}B
, thus by the above we can reduce to the case
\mathcal{Y}=\operatorname{Spec}B
, where we have canonical the identification

\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{Y}^{\wedge}_{I})\simeq B^{\wedge}_{I}
(see Remark
7.1.7
).
∎

Remark 7.1.18
.

Note that in the context of Lemma
7.1.17
we have an isomorphism
\mathcal{Y}^{\wedge}_{I}\simeq\mathcal{Y}^{\curlywedge}_{I}
, since in this case
\Spf A^{\wedge}_{I}\simeq\Spf A^{\curlywedge}_{I}
by Lemma
7.1.15
.

7.1.19.
Completed vector bundles

Recall that for an animated
\mathbb Q
-algebra
R
we have an equivalence
\mathrm{CAlg}{}_{R}^{\mathrm{an}}\simeq\mathrm{CAlg}{}(D(R)_{\geqslant 0})
, where
\mathrm{CAlg}{}
denotes the category of
\mathbb E_{\infty}
-algebras.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a prestack over
\mathbb Q
. Consider the free
\mathbb E_{\infty}
-algebra functor
\Sym_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}\colon\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\mathrm{CAlg}{}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X}))
; in the limit presentations

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})\simeq\operatorname*{lim}_{\operatorname{Spec}R\rightarrow\mathcal{X}}D(R)\qquad\qquad\mathrm{CAlg}{}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X}))\simeq\operatorname*{lim}_{\operatorname{Spec}R\rightarrow\mathcal{X}}\mathrm{CAlg}{}(D(R))

it is given by
\operatorname*{lim}_{\operatorname{Spec}R\rightarrow\mathcal{X}}\Sym_{R}
where
\Sym_{R}
is the free
\mathbb E_{\infty}
-algebra functor on
D(R)
. Functor
\Sym_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}
is naturally
\mathbb N
-graded:
\Sym_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}\!(\mathcal{F})\simeq\oplus_{i=0}^{\infty}\Sym_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}^{i}(M)\simeq\oplus_{i=0}^{\infty}(\underbrace{\mathcal{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}\ldots\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}\mathcal{F}}_{i})_{\Sigma_{i}}
and promotes to a functor with values in commutative algebras in the
\mathbb N
-graded category
17
17

17

With the monoidal structure given by Day’s convolution:
(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G})_{n}=\otimes_{i+j=n}\mathcal{F}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{j}
.

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})^{\mathbb N\mathrm{-gr}}:=\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbb N,\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X}))
. Since the subcategory
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})_{\geqslant 0}\subset\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})
is closed under the monoidal structure,
\Sym_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}
also restricts to a functor

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})_{\geqslant 0}\rightarrow\mathrm{CAlg}{}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})^{\mathbb N\mathrm{-gr}}_{\geqslant 0}).

Let
n\geqslant 0
be a number and also consider the category

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})^{[0,n]\mathrm{-gr}}:=\mathrm{Fun}([0,n],\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X}))

of quasi-coherent sheaves graded by numbers from
0
to
n
. There is a restriction functor

(-)^{\leqslant n}\colon\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})^{\mathbb N\mathrm{-gr}}\rightarrow\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})^{[0,n]\mathrm{-gr}}

which has a fully faithful left and right adjoint
\iota_{n}\colon\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})^{[0,n]\mathrm{-gr}}\subset\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})^{\mathbb N\mathrm{-gr}}
(with
\iota_{n}(\mathcal{F})_{i}=\mathcal{F}_{i}
if
0\leqslant i\leqslant n
and
\iota_{n}(\mathcal{F})_{i}=0
for
i>n
). In fact,
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})^{[0,n]\mathrm{-gr}}
is a localization of
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})^{\mathbb N\mathrm{-gr}}
with respect to the class of morphisms
f
such that
\mathrm{fib}(f)_{i}=0
for
i\leqslant n
. The monoidal structure on
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})^{\mathbb N\mathrm{-gr}}
preserves that class and so induces a monoidal structure on
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})^{[0,n]\mathrm{-gr}}
, such that the functors
(-)^{\leqslant n}
and
\iota_{n}
lift to a strong and lax symmetric monoidal functors correspondingly. In particular, they induce functors

\iota_{n}\colon\mathrm{CAlg}{}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})^{[0,n]\mathrm{-gr}})\leftrightarrows\mathrm{CAlg}{}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})^{\mathbb N\mathrm{-gr}})\ \!:\!(-)^{\leqslant n}

with
(-)^{\leqslant n}
being the left adjoint to
\iota_{n}
. Functor
\iota_{n}
is fully-faithful and its image is spanned by those commutative algebras in
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})^{\mathbb N\mathrm{-gr}}
whose underlying object lies in
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})^{[0,n]\mathrm{-gr}}
.

We define
\Sym^{\leqslant n}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}\colon\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\mathrm{CAlg}{}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})^{[0,n]\mathrm{-gr}})
as the composition
(-)^{\leqslant n}\circ\Sym^{\star}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}
. For a given
\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})
one has:

(7.1.20)

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{CAlg}{}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})^{\mathbb N\mathrm{-gr}})}(\Sym^{\star}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{E}),\iota_{n}(\mathcal{A}))\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{CAlg}{}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})^{[0,n]\mathrm{-gr}})}(\Sym^{\leqslant n}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}(\mathcal{E}),\mathcal{A})

for any
\mathcal{A}\in\mathrm{CAlg}{}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})^{[0,n]\mathrm{-gr}})
. The same discussion applies to
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})
replaced by
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})_{\geqslant 0}
.

Definition 7.1.21
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a prestack over
\mathbb Q
and let
\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})_{\geqslant 0}
be a connective quasi-coherent sheaf. We define the corresponding
(generalized) vector bundle
to be the relative spectrum

\mathbf{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E}):=\operatorname{Spec}_{\mathcal{X}}(\Sym_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}\!(\mathcal{E})).

For any
n\in\mathbb N
will also denote by

\mathbf{V}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\leqslant n}(\mathcal{E}):=\operatorname{Spec}_{\mathcal{X}}(\Sym^{\leqslant n}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}\!(\mathcal{E}))

the relative spectrum of the corresponding truncation. Note that
\mathbf{V}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\leqslant 0}(\mathcal{E})\simeq\operatorname{Spec}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}})\simeq\mathcal{X}
.

Example 7.1.22
.

In the case
\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X})
one can view
\mathbf{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})
as the total space of the dual perfect complex
\mathcal{E}^{\vee}\in\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X})
. In general we do not impose any dualizability assumptions on
\mathcal{E}
and thus call the corresponding vector bundles “generalized”.

Remark 7.1.23
.

By the definition of relative spectrum we have an affine map

p\colon\mathbf{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\mathcal{X}

whose fiber over a given point
(f\colon\operatorname{Spec}R\rightarrow\mathcal{X})\in\mathcal{X}(R)
is naturally identified with the derived affine scheme
\operatorname{Spec}(\Sym_{R}(f^{*}\mathcal{E})))
. Similarly, we have an affine map
\mathbf{V}^{\leqslant n}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
whose fiber over
f\colon\operatorname{Spec}R\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
is naturally identified with
\operatorname{Spec}(\Sym_{R}^{\leqslant n}(f^{*}\mathcal{E})))
. Commutative algebra maps
\Sym_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}\!(\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\Sym^{\leqslant n}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}\!(\mathcal{E})
induce closed embeddings

z_{n}\colon\mathbf{V}^{\leqslant n}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\mathbf{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})

of prestacks over
\mathcal{X}
. The map
z_{0}\colon\mathcal{X}=\mathbf{V}^{\leqslant 0}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\mathbf{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})
models the “embedding of the zero section”.

Definition 7.1.24
.

The
completed (generalized) vector bundle

\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})
is the absolute formal completion
\mathbf{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})^{\wedge}_{z_{0}}
of
\mathbf{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})
along the zero section
z_{0}\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbf{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})
.

Remark 7.1.25
.

For any
f\colon\operatorname{Spec}A\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
and
n\geqslant 0
,

\pi_{0}(\Sym_{A}^{\leqslant n}(f^{*}\mathcal{E}))\simeq\Sym_{\pi_{0}(A)}^{\leqslant n}\pi_{0}(f^{*}\mathcal{E}).

We have that the kernel
I
of the natural surjection

\pi_{0}(\Sym_{A}^{\leqslant n}(f^{*}\mathcal{E}))\twoheadrightarrow\Sym_{\pi_{0}(A)}^{\leqslant 0}\!\pi_{0}(f^{*}\mathcal{E})\simeq\pi_{0}(A)

is nilpotent (of order
n
). Consequently, given any
f\colon\operatorname{Spec}A\rightarrow\mathbf{V}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\leqslant n}(\mathcal{E})
, the preimage
f^{-1}(I)\subset A
is also a nilpotent ideal. It follows that the maps
z_{n}\colon\mathbf{V}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\leqslant n}(\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\mathbf{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})
all factor uniquely through the formal completion
\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})
.

By the above remark, taking colimit over
n
, we get a natural map

\operatorname*{colim}_{n}\mathbf{V}^{\leqslant n}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})

of prestacks over
\mathcal{X}
. Even though this map is not necessarily an isomorphism (see Remark
7.1.27
) it is if we consider the source and target as functors on bounded rings:

Proposition 7.1.26
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a convergent prestack and let
\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})_{\geqslant 0}
. Assume that for any
f\colon\operatorname{Spec}A\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
the
\pi_{0}(A)
-module
\pi_{0}(f^{*}\mathcal{E})
is finitely generated. Then the natural map

{\operatorname*{colim}_{n}}^{\mathrm{conv}}\mathbf{V}^{\leqslant n}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})

is an isomorphism, where
\operatorname*{colim}^{\mathrm{conv}}
denotes the colimit in convergent prestacks.

Proof.

See Appendix
A.1.1
.
∎

Remark 7.1.27
.

It is
not
necessarily true that

\operatorname*{colim}_{n}\mathbf{V}^{\leqslant n}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})\simeq\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E}).

For example, consider
\mathcal{X}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q
and
\mathcal{E}=k[2]\in\mathrm{D}(\mathbb Q)_{\geqslant 0}
. Then
\pi_{*}(\Sym_{\mathbb Q}(\mathbb Q[2]))\simeq\mathbb Q[x]
and
\pi_{*}(\Sym_{\mathbb Q}^{\leqslant n}(\mathbb Q[2]))\simeq\mathbb Q[x]/x^{n+1}
with
\deg x=2
. Since
\pi_{0}(\Sym_{\mathbb Q}(\mathbb Q[2]))\simeq\mathbb Q
, the zero section map is a closed embedding which is an isomorphism on
\pi_{0}
, and thus (by Example
7.1.5
)

\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})\simeq\mathbf{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E}).

The identity map
\Sym_{\mathbb Q}(\mathbb Q[2])\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}}\Sym_{\mathbb Q}(\mathbb Q[2])
gives a
\Sym_{\mathbb Q}(\mathbb Q[2])
-point of
\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})
; however it doesn’t factor through
\operatorname*{colim}_{n}\mathbf{V}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\leqslant n}(\mathcal{E})
since for any algebra map
\Sym_{\mathbb Q}(\mathbb Q[2])\rightarrow\Sym^{\leqslant n}_{\mathbb Q}(\mathbb Q[2])
the image of the generator
x\in\pi_{2}(\Sym_{\mathbb Q}(\mathbb Q[2]))
would necessarily be nilpotent, while its image under the identity map is clearly not.

7.2.
Loop prestacks and their completions

We now discuss Hochschild homology under its geometric guise of loop prestacks. We will work towards the Atiyah-Segal theorem for Hochschild homology as formulated in Theorem
7.3.1
. For this, it will be necessary to explain the relationship between various completions of the loop prestack. We note that even if our stacks are over a field
k
, for applications to
K
-theory we are still forced to consider Hochschild homology of
\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X})
as a
\mathbb Q
-linear category.

7.2.1.
Hochschild homology as functions on loops

When
\mathcal{X}
is a perfect stack, the Hochschild homology
HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q):=HH({\mathrm{Perf}}(\mathcal{X})/\mathbb Q)
relative to
\mathbb Q
admits a
geometric description as functions on another stack whose definition we will recall below.

Let
\underline{S^{1}}
denotes the constant prestack corresponding to the circle; this is an instance of Example
3.1.5
.

Definition 7.2.2
.

Let
\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{dPStk}_{\mathbb Q}
be a derived prestack.

(1)

The
free loop prestack

\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}
is defined as the mapping prestack

\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}:=\underline{\mathcal{M}\mathrm{aps}}(\underline{S^{1}},\mathcal{X}).

Since (see Example
3.1.5
) one has

\underline{S^{1}}\simeq\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\underset{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q\sqcup\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q}{\bigsqcup}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q\in\mathrm{PStk}_{\mathbb Q},

the prestack
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}
is explicitly described as the fiber product

\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}\simeq\mathcal{X}\underset{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}}{\times}\mathcal{X}

with respect to the diagonal maps
\Delta_{\mathcal{X}}\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
.

(2)

Restriction along the projection map
S^{1}\to\mathrm{pt}
induces an inclusion of
constant loops
inside all loops:

c_{\mathcal{X}}\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}.

Remark 7.2.3
.

Since sheaves are closed under limits, if
\mathcal{X}
is a derived stack, then so is
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}
. Similarly, since convergent prestacks are closed under finite limits, if
\mathcal{X}
is convergent, so is
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}
. Finally, if
\mathcal{X}
is a geometric stack, then so is
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}
(see
[
Che20
, Lemma 2.1.12]
).

Remark 7.2.4
.

If
\mathcal{X}
comes with a map to a prestack
\mathcal{S}
, we can also consider the relative version of the free loop prestack:

\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{X}\simeq\mathcal{X}\underset{\mathcal{X}\times_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{X}}{\times}\mathcal{X},

that is the mapping prestack from
S^{1}
to
\mathcal{X}
in the category of prestacks over
\mathcal{S}
.
One has a natural map
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}
whose composition with
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{S}
factors naturally through
c_{\mathcal{S}}\colon\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{S}
, and it’s not hard to see that in fact

\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{X}\simeq\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}\times_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}.

When
\mathcal{S}=\operatorname{Spec}R
for some
R\in\mathrm{CAlg}{}^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathbb Q}
we will denote
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{X}
by
\mathcal{L}_{R}\mathcal{X}
.

Recall that a derived stack
\mathcal{X}
is called perfect if it has affine diagonal and the natural functor
\mathrm{Ind}(\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X}))\rightarrow\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})
is an equivalence. As explained in
[
Che20
, Example 2.2.20]
, if
\mathcal{X}
is a perfect stack then global functions on
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}
compute Hochschild homology of
\mathcal{X}
:

Proposition 7.2.5
.

For a perfect stack
\mathcal{X}
over
\mathbb Q
we have a natural equivalence

HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)\simeq\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}).

Remark 7.2.6
.

In the case a perfect stack
\mathcal{X}
is equipped with a map to
\operatorname{Spec}R
one also has an equivalence

HH(\mathcal{X}/R):=HH(\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X})/R)\simeq\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{L}_{R}\mathcal{X}).

7.2.7.
Formal loops

Our next goal is to give a similar geometric description for the right Kan extension of Hochschild homology.
Following
[
BZN12
, Section 6]
and
[
Che20
, Section 2.1]
we recall the following definition.

Definition 7.2.8
.

Let
\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{PStk}_{\mathbb Q}
be a prestack.
The
formal loop
prestack
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}
is defined as the
absolute
derived formal completion of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}
along constant loops
c_{\mathcal{X}}\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}
(in the sense of Definition
7.1.1
).

Remark 7.2.9
.

One can instead consider the thin completion
({\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X})^{\curlywedge}_{c_{\mathcal{X}}}
. However, for a geometric stack this does not make a difference: in this case, by Lemma
A.3.7
the natural map
({\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X})^{\curlywedge}_{c_{\mathcal{X}}}\simeq\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}
is an isomorphism.

Let us discuss some basic examples of loop prestacks, as well as their completions.

Example 7.2.10
.

Let
S=\operatorname{Spec}R
be a derived affine scheme over
\mathbb Q
.

(1)

Let us denote
R\otimes R:=R\otimes_{\mathbb Q}R
. By definition, we have

\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}S:=S\underset{S\times S}{\times}S\simeq\operatorname{Spec}(R\otimes_{R\otimes R}R).

The embedding of constant loops
c_{S}\colon S\hookrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}S
corresponds to the multiplication map

R\otimes_{R\otimes R}R\rightarrow R

on the level of functions. Note that this map is an isomorphism on
\pi_{0}
; thus, via Example
7.1.5

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}S
is already complete along
c_{S}
; in other words

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}S\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}S.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow S
be a prestack over
S
; let
\mathcal{L}_{R}\mathcal{X}
be the corresponding relative loop stack. By the above, we have that the map
c_{S}^{\mathrm{cl}}\colon S^{\mathrm{cl}}\rightarrow(\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}S)^{\mathrm{cl}}
of the underlying classical stacks is an isomorphism. Following Remark
7.2.4
, we then get that the natural map of classical stacks

(\mathcal{L}_{R}\mathcal{X})^{\mathrm{cl}}\rightarrow(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X})^{\mathrm{cl}}

is an isomorphism.

Example 7.2.11

(Loop stacks on
BG
)
.

Let
G
be an algebraic group over a field
k\supset\mathbb Q
, and let
\mathcal{X}=BG:=[\operatorname{Spec}k/G]
be the corresponding classifying stack. We would like to describe the relative loop stacks
\mathcal{L}_{k}(BG)
and
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(BG)
, as well as say something about
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}(BG)
. Let
S:=\operatorname{Spec}k
.

(1)

First, we describe
\mathcal{L}_{k}(BG)
. By definition, we have

\mathcal{L}_{k}(BG):=BG\times_{BG\times_{S}BG}BG.

Let us identify
BG
with the quotient
[G/(G\times_{S}G)]
by the action
(h_{1},h_{2})\circ g=h_{1}gh_{2}^{-1}
. This gives an identification

\mathcal{L}_{k}(BG)\simeq[(G\times_{S}G)/(G\times_{S}G)],

where the action on the right is given by
(h_{1},h_{2})\circ(g_{1},g_{2})=(h_{1}g_{1}h_{2}^{-1},h_{1}g_{2}h_{2}^{-1})
. The quotient of
G\times_{k}G
by the action of
\{1\}\times G\subset G\times_{S}G
can be identified with
G
, on which the remaining action of
G\times\{1\}\subset G\times_{k}G
is given by conjugation
h\circ g=hgh^{-1}
. This gives an identification

\mathcal{L}_{k}(BG)\simeq[G/_{\!\mathrm{ad}}G]

as the quotient stack for the adjoint action of
G
on itself.

Via this identification the inclusion of constant loops
c_{\mathcal{X}}\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{X}
is given by the embedding

[e/G]\colon BG\hookrightarrow[G/G]

where
e\colon S\hookrightarrow G
is the unit element. By Corollary
7.1.16
the corresponding derived completion
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{X}
is given by the embedding

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(BG)\simeq[G^{\wedge}_{e}/G]\hookrightarrow[G/G]\simeq\mathcal{L}_{k}(BG)

of the quotient of the corresponding formal group by the conjugation of
G
.

(2)

By Example
7.2.10
(2) we also get an isomorphism of classical stacks

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}(BG))^{\mathrm{cl}}\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{L}_{k}(BG))^{\mathrm{cl}}\simeq[G/G];

the map
c_{BG}\colon BG\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}(BG)
factors through
\tau^{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}(BG))\simeq[G/G]
.
The formal loops
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}(BG)
can be described as the completion of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}(BG)
along
BG\hookrightarrow[G/G]
in its classical locus.

More generally, for
\mathcal{X}=[X/G]
the difference between
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}
is controlled by the difference between
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}(BG)
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}(BG)
:

Proposition 7.2.12
.

Let
\mathbb Q\subset k
be a field. Let
X
be a derived algebraic space endowed with an action of an algebraic group
G
, both over
k
.
Then
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}([X/G])
is identified with the fiber product

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}([X/G])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}([X/G])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}(BG)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}(BG).}

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
A.3.8
applied to
\mathcal{X}=[X/G]
and
\mathcal{Y}=BG
.
∎

7.2.13.
Exponential map

Recall that for a derived algebraic stack
\mathcal{X}
over
\mathbb Q
the suspension
\mathbb L_{\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q}[1]\in\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{X})
is connective (
[
GR17b
, Chapter 1, Proposition 7.4.2]
).
In particular, we can consider the corresponding generalized vector bundle
\mathbf{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb L_{\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q}[1])
.

Theorem 7.2.14

(
[
BZN12
, Theorem 1.23]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a derived geometric stack over
\mathbb Q
. Then there is a natural equivalence

\mathrm{exp}_{\mathcal{X}}\colon\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb L_{\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q}[1])\simeq\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}.

Proof.

See Appendix
A.3.4
.
∎

This leads to the following:

Proposition 7.2.15
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a geometric stack over
\mathbb Q
. Then there is a natural equivalence

R^{\mathrm{\mathrm{dAff}}}HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)\simeq\mathcal{O}(\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an affine scheme. We have natural equivalences

R^{\mathrm{\mathrm{dAff}}}HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)\simeq HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)\simeq\mathcal{O}({\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X})\simeq\mathcal{O}(\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X})

where second and third isomorphism are given by Proposition
7.2.5
and Example
7.2.10
correspondingly.
It thus suffices to show that the functor
\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathcal{O}(\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X})
is right Kan extended from derived affine schemes.
By Theorem
7.2.14
(and Remark
A.1.6
) we know that
\mathcal{O}(\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X})\simeq\mathcal{O}(\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb L_{\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q}[1]))
and by Lemma
A.1.5

we may compute

\mathcal{O}(\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb L_{\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q}[1]))\simeq\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}R\Gamma(\mathcal{X},\Sym^{i}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}\!(\mathbb L_{\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q}[1])).

The right-hand side now is right Kan extended from derived affine schemes by Proposition
A.2.1
.
∎

Remark 7.2.16
.

Following the construction of the isomorphism in Proposition
7.2.15
, in the case
\mathcal{X}
is a perfect stack we can identify the map

HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)\rightarrow R^{\mathrm{\mathrm{dAff}}}HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)

with the pull-back map
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X})
for the inclusion
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}
.

7.2.17.
AS-completion
HH
and unipotent loops

We will now identify the AS-completion of Hochschild homology. Let
G
be a reductive algebraic group over a field
k
. The stack
BG
is both geometric and perfect. Following Proposition
7.2.5

and Example
7.2.11
we can identify

HH(BG/k)\simeq\mathcal{O}([G/G])\simeq\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}.

The Dennis trace map
K_{0}(BG)\rightarrow HH_{0}(BG/k)
, via the identifications
\mathcal{R}(G)\simeq K_{0}(BG)
and

HH_{0}(BG)\simeq\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}
, is given by the map

\operatorname{tr}\colon\mathcal{R}(G)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(G)^{G},

sending a representation
V\in\mathrm{Rep}^{\mathrm{f.g.}}(G)
to the corresponding trace function

\operatorname{tr}_{V}\colon g\mapsto\operatorname{tr}(g|V)\in\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}
.

Let us denote by
J_{G}
the kernel of the map
e^{*}\colon\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}\rightarrow k
given by evaluation at the unit element
e\in G(k)
. Note that its composition with
\operatorname{tr}
is given by the dimension map
\dim\colon\mathcal{R}(G)\rightarrow k
; in particular,
I_{G}
maps to
J_{G}
under
\operatorname{tr}
; this is recorded in the following diagram of exact sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}(G)\cong K_{0}(BG)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HH_{0}(BG)\cong\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{tr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{dim}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{*}}

It will be useful to know that completions at either of the ideals coincide. Often, the ideals coincide on the nose:

Lemma 7.2.18
.

Let
G
be a reductive group over a field
k
of characteristic 0.

(1)

If
k
is algebraically closed the map

\operatorname{tr}\colon\mathcal{R}(G)\otimes_{\mathbb Z}k\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}

is an isomorphism.

(2)

The map
\operatorname{tr}

is also an isomorphism if
G
connected and split.

(3)

Assume that
G^{\circ}
is split and
\pi_{0}(G)
is a constant group scheme. Then
\operatorname{tr}
induces an isomorphism

\operatorname{tr}(I_{G})\cdot\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}\simeq J_{G}.

Remark 7.2.19
.

As will soon be clear from the proof, in general case the main subtlety is in the potential existence of irreducible representations
V
of
G
such that
V_{\overline{k}}
is not irreducible (as a
G_{\overline{k}}
-representation). In this case
\End(V)^{G}\not\simeq k\cdot\mathrm{Id}_{V}
and this is exactly what prevents
\operatorname{tr}
from being surjective.

Proof.

(1) By (the algebraic version of) Peter-Weyl theorem
\mathcal{O}(G)
decomposes
G
-equivariantly into the
direct sum

\mathcal{O}(G)\simeq\oplus_{V\in\mathrm{Irr}(G)}\End(V)

with
G
-action on the right given by conjugation; here
A\in\End(V)
defines a function
f_{A}(g):=\operatorname{tr}(\rho_{V}(g)A)
. In particular,
\mathrm{Id}_{V}\in\End(V)
corresponds to the function
\operatorname{tr}_{V}
(sending
g\mapsto\operatorname{tr}_{V}(\rho_{V}(g))
).
We have
\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}\simeq\oplus_{V\in\mathrm{Irr}(G)}\End(V)^{G}
and, by Schur’s lemma, we also have

\End(V)^{G}\simeq k\cdot\mathrm{Id}_{V}
, which shows that
\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}
is linearly spanned over
k
by functions of the form
\operatorname{tr}_{V}
(and thus
\operatorname{tr}
is an isomorphism).

(2) Note that it would be enough to show that
\operatorname{tr}
becomes an isomorphism after base change to
\overline{k}
. Identifying
\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}\otimes_{k}\overline{k}\simeq\mathcal{O}(G_{\overline{k}})^{G_{\overline{k}}}
and using part (1) it then remains to show that the natural map
\mathcal{R}(G)\rightarrow\mathcal{R}(G_{\overline{k}})
is an isomorphism. Indeed, for a connected split reductive group
G
in characteristic 0, all the irreducible representations are of the form
H^{0}(G/B,\mathcal{O}(\lambda))
for a fixed choice
B\subset G
of a Borel subgroup,
\lambda
running through dominant weights of the maximal torus
T\subset G
and
\mathcal{O}(\lambda)
being the corresponding
G
-equivariant line bundle over
G/B
. By base change, one has
H^{0}((G/B)_{\overline{k}},\mathcal{O}(\lambda))\simeq H^{0}(G/B,\mathcal{O}(\lambda))\otimes_{k}\overline{k}
, which shows that basis of irreducibles for
G
in
\mathcal{R}(G)
maps identically to the basis of irreducibles for
G_{\overline{k}}
.

(3) Let us first consider the case
G=\pi_{0}(G)
; so
G
here is a finite, constant group. Let
k[G]
denote the regular representation; the element
[k[G]]-|G|\in\mathcal{R}(G)
lies in
I_{G}
. Note that
\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}\simeq k^{\mathrm{Conj}(G)}
is the ring of
k
-valued functions on the set of conjugacy classes on
G
. The function
\operatorname{tr}([k[G]]-|G|)=\operatorname{tr}_{k[G]}-|G|\in\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}
is 0 on
\{e\}
but equal to
-|G|
on any other conjugacy class; in particular it generates
J_{G}
(which is the zero ideal of the subset
\{e\}\subset\mathrm{Conj}(G)\simeq\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}
). Consequently,
\operatorname{tr}(I_{G})\cdot\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}\simeq J_{G}
as desired.

In general, the projection
G\rightarrow\pi_{0}(G)
induces a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{R}(G)_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{R}(\pi_{0}(G))_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}(\pi_{0}(G))^{\pi_{0}(G)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Conj}(\pi_{0}(G)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{\pi_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Our claim amounts to saying that the pullback along
\operatorname{tr}
of
I_{G,k}
agrees with
J_{G}
. The affine scheme
\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}
splits as disjoint union of fibers over points of
\mathrm{Conj}(\pi_{0}(G))
and since
J_{\pi_{0}(G)}
maps inside
J_{G}
under
q^{*}
we see that
J_{G}
is supported on the preimage of
\{e\}\in\mathrm{Conj}(\pi_{0}(G))
. Since
I_{\pi_{0}(G),k}
maps inside
I_{G,k}
under
p^{*}
and the pullback of
I_{\pi_{0}(G),k}
under
\operatorname{tr}_{\pi_{0}}
is
J_{\pi_{0}(G)}
we get that
\operatorname{tr}^{*}I_{G,k}
is supported on the fiber over
\{e\}
as well. Thus we only need to check if
\operatorname{tr}^{*}I_{G,k}
agrees with
J_{G}
on the fiber over the conjugacy class of
e
.

There we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}(G)_{k}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}(\pi_{0}(G))}k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}(\pi_{0}(G))^{\pi_{0}(G)}}k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}(G^{\circ})_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(G^{\circ})^{G^{\circ}}}

where the vertical maps are induced by the commutative (in fact fiber) square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BG^{\circ}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BG}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{*\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\pi_{0}(G)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

upon applying
K_{0}(-)_{k}
and
HH(-)
correspondingly. The map
f\colon BG^{\circ}\rightarrow BG
is a
\pi_{0}(G)
-torsor, in particular it is finite étale. So the push-forward
f_{*}
(in terms of representations given by the induction
\mathrm{Ind}_{G_{0}}^{G}
) restricts to a functor
\mathrm{Perf}(BG^{\circ})\rightarrow\mathrm{Perf}(BG)
giving “transfer” maps
\mathcal{R}(G^{\circ})_{k}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}(G)_{k}
and
\mathcal{O}(G^{\circ})^{G^{\circ}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}
after taking
K_{0}
and
HH_{0}
respectively.

By projection formula the composition

\mathrm{Perf}(BG)\xrightarrow{f^{*}}\mathrm{Perf}(BG^{\circ})\xrightarrow{f_{*}}\mathrm{Perf}(BG)

is given by tensoring with
f_{*}f^{*}\mathcal{O}_{BG}
, which then can also be identified with
p^{*}q_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\{*\}}
via base change. In the language of representations the latter object is the group algebra
k[\pi_{0}(G)]
considered as a representation of
G
.

We claim that
f^{*}
induces an isomorphism
\mathcal{R}(G)_{k}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}(\pi_{0}(G))}k\cong\mathcal{R}(G^{\circ})_{k}^{\pi_{0}(G)}
. The endofunctor
f_{*}f^{*}
induces on
\mathcal{R}(G)_{k}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}(\pi_{0}(G))}k
multiplication on
p^{*}(k[G])\in\mathcal{R}(G)_{k}
. This is equivalent to multiplication by
|G|
in the above tensor product. Since
k
is of characteristic zero, the vector space
\mathcal{R}(G)_{k}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}(\pi_{0}(G))}k
splits off
\mathcal{R}(G^{\circ})_{k}
. We further claim that, we can identify its image in
\mathcal{R}(G)_{k}
exactly with
\pi_{0}(G)
-invariants.

First, the image of
f^{*}
lies in the subgroup of invariants
\mathcal{R}(G^{\circ})_{k}^{\pi_{0}(G)}\subset\mathcal{R}(G)_{k}
. Indeed, the action of
G
by conjugation on
G^{\circ}
induces an action of
G
on
BG^{\circ}
whose restriction to
G^{\circ}
is trivial. This action induces a non-trivial actions of
\pi_{0}(G)=G/G^{\circ}
on
K_{0}(BG)\simeq\mathcal{R}(G)
. Since the action of
G
on
BG
is trivial, given any
V\in\mathcal{R}(G)
its pull-back
f^{*}V\in\mathcal{R}(G)
is
\pi_{0}(G)
-invariant.

Next, we claim that any element in the group of invariants
\mathcal{R}(G^{\circ})_{k}^{\pi_{0}(G)}
also lies in the image of
f^{*}
: indeed,

\mathcal{R}(G^{\circ})_{k}^{\pi_{0}(G)}\simeq\left(\frac{1}{|G|}\cdot\sum_{g\in\pi_{0}(G)}g\right)\cdot\mathcal{R}(G^{\circ})_{k},

while for any
G^{\circ}
-representation
V
, one has
f^{*}f_{*}V\simeq\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{G^{\circ}}(\mathrm{Ind}_{G^{\circ}}^{G}(V))\simeq\oplus_{g\in\pi_{0}(G)}V^{g}
(where
V^{g}
denotes the pull-back of
V
via the outer automorphism of
G^{\circ}
induced by
g
). Therefore, we see that the class of
f^{*}f_{*}V
in
\mathcal{R}(G^{\circ})
is given by
\sum_{g\in\pi_{0}(G)}g\cdot[V]
, and so the claim is proved.

Applying the same argument with
K_{0}(-)_{k}
replaced with
HH(-)
we get that

\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}(\pi_{0}(G))^{\pi_{0}(G)}}k\simeq(\mathcal{O}(G^{\circ})^{G^{\circ}})^{\pi_{0}(G)}\simeq\mathcal{O}(G^{\circ})^{G}

(which is also more or less obvious to see directly).

Summarizing, we get a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}(G)_{k}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}(\pi_{0}(G))}k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}(\pi_{0}(G))^{\pi_{0}(G)}}k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}(G^{\circ})_{k}^{\pi_{0}(G)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{O}(G^{\circ})^{G^{\circ}})^{\pi_{0}(G)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wr}

Now by part (2), the low horizontal map is an isomorphism, thus so is the top horizontal one. It follows that
I_{G}\otimes k
maps isomorphically to
J_{G}\otimes k
, as we wanted.
∎

We now deduce the result for a general reductive group
G
.

Proposition 7.2.20
.

Let
G
be a reductive group over
k
. For
n\gg 0
we also have

J_{G}^{n}\subset\operatorname{tr}(I_{G})\cdot\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}\subset J_{G}.

Consequently, given a prestack
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow BG
, the AS-completion
HH(\mathcal{X})^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}
coincides with
J_{G}
-adic completion of
HH(\mathcal{X})
as a
HH(BG)
-module.

Proof.

Let as before
G^{\circ}
be the connected component of
e
and
\pi_{0}(G)
be the “group of connected components”; the latter is a finite étale group scheme. We have a short exact sequence

1\rightarrow G^{\circ}\rightarrow G\rightarrow\pi_{0}(G)\rightarrow 1.

We will split the argument in two steps. First we discuss the case when the AS-ideal generates
J_{G}
on the nose.
Now, for a general reductive
G
, there is a Galois extension
\ell/k
such that part (3) of Lemma
7.2.18
applies to the base change
G_{\ell}
(considered as a group scheme over
\ell
). To get back to
G
we can look at the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}(G_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(G_{\ell})^{G_{\ell}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{\ell}}

Note that
\mathcal{O}(G_{\ell})^{G_{\ell}}\simeq\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}\otimes_{k}\ell
and that
J_{G_{\ell}}\simeq J_{G}\otimes_{k}\ell
. Thus,
J_{G}^{n}\subset\operatorname{tr}_{k}(I_{G})\cdot\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}
if and only if
J_{G_{\ell}}^{n}\subset\operatorname{tr}_{k}(I_{G})\cdot\mathcal{O}(G_{\ell})^{G_{\ell}}
. But, by Corollary
5.4.4
we have
I_{G_{\ell}}^{n}\subset I_{G}\cdot\mathcal{R}(G_{\ell})
for
n\gg 0
and by Lemma
7.2.18
it follows that

J_{G_{\ell}}^{n}=\operatorname{tr}_{\ell}(I_{G_{\ell}}^{n})\cdot\mathcal{O}(G_{\ell})^{G_{\ell}}\subset\operatorname{tr}_{k}(I_{G})\cdot\mathcal{O}(G_{\ell})^{G_{\ell}}.

∎

We next would like to interpret the ideal
J_{G}
(as well as the
J_{G}
-adic completion) more geometrically.

Definition 7.2.21
.

Let
G
be a reductive group over
k
and denote by
G/\!\!/G:=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}
the GIT quotient (by conjugation). We have a point
[e]\in(G/\!\!/G)(k)
corresponding to
e^{*}\colon\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}\rightarrow k
and a map
G\rightarrow G/\!\!/G
corresponding to the embedding
\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}\subset\mathcal{O}(G)
. We define
the locus of unipotent elements

\mathrm{Uni}(G)\subset G
as the (a priori derived) fiber product

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Uni}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[e]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G/\!\!/G.}

Remark 7.2.22
.

The above definition diverges in general from the classical notion of “unipotent cone of
G
” (which is typically considered in the case of algebraically closed field). Namely, our
\mathrm{Uni}(G)
might in general neither be reduced nor classical. However, we will be interested in the completion of
G
along
\mathrm{Uni}(G)
; the latter construction only depends on the reduced locus
\mathrm{Uni}(G)^{\mathrm{red}}
. Moreover, since over
\overline{k}
the ring of
G
-invariants
\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}
is generated by functions of the form
\operatorname{tr}_{V}
, the
\overline{k}
-points
\mathrm{Uni}(G)(\overline{k})
are given exactly by group elements
g\in G(\overline{k})
whose characteristic polynomial in any
G
-representation
V
is the same as for the identity element
e\in G(\overline{k})
; these are exactly the unipotent elements in
G(\overline{k})
.

Example 7.2.23
.

(1)

Let
G
be abelian (and reductive). Then
\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}\simeq\mathcal{O}(G)
and the map
G\rightarrow G/\!\!/G
is the identity map, so
\mathrm{Uni}(G)
is given by the unit element
e\in G
.

(2)

Let
G
be nice. Then we claim that the map
\{e\}\rightarrow\mathrm{Uni}(G)^{\mathrm{red}}
is an isomorphism. Indeed, we have a short exact sequence

1\rightarrow G^{\circ}\rightarrow G\xrightarrow{p}\pi_{0}(G)\rightarrow 1

where
G^{\circ}
is a torus (in particular abelian). It is enough to check the statement after base change to
\overline{k}
, in particular we can assume that
\pi_{0}(G)
is constant. We have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G/\!\!/G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(G)/\!\!/\pi_{0}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Conj}(\pi_{0}(G))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\pi_{0}(G)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

from which one sees that
\mathrm{Uni}(G)=\pi_{G}^{-1}([e])\subset G
is in fact a subscheme of
p^{-1}(e)\simeq G^{\circ}
. More precisely, it is identified with the spectrum of
\mathcal{O}(G^{\circ})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}(G^{\circ})^{\pi_{0}(G)}}k
as a
\mathcal{O}(G^{\circ})
-algebra. Since the defining ideal
I_{e}\subset\mathcal{O}(G^{\circ})
of
e\in G^{\circ}
is
\pi_{0}(G)
-invariant, by Hilbert’s theorem in invariant theory it is true that, up to taking radicals,
I_{e}
generated by elements in
\mathcal{O}(G^{\circ})^{\pi_{0}(G)}
; in other words

\sqrt{I_{e}}=\sqrt{(\mathcal{O}(G^{\circ})^{\pi_{0}(G)})}.

Thus
\mathcal{O}(G^{\circ})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}(G^{\circ})^{\pi_{0}(G)}}k
is a nilpotent extension of
k
, or, in other words,
\mathrm{Uni}(G)^{\mathrm{red}}=\{e\}
.

We now will describe the AS-completion of
HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)
as functions on the completion along a certain substack of a loop stack. Below let us denote by
G_{\mathrm{uni}}^{\wedge}\subset G
the formal completion of
G
along
\mathrm{Uni}(G)
.

Construction 7.2.24

(Unipotent loops)
.

Recall the identification
\tau^{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}BG)\simeq[G/G]
(see Example
7.2.11
). Let
(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}BG)_{\mathrm{uni}}
denote the completion of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}BG
along the composition

[\mathrm{Uni}(G)/G]\hookrightarrow[G/G]\simeq\tau^{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}BG)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}BG.

More generally, for a prestack
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow BG
over
BG
let
(\mathcal{L}Q\mathcal{X})_{\mathrm{uni}}
be the fiber product

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X})_{\mathrm{uni}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}BG)_{\mathrm{uni}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}BG.}

Note that
HH(BG/\mathbb Q)
is connective. Indeed,
\mathrm{Perf}(BG)
splits into direct sum of copies of
\mathrm{Vect}(k)
indexed by irreducible representations of
G
, and so
HH(BG/\mathbb Q)\simeq\oplus_{[V_{\lambda}\in\mathrm{Irr}(G)]}HH(k/\mathbb Q)
. Thus we can consider the derived affine scheme
\operatorname{Spec}HH(BG/\mathbb Q)
. Moreover, since
HH_{0}(k/\mathbb Q)\simeq k
, we also see that
HH_{0}(BG/\mathbb Q)\simeq HH_{0}(BG/k)\simeq\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}
. We denote by
\Spf(HH(BG/\mathbb Q)^{\wedge}_{I_{G}})
the derived completion of
\operatorname{Spec}HH(BG/\mathbb Q)
with respect to the (finitely generated) Atiyah–Segal ideal
I_{G}\subset HH_{0}(BG/\mathbb Q)
.

Lemma 7.2.25
.

There is a natural fiber square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}BG)_{\mathrm{uni}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}BG}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spf(HH(BG/\mathbb Q)^{\wedge}_{I_{G}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}(HH(BG/\mathbb Q)).}

Proof.

By definition, the top arrow is the completion of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}BG
along the embedding
[\mathrm{Uni}(G)/G]\hookrightarrow[G/G]
into the classical locus. By definition of
\mathrm{Uni}(G)
(and isomorphism
HH_{0}(BG/\mathbb Q)\simeq\mathcal{O}(G)^{G}
) the latter map fits into a fiber square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathrm{Uni}(G)/G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[G/G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}(HH_{0}(BG/\mathbb Q)/J_{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}(HH_{0}(BG/\mathbb Q)).}

Thus, by Remark
7.1.3
we get that there is a fiber square for formal completions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}BG)_{\mathrm{uni}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}BG}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spf(HH(BG/\mathbb Q)^{\wedge}_{J_{G}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}(HH(BG/\mathbb Q)).}

Now, by Proposition
7.2.20
,
\Spf(HH(BG/\mathbb Q)^{\wedge}_{I_{G}})\simeq\Spf(HH(BG/\mathbb Q)^{\wedge}_{J_{G}})
, and the statement of the lemma follows.
∎

As a consequence we get the following:

Proposition 7.2.26
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a perfect stack endowed with a map
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow BG
. Then

HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}\simeq\mathcal{O}((\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X})_{\mathrm{uni}}).

Proof.

From Lemma
7.2.25
and the definition of
(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X})_{\mathrm{uni}}
we get a fiber square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X})_{\mathrm{uni}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spf(HH(BG/\mathbb Q)^{\wedge}_{I_{G}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}(HH(BG/\mathbb Q)).}

By Proposition
7.2.5
, using that
\mathcal{X}
is perfect, we have a natural identification
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X})\simeq HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)
. By Lemma
7.1.17
, we then have that
\mathcal{O}((\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X})_{\mathrm{uni}})\simeq HH(\mathcal{X})^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}
, as desired.

∎

Remark 7.2.27
.

In
[
Che20
, Proposition 2.1.25]
Chen discusses that
18
18

18

Strictly speaking, in his situation
k
is algebraically closed and
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow BG
is representable in derived schemes.
the stack
(\mathcal{L}\mathcal{X})_{\mathrm{uni}}
(defined as above) is an instance of the
unipotent loop stack
as defined by Ben-Zvi–Nadler (
[
BZN12
, Definition 6.1]
). In other words he identifies
(\mathcal{L}\mathcal{X})_{\mathrm{uni}}
with the following mapping stack

(\mathcal{L}\mathcal{X})_{\mathrm{uni}}\simeq\underline{\mathcal{M}\mathrm{aps}}(B\mathbb G_{a},\mathcal{X}).

The embedding
(\mathcal{L}\mathcal{X})_{\mathrm{uni}}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}\mathcal{X}
in these terms is induced by the natural
affinization
map
S^{1}\rightarrow B\mathbb G_{a}
(see Appendix
A.3
for a short reminder on the latter). The key computation in his argument is the identification

\underline{\mathcal{M}\mathrm{aps}}(B\mathbb G_{a},BG)\simeq[G_{\mathrm{Uni}(G)}^{\wedge}/G],

and then one deduces a result for
\mathcal{X}=[X/G]
via a “unipotent analogue” of Lemma
A.3.8
.

7.3.
AS completion theorem for
HH

Let
G
be a reductive group over a field
k
of characteristic 0 and let
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow BG
be a representable map of derived stacks; in other words
\mathcal{X}=[X/G]
where
X
is a derived algebraic space. The closed embeddings
e\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Uni}(G)\hookrightarrow G
induce maps of quotients
[e/G]\hookrightarrow[\mathrm{Uni}(G)/G]\hookrightarrow[G/G]
. Identifying
[G/G]\simeq\tau^{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}BG)
and completing
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}BG
along these closed substacks gives inclusions

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}BG\rightarrow(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}BG)_{\mathrm{uni}}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}BG

(see Example
7.2.11
(2) and Construction
7.2.24
). Pulling back along
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}BG
gives

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}\rightarrow(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X})_{\mathrm{uni}}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}

(see Proposition
7.2.12
and Construction
7.2.24
). In the case
\mathcal{X}=[X/G]
is perfect and geometric (e.g. if it is quasi-compact and ANS), we then get the natural maps

HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)\rightarrow HH^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)\rightarrow R^{\mathrm{dAff}}HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)

passing to global functions. Atiyah-Segal theorem, if true, tells that the second map is an equivalence. Even though this is
not
true in general (Remark
7.3.2
), it is true if we assume
G
to be nice.

Theorem 7.3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=[X/G]
where
X
is a quasi-compact derived algebraic space and
G
is a nice algebraic group. Then the natural map

HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}\rightarrow(R^{\mathrm{dAff}}HH)(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)

is an equivalence.

Proof.

Recall that by our convention any derived algebraic space has affine diagonal, so
\mathcal{X}
is ANS. Since
\mathcal{X}
is also quasi-compact it follows that it is perfect (see Example
3.1.32
). Thus, it is enough to show that

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X})_{\mathrm{uni}}.

Since
G
is nice, by Example
7.2.23
(2),
\mathrm{Uni}(G)^{\mathrm{red}}\simeq\{e\}
. From this it follows that the corresponding completions
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}BG\rightarrow(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}BG)_{\mathrm{uni}}
, and consequently
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}\rightarrow(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X})_{\mathrm{uni}}
, are isomorphic.
∎

Remark 7.3.2
.

If
G
is reductive but not necessarily nice, neither the map

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}\rightarrow(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X})_{\mathrm{uni}},

nor the induced map on global functions need to be isomorphisms in general.

As a concrete example, let

\mathbb G_{a}=\{\begin{pmatrix}1&*\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\}\subset SL_{2}
over
\mathbb Q

and consider
\mathcal{X}=[X/G]
for

X:=SL_{2}/\mathbb G_{a}
with the action of
G=SL_{2}
by left translations. Note that

\mathcal{X}\simeq[(SL_{2}/\mathbb G_{a})/SL_{2}]\simeq B\mathbb G_{a}.
By Example
7.2.11
we can identify

\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}\simeq[\mathbb G_{a}/\mathbb G_{a}]\simeq\mathbb G_{a}\times B\mathbb G_{a}\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\text{ and }\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}\simeq\widehat{\mathbb G_{a}}\times B\mathbb G_{a},

with
\widehat{\mathbb G_{a}}
being the completion of
\mathbb G_{a}
at
0
.

On the other hand, note that
\mathbb G_{a}\subset\mathrm{Uni}(G)
, so the composite map
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[G/G]\rightarrow G/\!\!/G
factors
though
[e]\in G/\!\!/G
. It follows that all loops in
\mathcal{X}
are unipotent:

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X})_{\mathrm{uni}}\simeq\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}\times_{G/\!\!/G}(G/\!\!/G)^{\wedge}_{[e]}\simeq\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}^{\wedge}_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}}\simeq\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}.

We get that the map
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}\rightarrow(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X})_{\mathrm{uni}}
is explicitly described by the natural embedding

\widehat{\mathbb G_{a}}\times B\mathbb G_{a}\subset{\mathbb G_{a}}\times B\mathbb G_{a}.

Passing to global functions on both sides we then also get

R^{\mathrm{dAff}}HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)\simeq\mathbb Q[[x]]\oplus\mathbb Q[[x]][-1],\quad\text{ while }\quad HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}\simeq\mathcal{O}({\mathbb G_{a}}\times B\mathbb G_{a})\simeq\mathbb Q[x]\oplus\mathbb Q[x][-1].

This illustrates that the AS-completion in general is weaker than the one given by
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}
.

For further applications to
K
-theory we will need a slight variant of Theorem
7.3.1
.

Theorem 7.3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=[X/G]\rightarrow BG
be a representable morphism from an ANS stack
\mathcal{X}
. Assume that
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}\rightarrow BG
is of finite type. Then the comparison map

HH(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}\rightarrow(R^{\mathrm{dAff}}HH)(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)

is an equivalence.

Proof.

Again, it is enough to show that the map
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}\rightarrow(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X})_{\mathrm{uni}}
is an isomorphism. Note that
({\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X})^{\mathrm{cl}}\simeq\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}})
(the inertia stack). We have a natural map
f\colon\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}})\rightarrow\mathcal{I}(BG)\simeq[G/G]
and an affine projection
p\colon\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}})\rightarrow\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}
. Note that by
[
Stacks
, Tag 04XS]

p
is locally of finite type. By construction and Proposition
7.2.12
, the two loop stacks in question are the completions of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb Q}\mathcal{X}
along the two closed substacks in the classical locus
\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}})
given by preimages
f^{-1}([\{e\}/G])
and
f^{-1}([\mathrm{Uni(G)}/G])
correspondingly. Note that by our assumption
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}
itself is also of finite type over the field
k
. Consequently, there is a simplicial (classical) affine scheme of finite type
Z_{\bullet}
such that
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}\simeq L_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(|Z_{\bullet}|)\in\mathrm{Stk}^{\mathrm{cl}}
. By Remark
7.1.4
(5)
it is enough to show that for any
i
the completion along substacks
f^{-1}([\{e\}/G])
and
f^{-1}([\mathrm{Uni(G)}/G])
agree after we pull-back further along
q_{i}\colon\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}})\times_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}}Z_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}})
. Both
(f\circ q_{i})^{-1}([\{e\}/G])
and
(f\circ q_{i})^{-1}([\mathrm{Uni(G)}/G])
are some closed subschemes of the finite type affine scheme
\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}})\times_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}}Z_{i}
and to check that the corresponding completions agree it’s enough to check that the reduced parts of all fibers over
\overline{k}
-points of
Z_{i}
coincide. Let
z\in Z_{i}(\overline{k})
and let
x\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}
be its image; then the fiber
\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}})\times_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}}\{x\}
is identified with the stabilizer
G_{x}:=\underline{\mathrm{Aut}}_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}}(\{x\})
. Moreover, the projection to
\mathcal{I}(BG)\simeq[G/G]
is identified with the natural compostion
G_{x}\hookrightarrow G\rightarrow[G/G]
. Note that since
\mathcal{X}
is ANS the group
G_{x}
is a nice
\overline{k}
-group scheme. The two closed subschemes in question are then identified with the preimage of
[\{e\}/G]
and
[\mathrm{Uni}(G)/G]
correspondingly; they are given by the unit
\{e\}\in G_{x}
and zeros of the ideal
J_{G}\cdot\mathcal{O}(G_{x})\subset\mathcal{O}(G_{x})
correspondingly. By the change-of-groups lemma for AS-ideals (Lemma
5.3.2
) and Proposition
7.2.20
we get
J_{G}\cdot\mathcal{O}(G_{x})
agrees with
J_{G_{x}}
up to passing to radicals. By Example
7.2.23
, using that
G_{x}
is nice, we then get that the reduced loci of the fibers over
\{x\}
are the same, which concludes the proof.
∎

7.4.
Cyclic homology

HH(\mathcal{C}/\mathbb Q)
admits a canonical action of
S^{1}
which leads to the three other localizing invariants:

negative cyclic homology:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HC^{-}(\mathcal{C}/\mathbb Q):=HH(\mathcal{C}/\mathbb Q)^{hS^{1}};}
periodic cyclic homology:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HP(\mathcal{C}/\mathbb Q):=HH(\mathcal{C}/\mathbb Q)^{tS^{1}};}
cyclic homology:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HC(\mathcal{C}/\mathbb Q):=HH(\mathcal{C}/\mathbb Q)_{hS^{1}}.}

We observe that Theorem
7.3.1
also implies the AS completion theorem for all the above versions of cyclic homology.

Corollary 7.4.1
.

Let
G
be a reductive group over a field of characteristic 0. Let
\mathcal{X}=[X/G]\rightarrow BG
be a representable morphism from an ANS stack
\mathcal{X}
, and assume that
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}\rightarrow BG
is of finite type.
Then the maps

\displaystyle HC^{-}(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}

\displaystyle\to R^{\mathrm{dAff}}HC^{-}(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)

\displaystyle HP(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}

\displaystyle\to R^{\mathrm{dAff}}HP(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)

\displaystyle HC(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}

\displaystyle\to R^{\mathrm{dAff}}HC(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb Q)

are all equivalences.

Proof.

Recall that there is a fiber sequence

HC\to HC^{-}\to HP

of functors, so to prove the claim it suffices to prove it for
HC^{-}
and for
HP
. The map in Theorem
7.3.3
is
S^{1}
-equivariant by construction, and so induces an equivalence of
S^{1}
-equivariant
k
-modules. Applying
S^{1}
-fixed points commutes both with right Kan extension and completion, this gives the claim for
HC^{-}
. The result for
HP
follows from Theorem
1.2.2
(recall that

HP
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant
[
GW89
, Theorem 4.1(iii)]
and is truncating

[
Goo85b
, Theorem IV.2.1]
).
∎

8.
Atiyah-Segal completion theorem for
K
-theory

We finally arrive at a proof of our main results. We begin by extending the relationship between
K
-theory, homotopy
K
-theory and negative cyclic homology to the case of derived algebraic stacks.

8.1.
Relations between K-theory, homotopy K-theory and negative cyclic homology of stacks

Recall that we are working in characteristic zero; to simplify matters we simply set
k=\mathbb{Q}
. One of the main tools in accessing the algebraic
K
-theory of non-smooth schemes in characteristic zero is the
Goodwillie Jones chern character

K\rightarrow HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})
which refines the Dennis trace map along the map
HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow HH(-/\mathbb{Q})
. This map fits as the top horizontal arrow of the following square, natural in qcqs schemes:

(8.1.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HC^{-}(X/\mathbb{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KH(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(X/\mathbb{Q}).}
19
19

19

The notation
L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(X/\mathbb{Q})
is a slight abuse of notation: it should really be
L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})(X)
.

The bottom horizontal map is the
\mathrm{cdh}
-sheafification of the top horizontal map and the square itself is cartesian; these results are highly non-obvious. They encode three “miracles” on algebraic
K
-theory: 1) that
KH
satisfies cdh descent
[
Cis13
]
, 2) that
L_{\mathrm{cdh}}\mathrm{K}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant
[
KST17
]
and that 3) the fiber of the chern character
K\rightarrow HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})
is a cdh sheaf, a result due to Cortiñas
[
Cn98
]
. We note that 1) and 2) in characteristic zero is one of the key results in Haesemeyer’s thesis
[
Hae04
]
, which gives the first hints that 1) and 2) can hold in very general settings.

In the next proposition, we extend (
8.1.1
) to certain derived algebraic stacks. As we have avoided speaking of the cdh topology on derived algebraic stacks, we will make use of the following definition instead; see also
[
EM23
, Lemma 5.7]
.

Definition 8.1.2
.

Define the localizing invariant
L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})
to be the pushout of functors

L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q}):=\operatorname*{colim}\left(HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})\leftarrow K\rightarrow KH\right):\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{k}\rightarrow\mathrm{Spt}.

Noting that pushouts commute with fibre sequences, the pushout above can be taken pointwise in the
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{\mathbb{Q}},\mathrm{Spt})
.

Proposition 8.1.3
.

The following hold for
L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})
:

(1)

on qcqs derived
k
-schemes,
L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})
agrees with the
\mathrm{cdh}
-sheafification of
HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})
;

(2)

on a
k
-smooth algebraic stack
\mathcal{X}
, the map
HC^{-}(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{Q})
is an equivalence;

(3)

L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})
is a localizing invariant, is truncating and, in particular, nilinvariant;

(4)

on ANS algebraic
k
-stacks,
L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})
is cdh-excisive.

Proof.

(1)

This follows from the fact that (
8.1.1
) is a pushout as well on the category of qcqs derived
k
-schemes.

(2)

By construction, it suffices to prove that if
\mathcal{X}
is a smooth algebraic
k
-stack, then
K(\mathcal{X})\simeq KH(\mathcal{X})
. In this case, we just need to prove that
K(\mathcal{X})\simeq K(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{n})
for all
n\geqslant 0
. There is a map
K(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{r})\rightarrow G(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{r})
induced by the functor of stable
\infty
-categories
\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{r})\rightarrow\mathrm{Coh}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{r})
. As explained in
[
Kha22
, 3.5]
,
G(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{r})\simeq G(\mathcal{X})
for any noetherian derived Artin stack, hence it suffices to prove that the functor
\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{r})\rightarrow\mathrm{Coh}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{r})
is an equivalence. By flat descent for both
\mathrm{Perf}
and
\mathrm{Coh}
, the functor agrees since they agree on smooth
k
-schemes.

(3)

By construction we have a fiber sequence

\mathrm{fib}\left(K\rightarrow HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})\right)\rightarrow KH\rightarrow L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q}).

Since
KH
is truncating
[
LT19
, Proposition 3.14]
, the result then follows from the fact that the fibre is truncating by Goodwillie’s theorem
[
Goo86
]
.

(4)

By
[
ES21
, Corollary 5.2.6]
, both
\mathrm{fib}\left(K\rightarrow HC^{-}(-/k)\right)
and
KH
are cdh-excisive since they are truncating. This implies that
L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})
is as well.

∎

Remark 8.1.4
.

We note that some aspects of Proposition
8.1.3
holds more generally. On the
\infty
-category of derived algebraic stacks, we can also define
L_{\mathrm{cdh}}TC
of stacks via the pushout of
TC\leftarrow K\rightarrow KH
and the analogs of Proposition
8.1.3
will also hold; here we use a version of (
8.1.1
) that holds for qcqs schemes and involves
TC

[
EM23
, Theorem 3.8]
. We also note that, as one can see from the proof, the agreement between
HC^{-}(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{Q})
and
L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{Q})
holds for stacks which are
K
-regular: those that satisfy
K(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{r})\simeq K(\mathcal{X})
for all
r\geqslant 0
. However, it is not clear how to prove the next corollary without resolution of singularities. In particular, it establishes an Atiyah-Segal completion theorem for a truncating invariant which is not
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant.

Corollary 8.1.5
.

Fix
G
a reductive group over a characteristic zero field
k
. Let
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow BG
be representable morphism where
\mathcal{X}
is derived algebraic stack, such that the morphism
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}\rightarrow BG
is finite type. Assume further that
\mathcal{X}
is ANS. Then the canonical map

L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{Q})^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}\rightarrow R^{\mathrm{dAff}}L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})(\mathcal{X})

is an equivalence.

Proof.

Since both functors are cdh-excisive (by Proposition
8.1.3
for the domain and Lemma
4.4.3
for the target) we can reduce to the case of
\mathcal{X}
a smooth, ANS
k
-stack by an inductive argument similar to the proof of Theorem
1.2.2
. In this case, Proposition
8.1.3
(3) (resp. Lemma
8.1.7
) shows that
L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{Q})\simeq HC^{-}(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{Q})
(resp.
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{Q})\simeq HC^{-}(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{Q})
). Therefore, the result follows from the analogous statement for
HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})
in place of
L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})
, which is Corollary
7.4.1
.

∎

Remark 8.1.6

(Corollary
8.1.5
without resolution of singularities)
.

It might be possible to prove Corollary
8.1.5
without resolution of singularities; such a proof method will surely establish an analogous result for
L_{\mathrm{cdh}}TC
in mixed and positive characteristics. A possible strategy is to develop seriously the cdh topology on algebraic stacks, prove a hypercompleteness result just as in
[
EHIK20
]
and characterize the points of such a topology. The role of valuation rings in a stacky situation should be played by the root stacks. A hint that this is true is given by the valuative criterion for proper maps of tame stacks, recently established by Bresciani and Vistoli
[
BV24
]
.

We have used the following lemma in Corollary
8.1.5
.

Lemma 8.1.7
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a
k
-smooth, algebraic stack. Then we have a canonical equivalence

R^{\mathrm{dAff}}HC^{-}(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow R^{\mathrm{dAff}}L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})(\mathcal{X}).

Proof.

We first claim that any étale surjection of algebraic stacks
f:\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
is of universal descent for
R^{\mathrm{dAff}}L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})
. Using Proposition
4.2.2
, it suffices to prove that
L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})
satisfies étale descent for classical commutative rings (since it is nilinvariant); by Proposition
8.1.3
this is computed by
\mathrm{cdh}
-sheafifying
HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})
and then restricting to affine schemes. To see this we can appeal to
[
EM23
, Theorem A.3]
, applying it to the HKR filtration on
HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})

[
EM23
, Theorem 4.2]
. Note that we can reduce to the quasisyntomic case for
L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})
by resolution of singularities. Alternatively, we know that the fibre of the map
HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})
is the fibre of
K\rightarrow KH
by (
8.1.1
). The fibre of
K\rightarrow KH
then satisfies étale descent by a theorem of van der Kallen
[
vdK86
]
.

Now, since
\mathcal{X}
is a smooth algebraic
k
-stack, it admits an étale surjection
\operatorname{Spec}R\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
where
R
is a smooth
k
-algebra. Since any étale surjection is of universal
HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})
-descent it suffices to note that
HC^{-}(X/\mathbb{Q})\simeq L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(X/\mathbb{Q})
on smooth
k
-schemes
20
20

20

The argument of the first paragraph of
[
CHSW08
, Theorem 3.12]
applies, using resolution of singularities.
.
∎

8.2.
Proof of Theorem
8.2.1

Now we are ready to prove our main theorem. We restate it for the reader’s convenience.

Theorem 8.2.1
.

Fix
G
a reductive group over a characteristic zero field
k
. Let
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow BG
be representable morphism where
\mathcal{X}
is derived algebraic stack, such that the morphism
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}\rightarrow BG
is finite type. Assume further that
\mathcal{X}
is ANS. Then we have an equivalence:

(8.2.2)

K(\mathcal{X})^{\wedge}_{I_{G}}\simeq R^{\mathrm{dAff}}K(\mathcal{X}),

where the limit is taken across all morphisms from an derived affine scheme to
\mathcal{X}
.

Proof.

First, take the right Kan extension of the square (
8.1.1
) to obtain the cartesian square on derived algebraic stacks over
\mathbb{Q}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\mathrm{dAff}}K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\mathrm{dAff}}HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\mathrm{dAff}}KH}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\mathrm{dAff}}L_{\mathrm{cdh}}HC^{-}(-/\mathbb{Q})}

We have a comparison map of cartesian squares
